denote the solution set of Equation (
3.54
).
Then
\mathfrak{Z}
can be written as a disjoint union

\mathfrak{Z}=\bigcup_{(r_{11},\ldots,r_{KN})\in\{0,1,\ldots,p^{s}-1\}^{KN}}p^{s}\cdot\Theta_{s;r_{11},\ldots,r_{KN}}\mathfrak{Z}+(r_{11},\ldots,r_{KN}),

where each
\Theta_{s;r_{11},\ldots,r_{KN}}\mathfrak{Z}
is the solution set of the following “shifted” S-unit equation

\sum_{i=1}^{K}\Phi^{s}(A_{1})^{p^{s}z^{\prime}_{i1}}\Phi^{s}(A_{2})^{p^{s}z^{\prime}_{i2}}\cdots\Phi^{s}(A_{N})^{p^{s}z^{\prime}_{iN}}\cdot\Phi^{s}(A_{1})^{r_{i1}}\Phi^{s}(A_{2})^{r_{i2}}\cdots\Phi^{s}(A_{N})^{r_{iN}}\Phi^{s}(v_{i})=\Phi^{s}(v_{0}).

(3.56)

Note that a finite union of
p
-normal sets is still
p
-normal, and the set
p^{s}\cdot\Theta_{s;r_{11},\ldots,r_{KN}}\mathfrak{Z}+(r_{11},\ldots,r_{KN})
is
p
-normal if
\Theta_{s;r_{11},\ldots,r_{KN}}\mathfrak{Z}
is
p
-normal.
Therefore, it suffices to show that each
\Theta_{s;r_{11},\ldots,r_{KN}}\mathfrak{Z}
is
p
-normal.
Note that the defining equation (
3.56
) of
\Theta_{s;r_{11},\ldots,r_{KN}}\mathfrak{Z}
can be written as

\sum_{i=1}^{K}\left(A^{\prime}_{1}\right)^{z^{\prime}_{i1}}\left(A^{\prime}_{2}\right)^{z^{\prime}_{i2}}\cdots\left(A^{\prime}_{N}\right)^{z^{\prime}_{iN}}\cdot v^{\prime}_{i}=v^{\prime}_{0},

(3.57)

where
A^{\prime}_{j}\coloneqq\Phi^{s}(A_{j})
for
j=1,\ldots,N
; and
v^{\prime}_{i}\coloneqq\Phi^{s}(A_{1})^{r_{i1}}\Phi^{s}(A_{2})^{r_{i2}}\cdots\Phi^{s}(A_{N})^{r_{iN}}\Phi^{s}(v_{i})
for
i=1,\ldots,K
; and
v^{\prime}_{0}\coloneqq\Phi^{s}(v_{0})
.
Note that (
3.57
) is an equation over the
\Phi^{s}(\mathcal{A})
-module
\Phi^{s}(\mathcal{V})=\mathbb{Z}_{/p^{e}}(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muX\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu)^{p^{sn}d}
.
Therefore, we can without loss of generality replace
\mathcal{A}
by
\Phi^{s}(\mathcal{A})
(note that this does not change the ring structure of
\mathcal{A}
, so it is still local with maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}
), replace
\mathcal{V}
by
\Phi^{s}(\mathcal{V})=\mathbb{Z}_{/p^{e}}(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muX\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu)^{p^{sn}d}
(and consequently replace the dimension
d
by
p^{sn}d
), as well as replacing each
A^{\prime}_{j}=\Phi^{s}(A_{j})
by
A_{j}
and each
v^{\prime}_{i}
by
v_{i}
.
In this way, by Proposition
3.28
, we can suppose from now on that
R\in\mathsf{GL}_{p^{n}d}(\mathbb{Z}_{/p^{e}}(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muX\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu))
satisfies

R^{-1}\cdot\Phi(A_{i})^{p}\cdot R=\operatorname{diag}\big(A_{i},\ldots,A_{i}\big)

(3.58)

for
i=1,\ldots,N
.

Additional condition: homogeneity.

We now show we can suppose
v_{0}=0
without loss of generality.
Indeed, let
\widetilde{\mathfrak{Z}}
denote the set of solutions
(z_{11},\ldots,z_{KN},z_{01},\ldots,z_{0N})\in\mathbb{Z}^{(K+1)N}
to the equation

\sum_{i=1}^{K}A_{1}^{z_{i1}}A_{2}^{z_{i2}}\cdots A_{N}^{z_{iN}}v_{i}+A_{1}^{z_{01}}A_{2}^{z_{02}}\cdots A_{N}^{z_{0N}}\cdot(-v_{0})=0.

Then
\mathfrak{Z}=\widetilde{\mathfrak{Z}}\cap\{(z_{11},\ldots,z_{KN},z_{01},\ldots,z_{0N})\mid z_{01}=\cdots=z_{0N}=0\}
.
The second set of the intersection is obviously
p
-normal.
Therefore in order to show that
\mathfrak{Z}
is
p
-normal, it suffices to show that
\widetilde{\mathfrak{Z}}
is
p
-normal, because the intersection of two
p
-normal sets is
p
-normal (Proposition
3.8
).
Hence, by replacing
K
with
K+1
, we reduce to the case of
homogeneous
equations, that is, where the right hand side of (
3.54
) is zero.
From now on we suppose without loss of generality that
v_{0}=0
.

For any sequence
\gamma\colon\mathbb{Z}^{KN}\rightarrow\mathcal{V}
of the form

\gamma(z_{11},\ldots,z_{KN})=\sum_{i=1}^{K}A_{1}^{z_{i1}}A_{2}^{z_{i2}}\cdots A_{N}^{z_{iN}}w_{i},\quad\text{ where }w_{1},\ldots,w_{N}\in\mathcal{V},

(3.59)

denote

\mathfrak{Z}(\gamma)\coloneqq\left\{(z_{11},\ldots,z_{KN})\in\mathbb{Z}^{KN}\;\middle|\;\gamma(z_{11},\ldots,z_{KN})=0\right\}.

Our goal now is to show that
\mathfrak{Z}(\alpha)
is
p
-normal, where
\alpha(z_{11},\ldots,z_{KN})\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{K}A_{1}^{z_{i1}}A_{2}^{z_{i2}}\cdots A_{N}^{z_{iN}}v_{i}
.

For
j=0,1,\ldots,p^{n}-1
, denote the projection

\pi_{j}\colon\Phi(\mathcal{V})=\mathbb{Z}_{/p^{e}}(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muX\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu)^{p^{n}d}\longrightarrow\mathcal{V}=\mathbb{Z}_{/p^{e}}(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muX\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu)^{d},\quad(f_{1},f_{2},\ldots,f_{p^{n}d})\mapsto(f_{jd+1},\ldots,f_{jd+d}).

To show that
\mathfrak{Z}(\alpha)
is
p
-automatic, we need to describe
\Theta_{\epsilon_{11},\ldots,\epsilon_{KN}}(\mathfrak{Z}(\alpha))
for all
(\epsilon_{11},\ldots,\epsilon_{KN})\in\Sigma_{p}^{KN}
.
The next lemma expresses
\Theta_{\epsilon_{11},\ldots,\epsilon_{KN}}(\mathfrak{Z}(\alpha))
in terms of zero sets of other sequences.

Lemma 3.32
.

Let

\gamma(z_{11},\ldots,z_{KN})=\sum_{i=1}^{K}A_{1}^{z_{i1}}A_{2}^{z_{i2}}\cdots A_{N}^{z_{iN}}w_{i}
.
For any
(\epsilon_{11},\ldots,\epsilon_{KN})\in\Sigma_{p}^{KN}
, we have

\Theta_{\epsilon_{11},\ldots,\epsilon_{KN}}(\mathfrak{Z}(\gamma))=\bigcap_{j=0}^{p^{n}-1}\mathfrak{Z}\left(\Upsilon_{\epsilon_{11},\ldots,\epsilon_{KN};j}(\gamma)\right),

where

\Upsilon_{\epsilon_{11},\ldots,\epsilon_{KN};j}(\gamma)(z_{11},\ldots,z_{KN})\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{K}A_{1}^{z_{i1}}A_{2}^{z_{i2}}\cdots A_{N}^{z_{iN}}\cdot\pi_{j}\left(R\Phi(A_{1})^{\epsilon_{i1}}\cdots\Phi(A_{N})^{\epsilon_{iN}}\Phi(w_{i})\right).

(3.60)

Here,
R\in\mathsf{GL}_{p^{n}d}(\mathbb{Z}_{/p^{e}}(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muX\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu))
is defined in Equation (
3.58
).

Proof.

We have
\gamma(z_{11},\ldots,z_{KN})=0
if and only if
\Phi(\gamma)(z_{11},\ldots,z_{KN})=0
.
Write
(z_{11},\ldots,z_{KN})=p\cdot(z^{\prime}_{11},\ldots,z^{\prime}_{KN})+(\epsilon_{11},\ldots,\epsilon_{KN})
, then

\displaystyle\;\Phi(\gamma)(z_{11},\ldots,z_{KN})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\sum_{i=1}^{K}\Phi(A_{1}^{pz^{\prime}_{i1}+\epsilon_{i1}}\cdots A_{N}^{pz^{\prime}_{iN}+\epsilon_{iN}}w_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\sum_{i=1}^{K}\left(\Phi(A_{1})^{p}\right)^{z^{\prime}_{i1}}\cdots\left(\Phi(A_{N})^{p}\right)^{z^{\prime}_{iN}}\cdot\Phi(A_{1}^{\epsilon_{i1}}\cdots A_{N}^{\epsilon_{in}})\Phi(w_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;R^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{K}\left(R\Phi(A_{1})^{p}R^{-1}\right)^{z^{\prime}_{i1}}\cdots\left(R\Phi(A_{N})^{p}R^{-1}\right)^{z^{\prime}_{iN}}\cdot R\Phi(A_{1}^{\epsilon_{i1}}\cdots A_{N}^{\epsilon_{in}})\Phi(w_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;R^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{K}\Big(\operatorname{diag}(A_{1},\ldots,A_{1})\Big)^{z^{\prime}_{i1}}\cdots\Big(\operatorname{diag}(A_{N},\ldots,A_{N})\Big)^{z^{\prime}_{iN}}\cdot R\Phi(A_{1}^{\epsilon_{i1}}\cdots A_{N}^{\epsilon_{in}})\Phi(w_{i})\quad\quad(\text{by \eqref{eq:assumeFrob}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;R^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{K}\operatorname{diag}\Big(A_{1}^{z^{\prime}_{i1}}A_{2}^{z^{\prime}_{i2}}\cdots A_{N}^{z^{\prime}_{iN}},\ldots,A_{1}^{z^{\prime}_{i1}}A_{2}^{z^{\prime}_{i2}}\cdots A_{N}^{z^{\prime}_{iN}}\Big)\cdot R\Phi(A_{1}^{\epsilon_{i1}}\cdots A_{N}^{\epsilon_{in}})\Phi(w_{i})

Note that
\Phi(\gamma)=0
if and only if
R\Phi(\gamma)=0
, if and only if
\pi_{j}(R\Phi(\gamma))=0
for all
j=0,1,\ldots,p^{n}-1
.
That is,

\mathfrak{Z}(\gamma)=\mathfrak{Z}(\Phi(\gamma))=\bigcap_{j=0}^{p^{n}-1}\mathfrak{Z}(\pi_{j}(R\Phi(\gamma))),

where

\displaystyle\;\pi_{j}(R\Phi(\gamma))(z_{11},\ldots,z_{KN})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\pi_{j}\left(RR^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{K}\operatorname{diag}\Big(A_{1}^{z^{\prime}_{i1}}A_{2}^{z^{\prime}_{i2}}\cdots A_{N}^{z^{\prime}_{iN}},\ldots,A_{1}^{z^{\prime}_{i1}}A_{2}^{z^{\prime}_{i2}}\cdots A_{N}^{z^{\prime}_{iN}}\Big)\cdot R\Phi(A_{1}^{\epsilon_{i1}}\cdots A_{N}^{\epsilon_{in}})\Phi(w_{i})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\sum_{i=1}^{K}\pi_{j}\left(\operatorname{diag}\Big(A_{1}^{z^{\prime}_{i1}}A_{2}^{z^{\prime}_{i2}}\cdots A_{N}^{z^{\prime}_{iN}},\ldots,A_{1}^{z^{\prime}_{i1}}A_{2}^{z^{\prime}_{i2}}\cdots A_{N}^{z^{\prime}_{iN}}\Big)\cdot R\Phi(A_{1}^{\epsilon_{i1}}\cdots A_{N}^{\epsilon_{in}})\Phi(w_{i})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\sum_{i=1}^{K}A_{1}^{z^{\prime}_{i1}}A_{2}^{z^{\prime}_{i2}}\cdots A_{N}^{z^{\prime}_{iN}}\cdot\pi_{j}\left(R\Phi(A_{1}^{\epsilon_{i1}}\cdots A_{N}^{\epsilon_{in}})\Phi(w_{i})\right).

Therefore,

\Theta_{\epsilon_{11},\ldots,\epsilon_{KN}}(\mathfrak{Z}(\gamma))=\bigcap_{j=0}^{p^{n}-1}\mathfrak{Z}\left(\Upsilon_{\epsilon_{11},\ldots,\epsilon_{KN};j}(\gamma)\right),

where

\Upsilon_{\epsilon_{11},\ldots,\epsilon_{KN};j}(\gamma)(z_{11},\ldots,z_{KN})\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{K}A_{1}^{z_{i1}}A_{2}^{z_{i2}}\cdots A_{N}^{z_{iN}}\cdot\pi_{j}\left(R\Phi(A_{1}^{\epsilon_{i1}}\cdots A_{N}^{\epsilon_{in}})\Phi(w_{i})\right).

∎

Based on Lemma
3.32
, we now construct a first automaton
\widetilde{\mathscr{U}}
that accepts the zero set
\mathfrak{Z}(\alpha)
.
This automaton will have the critical flaw that it contains an
infinite
number of states.
The final automaton
\mathscr{U}
that we will construct later will be a finite
sub-automaton
of
\widetilde{\mathscr{U}}
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
denote the set of all sequences of the form (
3.59
):

\mathcal{G}\coloneqq\left\{\gamma\;\middle|\;\gamma(z_{11},\ldots,z_{KN})\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{K}A_{1}^{z_{i1}}\cdots A_{N}^{z_{iN}}w_{i},w_{1},\ldots,w_{K}\in\mathcal{V}\right\}.

This is
a priori
an infinite set.
We now construct the automaton
\widetilde{\mathscr{U}}
as follows.

States of
\widetilde{\mathscr{U}}
.

The state set of
\widetilde{\mathscr{U}}
is

2^{\mathcal{G}}\coloneqq\{W\mid W\subseteq\mathcal{G}\},

that is, the set of all subsets of
\mathcal{G}
.
In other words, each state
W
of
\widetilde{\mathscr{U}}
is a set of sequences
\{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\ldots\}
, which can be considered as system of S-unit equations “
\gamma_{1}(z_{11},\ldots,z_{KN})=\gamma_{2}(z_{11},\ldots,z_{KN})=\cdots=0
”.
We denote by
\mathfrak{Z}(W)
its zero set:

\mathfrak{Z}(W)\coloneqq\bigcap_{\gamma\in W}\mathfrak{Z}(\gamma).

When
W
is a singleton
\{\gamma\}
, we will not distinguish between
\mathfrak{Z}(\{\gamma\})
and
\mathfrak{Z}(\gamma)
.

Transitions of
\widetilde{\mathscr{U}}
.

There is a transition from the state
W
to
W^{\prime}
, labeled
(\epsilon_{11},\ldots,\epsilon_{KN})\in\Sigma_{p}^{KN}
, if and only if

\bigcup_{\gamma\in W}\bigcup_{j=0}^{p^{n}-1}\big\{\Upsilon_{\epsilon_{11},\ldots,\epsilon_{KN};j}(\gamma)\big\}=W^{\prime},

see Figure
5
. Note that the above union might not be disjoint.

\{\gamma\mid\gamma\in W\}
\big\{\Upsilon_{\epsilon_{11},\ldots,\epsilon_{KN};j}(\gamma)\;\big|\;\gamma\in W,j\in\{0,1,\ldots,p^{n}-1\}\big\}
(\epsilon_{11},\ldots,\epsilon_{KN})

Figure 5:
A transition of the automaton
\widetilde{\mathscr{U}}
.

If there is a transition from
W
to
W^{\prime}
labeled
(\epsilon_{11},\ldots,\epsilon_{KN})
, then

\displaystyle\mathfrak{Z}(W^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\bigcap_{\gamma^{\prime}\in W^{\prime}}\mathfrak{Z}(\gamma^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\bigcap_{\gamma^{\prime}\in\bigcup_{\gamma\in W}\bigcup_{j=0}^{p^{k}-1}\left\{\Upsilon_{\epsilon_{11},\ldots,\epsilon_{KN};j}(\gamma)\right\}}\mathfrak{Z}(\gamma^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\bigcap_{\gamma\in W}\bigcap_{j=0}^{p^{k}-1}\mathfrak{Z}(\Upsilon_{\epsilon_{11},\ldots,\epsilon_{KN};j}(\gamma))

\displaystyle=\bigcap_{\gamma\in W}\Theta_{\epsilon_{11},\ldots,\epsilon_{KN}}\left(\mathfrak{Z}(\gamma)\right)

\displaystyle=\Theta_{\epsilon_{11},\ldots,\epsilon_{KN}}\left(\mathfrak{Z}(W)\right)

(3.61)

by Lemma
3.32
.

Initial and accepting states of
\widetilde{\mathscr{U}}
.

The initial state of
\widetilde{\mathscr{U}}
is
\{\alpha\}\in 2^{\mathcal{G}}
.
The accepting states of
\widetilde{\mathscr{U}}
are those states
W\in 2^{\mathcal{G}}
satisfying

(0,\ldots,0)\in\mathfrak{Z}(W)
.
Note that whether
(0,\ldots,0)\in\mathfrak{Z}(W)
can be checked effectively if
W
contains finitely many sequences.
This can be done by simply checking whether
\gamma(0,\ldots,0)=0
for all
\gamma\in W
.

From the definition of
\widetilde{\mathscr{U}}
, it is immediate that
\widetilde{\mathscr{U}}
accepts exactly the set
\mathfrak{Z}(\alpha)
.
Indeed, suppose
\boldsymbol{z}=\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{0}+p\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{1}+\cdots+p^{\ell}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{\ell}\in\mathfrak{Z}(\alpha)
, where
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{i}\in\Sigma_{p}^{KN},i=0,\ldots,\ell
.
Let
\delta\left(\{\alpha\},\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{0}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{1}\cdots\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{\ell}\right)
denote the state reached by reading the word “
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{0}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{1}\cdots\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{\ell}
” starting from
\{\alpha\}
.
Then by Equation (
3.5
),

\mathfrak{Z}\big(\delta\left(\{\alpha\},\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{0}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{1}\cdots\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{\ell}\right)\big)=\Theta_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{\ell}}\cdots\Theta_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{1}}\Theta_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{0}}\left(\mathfrak{Z}(\alpha)\right)\ni\Theta_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{\ell}}\cdots\Theta_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{1}}\Theta_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{0}}\boldsymbol{z}=\boldsymbol{0}.

Therefore
\delta\left(\{\alpha\},\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{0}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{1}\cdots\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{\ell}\right)
is an accepting state.
Similarly, if
\delta\left(\{\alpha\},\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{0}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{1}\cdots\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{\ell}\right)
is an accepting state, then

\boldsymbol{0}\in\mathfrak{Z}\big(\delta\left(\{\alpha\},\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{0}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{1}\cdots\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{\ell}\right)\big)=\Theta_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{\ell}}\cdots\Theta_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{1}}\Theta_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{0}}\left(\mathfrak{Z}(\alpha)\right),

so
\boldsymbol{z}=\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{0}+p\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{1}+\cdots+p^{\ell}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{\ell}
belongs to
\mathfrak{Z}(\alpha)
.

Although
\widetilde{\mathscr{U}}
is infinite, not all states of
\widetilde{\mathscr{U}}
are reachable from
\{\alpha\}
.
We now show that the number of states reachable from
\{\alpha\}
is in fact finite, by bounding the coefficients defining these states.
The following lemma can be considered as a generalization of
[
Der07
, Proposition 5.2]
.

Lemma 3.33
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a finite subset of
\mathcal{V}=\mathbb{Z}_{/p^{e}}(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muX\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu)^{d}
.
Let
\mathcal{T}
be a finite set of matrices in
\mathsf{GL}_{p^{n}d}(\mathbb{Z}_{/p^{e}}(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muX\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu))
.
Then there exists an effectively computable finite set
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\supseteq\mathcal{S}
, such that for all
s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
,
T\in\mathcal{T}
and
j\in\{0,1,\ldots,p^{n}-1\}
, we have
\pi_{j}(T\Phi(s^{\prime}))\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Let
h\in\mathbb{Z}_{/p^{e}}[\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muX\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu]
be a common denominator of all the entries appearing in elements of
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{S}
.
We construct
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
as the set

\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\coloneqq\left\{\left(\frac{g_{1}}{h^{2p^{e-1}}},\ldots,\frac{g_{d}}{h^{2p^{e-1}}}\right)\in\mathbb{Z}_{/p^{e}}(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muX\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu)^{d}\;\middle|\;g_{1},\ldots,g_{d}\in\mathbb{Z}_{/p^{e}}[\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muX\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu],\;\deg(g_{1})\leq c,\ldots,\deg(g_{d})\leq c\right\}

(3.62)

for some
c\in\mathbb{N}
that we will specify later.
Note that for any given
c
, the set
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
is finite since there are only finitely many polynomials in
\mathbb{Z}_{/p^{e}}[\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muX\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu]
with bounded degree.

Let
s^{\prime}=\left(\frac{g_{1}}{h^{2p^{e-1}}},\ldots,\frac{g_{d}}{h^{2p^{e-1}}}\right)\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
.
For any
j=1,\ldots,d
, the product
g_{j}h^{p^{e}-2p^{e-1}}
can be written as

g_{j}h^{p^{e}-2p^{e-1}}=\sum_{\epsilon_{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{n}\in\{0,1,\ldots,p-1\}}F_{\epsilon_{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{n}}(X_{1}^{p},\ldots,X_{n}^{p})\cdot X_{1}^{\epsilon_{1}}\cdots X_{n}^{\epsilon_{n}}

with
F_{\epsilon_{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{n}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{/p^{e}}[\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muX\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu]
for all
\epsilon_{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{n}\in\{0,1,\ldots,p-1\}
.
Recall from Lemma
3.13
that we can write
h^{p^{e}}
as
h^{p^{e-1}}(X_{1}^{p},\ldots,X_{n}^{p})
.
So

\frac{g_{j}}{h^{2p^{e-1}}}=\frac{g_{j}h^{p^{e}-2p^{e-1}}}{h^{p^{e}}}=\sum_{\epsilon_{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{n}\in\{0,1,\ldots,p-1\}}\frac{F_{\epsilon_{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{n}}(X_{1}^{p},\ldots,X_{n}^{p})}{h^{p^{e-1}}(X_{1}^{p},\ldots,X_{n}^{p})}\cdot X_{1}^{\epsilon_{1}}\cdots X_{n}^{\epsilon_{n}}.

Therefore

\Phi\left(\frac{g_{j}}{h^{2p^{e-1}}}\right)=\left(\frac{F_{0,\ldots,0}}{h^{p^{e-1}}},\ldots,\frac{F_{p-1,\ldots,p-1}}{h^{p^{e-1}}}\right),

where

\displaystyle\deg(F_{\epsilon_{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{n}})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\deg(g_{j}h^{p^{e}-2p^{e-1}})-(\epsilon_{1}+\cdots+\epsilon_{n})}{p}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\deg(g_{j})+(p^{e}-2p^{e-1})\deg(h)}{p}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c}{p}+\frac{(p^{e}-2p^{e-1})\deg(h)}{p}.

To sum up the above discussion,
\Phi(s^{\prime})=\left(\Phi\left(\frac{g_{1}}{h^{2p^{e-1}}}\right),\ldots,\Phi\left(\frac{g_{d}}{h^{2p^{e-1}}}\right)\right)
can be written as a tuple
\left(\frac{f_{1}}{h^{p^{e-1}}},\ldots,\frac{f_{p^{n}d}}{h^{p^{e-1}}}\right)
, where each
f_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}_{/p^{e}}[\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muX\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu],k=1,\ldots,p^{n}d
, satisfies

\deg(f_{k})\leq\frac{c}{p}+\frac{(p^{e}-2p^{e-1})\deg(h)}{p}.

(3.63)

Write
\mathcal{T}=\{T_{1},\ldots,T_{m}\}
.
By multiplying both the numerator and the denominator by a suitable polynomial, we can write out the coefficients
(t_{i\ell k})_{\ell,k\in\{1,\ldots,p^{n}d\}}
of any
T_{i}\in\mathcal{T}
as

t_{i\ell k}=\frac{a_{i\ell k}}{h^{p^{e-1}}},\quad a_{i\ell k}\in\mathbb{Z}_{/p^{e}}[\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muX\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu].

Then
T_{i}\Phi(s^{\prime})=(s_{1},\ldots,s_{p^{n}d})
, where

s_{\ell}=\frac{a_{i\ell 1}}{h^{p^{e-1}}}\cdot\frac{f_{1}}{h^{p^{e-1}}}+\frac{a_{i\ell 2}}{h^{p^{e-1}}}\cdot\frac{f_{2}}{h^{p^{e-1}}}+\cdots+\frac{a_{i\ell(p^{n}d)}}{h^{p^{e-1}}}\cdot\frac{f_{p^{n}d}}{h^{p^{e-1}}}=\frac{\sum_{k=1}^{p^{n}d}a_{i\ell k}f_{k}}{h^{2p^{e-1}}}

for
\ell=1,\ldots,p^{n}d
.
Furthermore,

\displaystyle\deg\left(\sum_{k=1}^{p^{n}d}a_{i\ell k}f_{k}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\max_{k\in\{1,\ldots,p^{n}d\}}\left(\deg(a_{i\ell k})+\deg(f_{k})\right)

\displaystyle\leq\max_{k\in\{1,\ldots,p^{n}d\}}\deg(a_{i\ell k})+\frac{c}{p}+\frac{(p^{e}-2p^{e-1})\deg(h)}{p}

by Inequality (
3.63
).
Therefore, for any

c\geq\frac{p\cdot\max_{i\in\{1,\ldots,m\},\ell,k\in\{1,\ldots,p^{n}d\}}\deg(a_{ijk})+(p^{e}-2p^{e-1})\deg(h)}{p-1},

(3.64)

we will have
\deg\left(\sum_{k=1}^{p^{n}d}a_{i\ell k}f_{k}\right)\leq c
for
i=1,\ldots,m;\;\ell=1,\ldots,p^{n}d
.
In this case, we have
\pi_{j}(T_{i}\Phi(s^{\prime}))\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
for
j=0,1,\ldots,p^{n}-1
.

Recall that every coefficient appearing in elements of
\mathcal{S}
can be written as
\frac{g}{h}=\frac{gh^{2p^{e-1}-1}}{h^{2p^{e-1}}}
.
Therefore we can take
c
large enough so that
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
contains every element of
\mathcal{S}
.
By enlarging
c
so that it satisfies Condition (
3.64
), we obtain
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\supseteq\mathcal{S}
such that
\pi_{j}(T\Phi(s^{\prime}))\subseteq\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
for all
s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
,
T\in\mathcal{T}
and
j\in\{0,1,\ldots,p^{n}-1\}
.
∎

The finite automaton
\mathscr{U}
.

We now construct the finite sub-automaton
\mathscr{U}
of
\widetilde{\mathscr{U}}
by bounding the states reachable from
\{\alpha\}
, where

\alpha(z_{11},\ldots,z_{KN})=\sum_{i=1}^{K}A_{1}^{z_{i1}}A_{2}^{z_{i2}}\cdots A_{N}^{z_{iN}}v_{i}
.

Recall that taking a transition
(\epsilon_{11},\ldots,\epsilon_{KN})
from a state
W
, we reach the new state

\bigcup_{\gamma\in W}\bigcup_{j=0}^{p^{n}-1}\big\{\Upsilon_{\epsilon_{11},\ldots,\epsilon_{KN};j}(\gamma)\big\}

where
\Upsilon_{\epsilon_{11},\ldots,\epsilon_{KN};j}(\gamma)(z_{11},\ldots,z_{KN})
is defined as

\sum_{i=1}^{K}A_{1}^{z_{i1}}A_{2}^{z_{i2}}\cdots A_{N}^{z_{iN}}\cdot\pi_{j}\left(R\Phi(A_{1}^{\epsilon_{i1}}\cdots A_{N}^{\epsilon_{in}})\Phi(w_{i})\right)

for
\gamma=\sum_{i=1}^{K}A_{1}^{z_{i1}}A_{2}^{z_{i2}}\cdots A_{N}^{z_{iN}}w_{i}
.

Apply Lemma
3.33
with
\mathcal{S}\coloneqq\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{K}\}
, and

\mathcal{T}\coloneqq\left\{R\Phi(A_{1}^{\epsilon_{i1}}\cdots A_{N}^{\epsilon_{in}})\,\middle|\,\epsilon_{11},\ldots,\epsilon_{KN}\in\Sigma_{p}\right\},

we obtain a finite set
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\supseteq\mathcal{S}
satisfying

\pi_{j}\left(R\Phi(A_{1}^{\epsilon_{i1}}\cdots A_{N}^{\epsilon_{in}})\Phi(w)\right)\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}

(3.65)

for all
w\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
,
\epsilon_{11},\ldots,\epsilon_{KN}\in\Sigma_{p}
and
j\in\{0,1,\ldots,p^{n}-1\}
.
Let
\mathcal{H}\subset\mathcal{G}
be the set of sequences whose coefficients are in
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
:

\mathcal{H}\coloneqq\left\{\gamma\;\middle|\;\gamma(z_{11},\ldots,z_{KN})\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{K}A_{1}^{z_{i1}}\cdots A_{N}^{z_{iN}}w_{i},\;w_{1},\ldots,w_{K}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\right\}.

Then
\mathcal{H}
is finite and contains the sequence
\alpha
, and
\gamma\in\mathcal{H}\implies\Upsilon_{\epsilon_{11},\ldots,\epsilon_{KN};j}(\gamma)\in\mathcal{H}
for all
\epsilon_{11},\ldots,\epsilon_{KN}\in\Sigma_{p},\;j=0,1,\ldots,p^{n}-1
.
Therefore, a state in
2^{\mathcal{H}}\coloneqq\{W\mid W\subseteq\mathcal{H}\}
can only reach other states in
2^{\mathcal{H}}
.
We now take
\mathscr{U}
to be the sub-automaton of
\widetilde{\mathscr{U}}
containing all the states in
2^{\mathcal{H}}
.
Since it contains the initial state
\{\alpha\}
, the finite automaton
\mathscr{U}
accepts the zero set
\mathfrak{Z}(\alpha)
.

3.6 Decomposition of
\mathscr{U}
into strongly connected components

In the previous subsection we have shown that the zero set
\mathfrak{Z}(\alpha)
is
p
-automatic by constructing the automaton
\mathscr{U}
.
Our next step is to refine this result from
p
-automaticity to
p
-normality.
This refinement will be done by a combined analysis of the structure of
\mathscr{U}
and the structure of
\alpha
.
In this subsection we analyze the
strongly connected components
of
\mathscr{U}
.
We show that, roughly speaking, these strongly connected components will contribute to the subgroup
H
in the definition (
1.3
) of
p
-succinct sets.

Multiplicative independence.

We can suppose
A_{1},\ldots,A_{N}\in\mathcal{A}
to be multiplicatively independent, that is,

A_{1}^{z_{1}}A_{2}^{z_{2}}\cdots A_{N}^{z_{N}}=1\implies z_{1}=z_{2}=\cdots=z_{N}=0.

We can do so without loss of generality.
In fact, suppose
A_{1},\ldots,A_{N}
are not multiplicatively independent
4
4

4

Multiplicative dependence can be effectively computed using Noskov’s Lemma
[
Nos82
]

[
BCR94
, Proposition 2.4]
.
Then take a maximal subset of
\{A_{1},\ldots,A_{N}\}
that is multiplicatively independent, and without loss of generality denote them by
A_{1},\ldots,A_{s}
.
For each
j=s+1,\ldots,N
, there exists
t_{j}\geq 1
such that
A_{j}^{t_{j}}
is in the multiplicative subgroup generated by
A_{1},\ldots,A_{s}
.
We can write the zero set
\mathfrak{Z}(\alpha)
as a finite union

\bigcup_{r_{1j},\ldots,r_{Kj}\in\{0,1,\ldots,t_{j}-1\},j=s+1,\ldots,N}\Bigg\{(z_{11},\ldots,z_{1s},r_{1(s+1)},\ldots,r_{1N},\ldots,z_{K1},\ldots,z_{Ks},r_{K(s+1)},\ldots r_{KN})\\
\;\Bigg|\;\sum_{i=1}^{K}A_{1}^{z_{i1}}\cdots A_{s}^{z_{s1}}\cdot\left(A_{s+1}^{r_{i(s+1)}}\cdots A_{N}^{r_{iN}}v_{i}\right)=0\Bigg\}.

Thus, we have reduced the problem to showing that the solution set
(z_{11},\ldots,z_{1s},\ldots,z_{K1},\ldots,z_{Ks})
for each equation

\sum_{i=1}^{K}A_{1}^{z_{i1}}\cdots A_{s}^{z_{s1}}\cdot\left(A_{s+1}^{r_{i(s+1)}}\cdots A_{n}^{r_{iN}}v_{i}\right)=0

is
p
-normal, where
A_{1},\ldots,A_{s}
are multiplicatively independent.
Replacing
s
by
N
, we therefore reduce to the case where the elements
A_{1},A_{2},\ldots,A_{N}
, are multiplicatively independent.

From now on, let
\overline{A}
denote the tuple
(A_{1},\ldots,A_{N})
.
For a vector
\boldsymbol{z}=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{N})\in\mathbb{Z}^{N}
, we write

\overline{A}^{\boldsymbol{z}}\coloneqq A_{1}^{z_{1}}A_{2}^{z_{2}}\cdots A_{N}^{z_{N}}
.
Recall that
\mathcal{A}
is a local ring with maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}\ni p
, such that
\mathfrak{m}^{t}=0
for some
t\geq 1
.

Lemma 3.34
.

The maps
A_{1},\ldots,A_{N}
are multiplicatively independent in
\mathcal{A}
if and only if they are multiplicatively independent in the quotient
\mathcal{A}/\mathfrak{m}
.

Proof.

If
A_{1},\ldots,A_{N}
are multiplicatively independent in the quotient
\mathcal{A}/\mathfrak{m}
, then they are obviously multiplicatively independent in
\mathcal{A}
.

Suppose
A_{1},\ldots,A_{N}
multiplicatively independent in
\mathcal{A}
, and let
\boldsymbol{z}\in\mathbb{Z}^{N}
be such that
\overline{A}^{\boldsymbol{z}}\equiv 1\mod\mathfrak{m}
.
By Lemma
3.14
, we have
\overline{A}^{p^{t-1}\boldsymbol{z}}=1
, therefore
p^{t-1}\boldsymbol{z}=\boldsymbol{0}
by the multiplicative independence of
A_{1},\ldots,A_{N}
in
\mathcal{A}
.
Therefore
\boldsymbol{z}=\boldsymbol{0}
, and
A_{1},\ldots,A_{N}
are multiplicatively independent in
\mathcal{A}/\mathfrak{m}
.
∎

Prototype of the subgroup
H
.

Let
\boldsymbol{z}_{1}=(z_{11},\ldots,z_{1N}),\ldots,\boldsymbol{z}_{K}=(z_{K1},\ldots,z_{KN})\in\mathbb{Z}^{N}
, then
\alpha(z_{11},\ldots,z_{KN})=\sum_{i=1}^{K}A_{1}^{z_{i1}}\cdots A_{N}^{z_{iN}}v_{i}
can be written as
\alpha(\boldsymbol{z}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{z}_{K})=\sum_{i=1}^{K}\overline{A}^{\boldsymbol{z}_{i}}v_{i}
.

We start by giving some intuition of the subgroup
H
in the definition (
1.3
) of
p
-succinct sets.
For any
b\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
, we can replace
z_{1b},z_{2b},\ldots,z_{Kb}
, by
z_{1b}+1,z_{2b}+1,\ldots,z_{Kb}+1
, respectively, this will give us the sequence
A_{b}\cdot\alpha
.
Therefore, if
(z_{11},\ldots,z_{KN})\in\mathfrak{Z}(\alpha)
, then
(z_{11},\ldots,z_{KN})+\boldsymbol{e}_{\{1,\ldots,K\},b}
is also in
\mathfrak{Z}(\alpha)
, where

\boldsymbol{e}_{\{1,\ldots,K\},b}\coloneqq(\boldsymbol{e}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{e}_{K}),\quad\text{ with }\boldsymbol{e}_{1}=\cdots=\boldsymbol{e}_{K}=(0,\ldots,0,\underset{\underset{\text{$b$-th index}}{\uparrow}}{1},0,\ldots,0)\in\mathbb{Z}^{N}.

Therefore,
\mathfrak{Z}(\alpha)
is stable under translation by the group generated by
\boldsymbol{e}_{\{1,\ldots,K\},1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{e}_{\{1,\ldots,K\},N}
:

\mathfrak{Z}(\alpha)=\mathfrak{Z}(\alpha)+\sum_{b=1}^{N}\mathbb{Z}\boldsymbol{e}_{\{1,\ldots,K\},b}.

In this case, the subgroup
\sum_{b=1}^{N}\mathbb{Z}\boldsymbol{e}_{\{1,\ldots,K\},b}
a prototype of the subgroup
H
in the definition (
1.3
) of
p
-succinct sets.

Let us consider a more complicated example.
Let
W=\{\beta,\gamma\}
be a state of
\mathscr{U}
, where
\beta=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\overline{A}^{\boldsymbol{z}_{i}}w_{i},\;\gamma=\sum_{i=k+1}^{K}\overline{A}^{\boldsymbol{z}_{i}}w^{\prime}_{i}
, for some
1\leq k\leq K
.
Then by the homogeneity of
\beta
, for any
b\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
, we can replace
z_{1b},z_{2b},\ldots,z_{kb}
, by
z_{1b}+1,z_{2b}+1,\ldots,z_{kb}+1
, without changing the solution set to
\beta=0
.
This also does not change the solution set to
\gamma=0
, because the variables
z_{1b},z_{2b},\ldots,z_{kb}
do not appear in
\gamma
at all.
Similarly, we can replace
z_{(k+1)b},z_{(k+2)b},\ldots,z_{Kb}
, by
z_{(k+1)b}+1,z_{(k+2)b}+1,\ldots,z_{Kb}+1
, without changing the solution set.
This shows that
\mathfrak{Z}(\{\beta,\gamma\})
is stable under translation by the group generated by
\boldsymbol{e}_{\{1,\ldots,k\},1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{e}_{\{1,\ldots,k\},N}
,
\boldsymbol{e}_{\{k+1,\ldots,K\},1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{e}_{\{k+1,\ldots,K\},N}
:

\mathfrak{Z}(\{\beta,\gamma\})=\mathfrak{Z}(\{\beta,\gamma\})+\sum_{b=1}^{N}\mathbb{Z}\boldsymbol{e}_{\{1,\ldots,k\},b}+\sum_{b=1}^{N}\mathbb{Z}\boldsymbol{e}_{\{k+1,\ldots,K\},b},

where for any set
S\subseteq\{1,\ldots,K\}
,

\boldsymbol{e}_{S,b}\coloneqq(\boldsymbol{e}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{e}_{K}),\quad\text{ with }\boldsymbol{e}_{i}=\begin{cases}(0,\ldots,0,\underset{\underset{\text{$b$-th index}}{\uparrow}}{1},0,\ldots,0),\quad\text{ if }i\in S\\
(0,\ldots,0,\underset{\underset{\text{$b$-th index}}{\uparrow}}{0},0,\ldots,0),\quad\text{ if }i\notin S.\end{cases}

If additionally there is a transition labeled
(\epsilon_{11},\ldots,\epsilon_{KN})
from
\{\alpha\}
to
\{\beta,\gamma\}
, then
\Theta_{\epsilon_{11},\ldots,\epsilon_{KN}}\mathfrak{Z}(\alpha)=\mathfrak{Z}(\{\beta,\gamma\})
is stable under translation by
\sum_{b=1}^{N}\mathbb{Z}\boldsymbol{e}_{\{1,\ldots,k\},b}+\sum_{b=1}^{N}\mathbb{Z}\boldsymbol{e}_{\{k+1,\ldots,K\},b}
.
So
\mathfrak{Z}(\alpha)
contains a subset that is stable under translation by
p\cdot\left(\sum_{b=1}^{N}\mathbb{Z}\cdot\boldsymbol{e}_{\{1,\ldots,k\},b}+\sum_{b=1}^{N}\mathbb{Z}\cdot\boldsymbol{e}_{\{k+1,\ldots,K\},b}\right)
: this is another prototype of the subgroup
H
in
p
-succinct sets.

More generally, we can replace
\{1,\ldots,k\},\{k+1,\ldots,K\}
in the above example by any partition of
\{1,\ldots,K\}
.
This motivates the following definition.

Definition 3.35
.

A
partition
of the set
\{1,\ldots,K\}
is defined as a family
\Pi=\{S_{1},S_{2},\ldots,S_{r}\}
, where
S_{1},S_{2},\ldots,S_{r}
are non-empty disjoint subsets of
\{1,\ldots,K\}
, such that
S_{1}\cup\cdots\cup S_{r}=\{1,\ldots,K\}
.
The sets
S_{1},S_{2},\ldots,S_{r}
are called
blocks
of
\Pi
.
For any
b\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
and any subset
S\subseteq\{1,\ldots,K\}
, define

\boldsymbol{e}_{S,b}\coloneqq(e_{11},\ldots,e_{KN}),\quad e_{ij}=1\text{ for }i\in S,j=b;\text{ and }e_{ij}=0\text{ otherwise}.

(3.66)

For any partition
\Pi
of the set
\{1,\ldots,K\}
, define
(\mathbb{Z}^{N})^{\Pi}
to be the subgroup of
\mathbb{Z}^{KN}
generated by the elements
\boldsymbol{e}_{S,b},\;S\in\Pi,b\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
:

(\mathbb{Z}^{N})^{\Pi}\coloneqq\sum_{S\in\Pi}\sum_{b=1}^{N}\mathbb{Z}\boldsymbol{e}_{S,b}.

For a path
\pi
in the automaton
\mathscr{U}
, let
\mathsf{len}(\pi)
denote the length of
\pi
.
Recall that
\mathsf{eval}(\pi)\in\mathbb{Z}^{KN}
denotes the evaluation of
\pi
.
For two states
W,V
of the automaton
\mathscr{U}
, denote by
L(W,V)
the set of paths from
W
to
V
.

The following lemma shows that, whenever a state
W
of
\mathscr{U}
appears in two distinct cycles of the same length, the zero set
\mathfrak{Z}(W)
is stable under translation by a subgroup of the form
(\mathbb{Z}^{N})^{\Pi}
.

Lemma 3.36
.

Let
W\in 2^{\mathcal{H}}
be a state of the automaton
\mathscr{U}
.
Suppose there are two cycles
C_{1},C_{2}\in L(W,W)
such that
\mathsf{len}(C_{1})=\mathsf{len}(C_{2})=\ell
.
Write
\mathsf{eval}(C_{1})=(\boldsymbol{r}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{r}_{K})
,
\mathsf{eval}(C_{2})=(\boldsymbol{r}_{1}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{r}_{K}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{K})
, with
\boldsymbol{r}_{i},\boldsymbol{r}_{i}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}\in\{-(p^{\ell}-1),\ldots,0,\ldots,p^{\ell}-1\}^{N}
for all
i
.
Let
\Pi
be the partition of
\{1,\ldots,K\}
such that
i,j\in\{1,\ldots,K\}
fall in the same block of
\Pi
if and only if
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}=\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{j}
.
5
5

5

For example, if
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{1}=(5,6),\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{2}=(0,0),\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{3}=(5,6)
, then
\Pi
is the family
\big\{\{1,3\},\{2\}\big\}
.

Then

\mathfrak{Z}(W)=\mathfrak{Z}(W)+(\mathbb{Z}^{N})^{\Pi}.

(3.67)

Proof.

Let
S\subseteq\{1,\ldots,K\}
be any block of
\Pi
and let
b\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
, we will prove

\mathfrak{Z}(W)=\mathfrak{Z}(W)+\boldsymbol{e}_{S,b}

(3.68)

for the generator
\boldsymbol{e}_{S,b}
of
(\mathbb{Z}^{N})^{\Pi}
.

If
S=\{1,\ldots,K\}
then Equation (
3.67
) is obviously satisfied thanks to the homogeneity of each
\gamma\in W
.
Therefore suppose
S\neq\{1,\ldots,K\}
, pick another block
S^{\prime}\neq S
in the partition
\Pi
.

For each
\gamma\in W
,
\gamma(\boldsymbol{z}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{z}_{K})=\sum_{i=1}^{K}\overline{A}^{\boldsymbol{z}_{i}}w_{i}
, we claim that

\Theta_{\ell;\boldsymbol{r}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{r}_{K}}(\mathfrak{Z}(\gamma))\cap\Theta_{\ell;\boldsymbol{r}_{1}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{r}_{K}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{K}}(\mathfrak{Z}(\gamma))=\mathfrak{Z}(\gamma_{S})\cap\mathfrak{Z}(\gamma_{S^{\prime}})

(3.69)

where

\gamma_{S}(\boldsymbol{z}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{z}_{K})=\sum_{i\in\{1,\ldots,K\}\setminus S}\overline{A}^{p^{\ell}\boldsymbol{z}_{i}}x_{i},

(3.70)

with some
x_{i}\in\mathcal{V},i\in\{1,\ldots,K\}\setminus S
, and

\gamma_{S^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{z}_{K})=\sum_{i\in\{1,\ldots,K\}\setminus S^{\prime}}\overline{A}^{p^{\ell}\boldsymbol{z}_{i}}x^{\prime}_{i},

(3.71)

with some
x^{\prime}_{i}\in\mathcal{V},i\in\{1,\ldots,K\}\setminus S^{\prime}
.
6
6

6

Here, the subscript
S
in
\gamma_{S}
is meant to suggest that the coefficients
x_{i}
in
\gamma_{S}
vanish for
i\in S
. Same for
\gamma_{S^{\prime}}
.

Here,
\gamma_{S}
and
\gamma_{S^{\prime}}
can be seen as sequences over the tuple
\overline{A}^{p^{\ell}}\coloneqq\left(A_{1}^{p^{\ell}},\ldots,A_{N}^{p^{\ell}}\right)
.

Indeed, we have
(\boldsymbol{z}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{z}_{K})\in\Theta_{\ell;\boldsymbol{r}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{r}_{K}}(\mathfrak{Z}(\gamma))
if and only if

\sum_{i=1}^{K}\overline{A}^{p^{\ell}\boldsymbol{z}_{i}}\cdot\overline{A}^{\boldsymbol{r}_{i}}w_{i}=0.

(3.72)

Similarly, we have
(\boldsymbol{z}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{z}_{K})\in\Theta_{\ell;\boldsymbol{r}_{1}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{r}_{K}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{K}}(\mathfrak{Z}(\gamma))
if and only if

\sum_{i=1}^{K}\overline{A}^{p^{\ell}\boldsymbol{z}_{i}}\cdot\overline{A}^{\boldsymbol{r}_{i}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}}w_{i}=0.

(3.73)

Since
S
is a block in
\Pi
, there exists
\boldsymbol{a}\in\mathbb{Z}^{N}
, such that
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}=\boldsymbol{a}
for all
i\in S
, and
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}\neq\boldsymbol{a}
for all
i\notin S
.
Similarly, there exists
\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}^{N}
, such that
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}=\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}
for all
i\in S^{\prime}
, and
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}\neq\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}
for all
i\notin S^{\prime}
.
Of course,
\boldsymbol{a}\neq\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}
.
Let

\gamma_{S}\coloneqq\overline{A}^{\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot\left(\sum_{i=1}^{K}\overline{A}^{p^{\ell}\boldsymbol{z}_{i}}\cdot\overline{A}^{\boldsymbol{r}_{i}}w_{i}\right)-\left(\sum_{i=1}^{K}\overline{A}^{p^{\ell}\boldsymbol{z}_{i}}\cdot\overline{A}^{\boldsymbol{r}_{i}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}}w_{i}\right),

which can be written in the form (
3.70
) with
x_{i}=\overline{A}^{\boldsymbol{a}+\boldsymbol{r}_{i}}w_{i}-\overline{A}^{\boldsymbol{r}_{i}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}}w_{i}
.
This is because for
i\in S
, the coefficient
x_{i}
vanishes by
\boldsymbol{a}=\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}
.

Similarly, let

\gamma_{S^{\prime}}\coloneqq\overline{A}^{\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}}\cdot\left(\sum_{i=1}^{K}\overline{A}^{p^{\ell}\boldsymbol{z}_{i}}\cdot\overline{A}^{\boldsymbol{r}_{i}}w_{i}\right)-\left(\sum_{i=1}^{K}\overline{A}^{p^{\ell}\boldsymbol{z}_{i}}\cdot\overline{A}^{\boldsymbol{r}_{i}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}}w_{i}\right),

which can be written in the form (
3.71
) with
x^{\prime}_{i}=\overline{A}^{\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}+\boldsymbol{r}_{i}}w_{i}-\overline{A}^{\boldsymbol{r}_{i}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}}w_{i}
.
This is because for
i\in S^{\prime}
, the coefficient
x^{\prime}_{i}
vanishes.

The system of equations
\gamma_{S}=\gamma_{S^{\prime}}=0
is a linear transformation of the system of Equations (
3.72
) and (
3.73
).
We claim that the transformation matrix

\begin{pmatrix}\overline{A}^{\boldsymbol{a}}&-1\\
\overline{A}^{\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}}&-1\\
\end{pmatrix}

is invertible, so the two systems are equivalent.
Indeed, the determinant of the transformation matrix is
\overline{A}^{\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}}-\overline{A}^{\boldsymbol{a}}=\overline{A}^{\boldsymbol{a}}\left(\overline{A}^{\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{a}}-1\right)
. We have
\overline{A}^{\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{a}}\not\equiv 1\mod\mathfrak{m}
, by the multiplicative independence of
A_{1},\ldots,A_{N}
and Lemma
3.34
. Hence
\overline{A}^{\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{a}}-1\notin\mathfrak{m}
, and is therefore invertible.
Consequently the transformation matrix has an invertible determinant and is therefore invertible.
We thus conclude that the system
\gamma_{S}=\gamma_{S^{\prime}}=0
is equivalent to the system of Equations (
3.72
) and (
3.73
).
In other words,

\Theta_{\ell;\boldsymbol{r}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{r}_{K}}(\mathfrak{Z}(\gamma))\cap\Theta_{\ell;\boldsymbol{r}_{1}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{r}_{K}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{K}}(\mathfrak{Z}(\gamma))=\mathfrak{Z}(\gamma_{S})\cap\mathfrak{Z}(\gamma_{S^{\prime}}).

Since there are length-
\ell
paths from
W
to
W
evaluated at
(\boldsymbol{r}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{r}_{K})
and
(\boldsymbol{r}_{1}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{r}_{K}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{K})
, we have

\mathfrak{Z}(W)=\Theta_{\ell;\boldsymbol{r}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{r}_{K}}(\mathfrak{Z}(W))=\Theta_{\ell;\boldsymbol{r}_{1}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{r}_{K}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{K}}(\mathfrak{Z}(W)).

Therefore

\displaystyle\mathfrak{Z}(W)=

\displaystyle\;\Theta_{\ell;\boldsymbol{r}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{r}_{K}}(\mathfrak{Z}(W))\cap\Theta_{\ell;\boldsymbol{r}_{1}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{r}_{K}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{K}}(\mathfrak{Z}(W))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\bigcap_{\gamma\in W}\Theta_{\ell;\boldsymbol{r}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{r}_{K}}(\mathfrak{Z}(\gamma))\cap\bigcap_{\gamma\in W}\Theta_{\ell;\boldsymbol{r}_{1}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{r}_{K}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{K}}(\mathfrak{Z}(\gamma))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\bigcap_{\gamma\in W}\Big(\Theta_{\ell;\boldsymbol{r}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{r}_{K}}(\mathfrak{Z}(\gamma))\cap\Theta_{\ell;\boldsymbol{r}_{1}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{r}_{K}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{K}}(\mathfrak{Z}(\gamma))\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\bigcap_{\gamma\in W}\big(\mathfrak{Z}(\gamma_{S})\cap\mathfrak{Z}(\gamma_{S^{\prime}})\big).

Consider two cases:

Case 1:

If
S=\{1,\ldots,K\}\setminus S^{\prime}
.
Then both
\mathfrak{Z}(\gamma_{S})
and
\mathfrak{Z}(\gamma_{S^{\prime}})
are stable under translation by
\boldsymbol{e}_{S,b}
.
This is because
\gamma_{S^{\prime}}=\sum_{i\in\{1,\ldots,K\}\setminus S^{\prime}}\overline{A}^{p^{\ell}\boldsymbol{z}_{i}}x^{\prime}_{i}=\sum_{i\in S}\overline{A}^{p^{\ell}\boldsymbol{z}_{i}}x^{\prime}_{i}
contains only terms for
i\in S
, while
\gamma_{S}
does not contain terms for
i\in S
.
Consequently,
\mathfrak{Z}(W)
is stable under translation by
\boldsymbol{e}_{S,b}
.

Case 2:

If
S\subsetneq\{1,\ldots,K\}\setminus S^{\prime}
.
Then
\mathfrak{Z}(\gamma_{S})
is stable under translation by
\boldsymbol{e}_{S,b}
, but
\mathfrak{Z}(\gamma_{S^{\prime}})
is not necessarily stable under translation by
\boldsymbol{e}_{S,b}
.
Pick another set
S^{\prime\prime}\notin\{S,S^{\prime}\}
in the partition
\Pi
and repeat the above process for
(\gamma_{S},S^{\prime\prime})
and
(\gamma_{S^{\prime}},S^{\prime\prime})
in place of
(\gamma,S^{\prime})
.
That is, we can write

\Theta_{\ell;\boldsymbol{r}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{r}_{K}}(\mathfrak{Z}(\gamma_{S}))\cap\Theta_{\ell;\boldsymbol{r}_{1}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{r}_{K}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{K}}(\mathfrak{Z}(\gamma_{S}))=\mathfrak{Z}(\gamma_{S,S})\cap\mathfrak{Z}(\gamma_{S,S^{\prime\prime}}),

\Theta_{\ell;\boldsymbol{r}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{r}_{K}}(\mathfrak{Z}(\gamma_{S^{\prime}}))\cap\Theta_{\ell;\boldsymbol{r}_{1}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{r}_{K}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{K}}(\mathfrak{Z}(\gamma_{S^{\prime}}))=\mathfrak{Z}(\gamma_{S^{\prime},S})\cap\mathfrak{Z}(\gamma_{S^{\prime},S^{\prime\prime}}),

where each sequence
\gamma_{S_{1},S_{2}},\;S_{1},S_{2}\in\{S,S^{\prime},S^{\prime\prime}\}
, has the form

\gamma_{S_{1},S_{2}}=\sum_{i\in(\{1,\ldots,K\}\setminus S_{1})\setminus S_{2}}\overline{A}^{p^{2\ell}\boldsymbol{z}_{i}}x_{i}

with some
x_{i}\in\mathcal{V}
.
Then

\displaystyle\mathfrak{Z}(W)=

\displaystyle\;\Theta_{\ell;\boldsymbol{r}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{r}_{K}}(\mathfrak{Z}(W))\cap\Theta_{\ell;\boldsymbol{r}_{1}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{r}_{K}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{K}}(\mathfrak{Z}(W))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\Theta_{\ell;\boldsymbol{r}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{r}_{K}}\left(\bigcap_{\gamma\in W}\big(\mathfrak{Z}(\gamma_{S})\cap\mathfrak{Z}(\gamma_{S^{\prime}})\big)\right)\cap\Theta_{\ell;\boldsymbol{r}_{1}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{r}_{K}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{K}}\left(\bigcap_{\gamma\in W}\big(\mathfrak{Z}(\gamma_{S})\cap\mathfrak{Z}(\gamma_{S^{\prime}})\big)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\bigcap_{\gamma\in W}\big(\Theta_{\ell;\boldsymbol{r}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{r}_{K}}(\mathfrak{Z}(\gamma_{S}))\cap\Theta_{\ell;\boldsymbol{r}_{1}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{r}_{K}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{K}}(\mathfrak{Z}(\gamma_{S}))\big)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\cap\bigcap_{\gamma\in W}\Theta_{\ell;\boldsymbol{r}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{r}_{K}}(\mathfrak{Z}(\gamma_{S^{\prime}}))\cap\Theta_{\ell;\boldsymbol{r}_{1}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{r}_{K}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{K}}(\mathfrak{Z}(\gamma_{S^{\prime}}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\bigcap_{\gamma\in W}\big(\mathfrak{Z}(\gamma_{S,S})\cap\mathfrak{Z}(\gamma_{S,S^{\prime\prime}})\cap\mathfrak{Z}(\gamma_{S^{\prime},S})\cap\mathfrak{Z}(\gamma_{S^{\prime},S^{\prime\prime}})\big)

Repeating this process until we have found
S,S^{\prime},S^{\prime\prime},S^{\prime\prime\prime},\ldots
, such that the disjoint union
S\cup S^{\prime}\cup S^{\prime\prime}\cup S^{\prime\prime\prime}\cup\cdots
is equal to
\{1,\ldots,K\}
.
By doing so, we will have written

\mathfrak{Z}(W)=\bigcap_{\gamma\in W}\big(\mathfrak{Z}(\gamma_{S,S,S,\ldots})\cap\mathfrak{Z}(\gamma_{S,S^{\prime\prime},S,\ldots})\cap\cdots\cap\mathfrak{Z}(\gamma_{S^{\prime},S^{\prime\prime},S^{\prime\prime\prime},\ldots})\big),

where each set
\mathfrak{Z}(\gamma_{S_{1},S_{2},S_{3},\ldots})
except the last one contains
S
in the subscript (and is hence stable under translation by
\boldsymbol{e}_{S,b}
because the
\gamma_{S_{1},S_{2},S_{3},\ldots}
does not contain any term
x_{i}
with
i\in S
).
The last set,
\mathfrak{Z}(\gamma_{S^{\prime},S^{\prime\prime},S^{\prime\prime\prime},\ldots})
, is also stable under translation by
\boldsymbol{e}_{S,b}
because
S^{\prime}\cup S^{\prime\prime}\cup S^{\prime\prime\prime}\cup\cdots=\{1,\ldots,K\}\setminus S
, so
\gamma_{S^{\prime},S^{\prime\prime},S^{\prime\prime\prime},\ldots}
contains only terms
x_{i}
with
i\in S
.
Therefore, the total intersection
\mathfrak{Z}(W)
is also stable under translation by
\boldsymbol{e}_{S,b}
.

We have now shown that
\mathfrak{Z}(W)=\mathfrak{Z}(W)+\boldsymbol{e}_{S,b}
for each generator
\boldsymbol{e}_{S,b}
of
(\mathbb{Z}^{N})^{\Pi}
.
We conclude that
\mathfrak{Z}(W)=\mathfrak{Z}(W)+(\mathbb{Z}^{N})^{\Pi}
.
∎

For two cycles
C_{1},C_{2}\in L(W,W)
with
\mathsf{len}(C_{1})=\mathsf{len}(C_{2})
, we call the partition
\Pi
defined in Lemma
3.36
the partition
induced
by the pair
(C_{1},C_{2})
, and denote it by
\Pi_{(C_{1},C_{2})}
.

We say that a partition
\Pi
is
finer
than a partition
\Pi^{\prime}
, if each block of
\Pi
is a subset of some block of
\Pi^{\prime}
.
For example, the partition
\big\{\{1,2\},\{3\},\{4,5\}\big\}
is finer than the partition
\big\{\{1,2,3\},\{4,5\}\big\}
.
If
\Pi
is finer than
\Pi^{\prime}
, we write
\Pi\preceq\Pi^{\prime}
.
For two different partitions
\Pi,\Pi^{\prime}
, we can define their
meet
, denoted by
\Pi\wedge\Pi^{\prime}
, as the partition whose blocks are the intersections of a block of
\Pi
and a block of
\Pi^{\prime}
.
For example, if
\Pi
is the family
\big\{\{1,2\},\{3,4,5\}\big\}
and
\Pi^{\prime}
is the family
\big\{\{1,3\},\{2,4,5\}\big\}
, then
\Pi\wedge\Pi^{\prime}
is the family
\big\{\{1\},\{2\},\{3\},\{4,5\}\big\}
.
Obviously
\Pi\wedge\Pi^{\prime}\preceq\Pi
and
\Pi\wedge\Pi^{\prime}\preceq\Pi^{\prime}
.

Let
C_{1},C_{2},C^{\prime}_{1},C^{\prime}_{2}\in L(W,W)
be such that
\mathsf{len}(C_{1})=\mathsf{len}(C_{2}),\;\mathsf{len}(C^{\prime}_{1})=\mathsf{len}(C^{\prime}_{2})
, then we have
\mathsf{len}(C_{1}C_{1}C_{1}C^{\prime}_{1})=\mathsf{len}(C_{2}C_{1}C_{1}C^{\prime}_{2})
for the concatenated cycles
C_{1}C_{1}C_{1}C^{\prime}_{1},C_{2}C_{1}C_{1}C^{\prime}_{2}\in L(W,W)
.

Lemma 3.37
.

Let
C_{1},C_{2},C^{\prime}_{1},C^{\prime}_{2}\in L(W,W)
be such that
\mathsf{len}(C_{1})=\mathsf{len}(C_{2}),\;\mathsf{len}(C^{\prime}_{1})=\mathsf{len}(C^{\prime}_{2})
, then

\Pi_{(C_{1}C_{1}C_{1}C^{\prime}_{1},C_{2}C_{1}C_{1}C^{\prime}_{2})}=\Pi_{(C_{1},C_{2})}\wedge\Pi_{(C^{\prime}_{1},C^{\prime}_{2})}.

Proof.

Let
\ell
denote the length of
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
.
Write
\mathsf{eval}(C_{1})=(\boldsymbol{r}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{r}_{K})
with
\boldsymbol{r}_{i}\in\{-(p^{\ell}-1),\ldots,0,\ldots,p^{\ell}-1\}^{N}
, and
\mathsf{eval}(C_{2})=(\boldsymbol{r}_{1}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{r}_{K}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{K})
with
\boldsymbol{r}_{i}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}\in\{-(p^{\ell}-1),\ldots,0,\ldots,p^{\ell}-1\}^{N}
.
Then
\|\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}\|\leq 2p^{\ell}-2
, where
\|\boldsymbol{z}\|
denote the maximal absolute value among the entries of
\boldsymbol{z}
.
Similarly, let
\ell^{\prime}
denote the length of
C^{\prime}_{1}
and
C^{\prime}_{2}
, and write
\mathsf{eval}(C^{\prime}_{1})=(\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime}_{K})
,
\mathsf{eval}(C^{\prime}_{2})=(\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime}_{1}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime}_{K}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}_{K})
.

By direct computation,
\mathsf{eval}(C_{1}C_{1}C_{1}C^{\prime}_{1})
amounts to

\left(\boldsymbol{r}_{1}+p^{\ell}\boldsymbol{r}_{1}+p^{2\ell}\boldsymbol{r}_{1}+p^{3\ell}\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{r}_{K}+p^{\ell}\boldsymbol{r}_{K}+p^{2\ell}\boldsymbol{r}_{K}+p^{3\ell}\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime}_{K}\right),

whereas
\mathsf{eval}(C_{2}C_{1}C_{1}C^{\prime}_{2})
amounts to

\left((\boldsymbol{r}_{1}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{1})+p^{\ell}\boldsymbol{r}_{1}+p^{2\ell}\boldsymbol{r}_{1}+p^{3\ell}(\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime}_{1}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}_{1}),\ldots,(\boldsymbol{r}_{K}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{K})+p^{\ell}\boldsymbol{r}_{K}+p^{2\ell}\boldsymbol{r}_{K}+p^{3\ell}(\boldsymbol{r}^{\prime}_{K}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}_{K})\right).

Hence, the difference
\mathsf{eval}(C_{2}C_{1}C_{1}C^{\prime}_{2})-\mathsf{eval}(C_{1}C_{1}C_{1}C^{\prime}_{1})
amounts to

\left(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{1}+p^{3\ell}\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{K}+p^{3\ell}\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}_{K}\right).

(3.74)

Recall that
i,j\in\{1,\ldots,K\}
fall in the same block of
\Pi_{(C_{1},C_{2})}
if and only if
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}=\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{j}
.
Similarly,
i,j
fall in the same block of
\Pi_{(C^{\prime}_{1},C^{\prime}_{2})}
if and only if
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}_{i}=\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}_{j}
.
Therefore
i,j
fall in the same block of
\Pi_{(C_{1},C_{2})}\wedge\Pi_{(C^{\prime}_{1},C^{\prime}_{2})}
if and only if both
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}=\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{j}
and
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}_{i}=\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}_{j}
.
Therefore, if
i,j
fall in the same block of
\Pi_{(C_{1},C_{2})}\wedge\Pi_{(C^{\prime}_{1},C^{\prime}_{2})}
, then we have
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}+p^{3\ell}\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}_{i}=\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{j}+p^{3\ell}\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}_{j}
, so
i,j
are in the same block of
\Pi_{(C_{1}C_{1}C_{1}C^{\prime}_{1},C_{2}C_{1}C_{1}C^{\prime}_{2})}
.

On the other hand, if
i,j
fall in the same block of
\Pi_{(C_{1}C_{1}C_{1}C^{\prime}_{1},C_{2}C_{1}C_{1}C^{\prime}_{2})}
, then
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}+p^{3\ell}\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}_{i}=\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{j}+p^{3\ell}\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}_{j}
, which can be rewritten as
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{j}=p^{3\ell}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}_{j}-\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}_{i})
.
But
\|\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{j}\|\leq(2p^{\ell}-2)+(2p^{\ell}-2)<4p^{\ell}\leq p^{3\ell}
, so we must have
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}_{j}=0
, and consequently
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{j}=0
.
This shows that
i,j
fall in the same block of
\Pi_{(C_{1},C_{2})}\wedge\Pi_{(C^{\prime}_{1},C^{\prime}_{2})}
.

We conclude that
\Pi_{(C_{1}C_{1}C_{1}C^{\prime}_{1},C_{2}C_{1}C_{1}C^{\prime}_{2})}=\Pi_{(C_{1},C_{2})}\wedge\Pi_{(C^{\prime}_{1},C^{\prime}_{2})}
.
∎

Lemma
3.37
shows that, if
C_{1},C_{2},C^{\prime}_{1},C^{\prime}_{2}\in L(W,W)
are such that
\mathsf{len}(C_{1})=\mathsf{len}(C_{2}),\mathsf{len}(C^{\prime}_{1})=\mathsf{len}(C^{\prime}_{2})
, then there exist
C^{\prime\prime}_{1},C^{\prime\prime}_{2}\in L(W,W),\;\mathsf{len}(C^{\prime\prime}_{1})=\mathsf{len}(C^{\prime\prime}_{2})
, such that
\Pi_{(C^{\prime\prime}_{1},C^{\prime\prime}_{2})}=\Pi_{(C_{1},C_{2})}\wedge\Pi_{(C^{\prime}_{1},C^{\prime}_{2})}
.
This means that the set of partitions

\mathscr{P}(W)\coloneqq\left\{\Pi_{(C_{1},C_{2})}\;\middle|\;C_{1},C_{2}\in L(W,W),\;\mathsf{len}(C_{1})=\mathsf{len}(C_{2})\right\}

is closed under the meet operation (that is,
\Pi,\Pi^{\prime}\in\mathscr{P}(W)\implies\Pi\wedge\Pi^{\prime}\in\mathscr{P}(W)
).
Therefore,
\mathscr{P}(W)
contains a finest element, which we denote by
\Pi(W)
.
Namely,

\Pi(W)=\bigwedge_{C_{1},C_{2}\in L(W,W),\;\mathsf{len}(C_{1})=\mathsf{len}(C_{2})}\Pi_{(C_{1},C_{2})}.

Lemma 3.38
.

For any state
W
, the partition
\Pi(W)
can be effectively computed.

Proof.

It suffices to show that for any
i,j\in\{1,\ldots,K\}
, we can check whether
i,j
belong to the same block of
\Pi(W)
.
This can be done the following way.
For any cycle
C
, write
\mathsf{eval}(C)=\left(\mathsf{eval}(C)_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{eval}(C)_{K}\right)
, where each
\mathsf{eval}(C)_{i}
is a vector in
\mathbb{Z}^{N}
.
Then
i,j
belong to the same block of
\Pi(W)
if and only if

\forall C_{1},C_{2}\in L(W,W),\;\mathsf{len}(C_{1})=\mathsf{len}(C_{2})\implies\mathsf{eval}(C_{2})_{i}-\mathsf{eval}(C_{1})_{i}=\mathsf{eval}(C_{2})_{j}-\mathsf{eval}(C_{1})_{j}.

(3.75)

Modifying the automaton that accepts
L(W,W)
, it is easy to see that the set

\Big\{\big(p^{\mathsf{len}(C)},\mathsf{eval}(C)\big)\;\Big|\;C\in L(W,W)\Big\}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}^{KN}

is effectively
p
-automatic.
By Lemma
2.1
(2), the set

L_{ji}\coloneqq\Big\{\big(p^{\mathsf{len}(C)},\mathsf{eval}(C)_{j}-\mathsf{eval}(C)_{i}\big)\;\Big|\;C\in L(W,W)\Big\}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}^{N}

is effectively
p
-automatic.
Therefore the set

L_{ji}-L_{ji}\coloneqq\big\{(a_{1}-a_{2},b_{1}-b_{2})\;\big|\;(a_{1},b_{1}),(a_{2},b_{2})\in L_{ji}\big\}\\
=\bigg\{\big(p^{\mathsf{len}(C_{1})}-p^{\mathsf{len}(C_{2})},\big(\mathsf{eval}(C_{1})_{j}-\mathsf{eval}(C_{1})_{i}\big)-\big(\mathsf{eval}(C_{2})_{j}-\mathsf{eval}(C_{2})_{i}\big)\;\bigg|\;C_{1},C_{2}\in L(W,W)\bigg\}

is effectively
p
-automatic.
Therefore Condition (
3.75
) is equivalent to “
(L_{ji}-L_{ji})\cap(\{0\}\times\mathbb{Z}^{N})=\{(0,\boldsymbol{0})\}
”, which can be effectively verified
[
WB00
]
.
∎

The lemmas above gave an intuition of the subgroup
H
in the definition (
1.3
) of
p
-succinct sets.
In fact,
H
will be a suitable modification of the subgroup
(\mathbb{Z}^{N})^{\Pi(W)}
, where
W
ranges over the states of
\mathscr{U}
.
Next, we start working towards the term
p^{\ell k_{i}}\boldsymbol{a}_{i}
in the Equation (
1.3
).

Prototype of the term
p^{\ell k_{i}}\boldsymbol{a}_{i}
.

The following lemma characterizes the evaluation of cycles in
L(W,W)
, up to quotient by the subgroup
(\mathbb{Z}^{N})^{\Pi(W)}
identified in the previous lemmas.

Lemma 3.39
.

Let
W
be a state. Then there exists
\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathbb{Q}^{KN}
, whose denominators are not divisible by
p
, such that for every cycle
C\in L(W,W)
, we have

\mathsf{eval}(C)\in\left(p^{\mathsf{len}(C)}-1\right)\boldsymbol{b}+(\mathbb{Z}^{N})^{\Pi(W)}.

(3.76)

Proof.

Let
S_{1}=\{s_{11},\ldots,s_{1n_{1}}\},\ldots,S_{r}=\{s_{r1},\ldots,s_{rn_{r}}\}
be the blocks of
\Pi(W)
.
For
i\in\{1,\ldots,K\}
,
j\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
, let
\boldsymbol{e}_{ij}
denote the vector
(z_{11},\ldots,z_{KN})
where
z_{ij}=1
and all the other entries are
0
.
Recall the definition (
3.66
) of the generators
\boldsymbol{e}_{S_{1},b},\ldots,\boldsymbol{e}_{S_{r},b},b=1,\ldots,N
of
(\mathbb{Z}^{N})^{\Pi(W)}
.
It is easy to see that they can be extended to a
\mathbb{Z}
-basis

\boldsymbol{e}_{S_{1},b},\boldsymbol{e}_{s_{11}b},\boldsymbol{e}_{s_{12}b},\ldots,\boldsymbol{e}_{s_{1(n_{1}-1)}b},\ldots,\boldsymbol{e}_{S_{r},b},\boldsymbol{e}_{s_{r1}b},\boldsymbol{e}_{s_{r2}b},\ldots,\boldsymbol{e}_{s_{r(n_{r}-1)}b},\;b=1,\ldots,N,

of
\mathbb{Z}^{KN}
.
Therefore
\mathbb{Z}^{KN}
splits as a direct sum

\mathbb{Z}^{KN}=(\mathbb{Z}^{N})^{\Pi(W)}\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{M}

with
M=(K-r)N
.
We will write each element
\boldsymbol{z}
of
\mathbb{Z}^{KN}
as a pair
(\boldsymbol{z}_{\Pi},\boldsymbol{z}_{\perp})\in(\mathbb{Z}^{N})^{\Pi(W)}\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{M}
according to this direct sum.
Note that this naturally extends to a direct sum
\mathbb{Q}^{KN}=(\mathbb{Q}^{N})^{\Pi(W)}\oplus\mathbb{Q}^{M}
.

Let
C_{1},C_{2}\in L(W,W)
be two cycles of the same length.
By definition,
\mathsf{eval}(C_{1})-\mathsf{eval}(C_{2})
belongs to
(\mathbb{Z}^{N})^{\Pi(W)}
.
This means that
\mathsf{eval}(C_{1})_{\perp}=\mathsf{eval}(C_{2})_{\perp}
.

Let
C,C^{\prime}\in L(W,W)
be two cycles, not necessarily of the same length.
Then the two concatenations
C^{\mathsf{len}(C^{\prime})}\coloneqq\underbrace{CC\cdots C}_{\text{$\mathsf{len}(C^{\prime})$ iterations}}\in L(W,W)
and
(C^{\prime})^{\mathsf{len}(C)}\coloneqq\underbrace{C^{\prime}C^{\prime}\cdots C^{\prime}}_{\text{$\mathsf{len}(C)$ iterations}}\in L(W,W)
have the same length.
So
\mathsf{eval}\left((C^{\prime})^{\mathsf{len}(C)}\right)_{\perp}=\mathsf{eval}\left(C^{\mathsf{len}(C^{\prime})}\right)_{\perp}
.
Since
\mathsf{eval}\left((C^{\prime})^{\mathsf{len}(C)}\right)=\frac{p^{\mathsf{len}(C^{\prime})\mathsf{len}(C)}-1}{p^{\mathsf{len}(C^{\prime})}-1}\cdot\mathsf{eval}(C^{\prime})
, and
\mathsf{eval}\left(C^{\mathsf{len}(C^{\prime})}\right)=\frac{p^{\mathsf{len}(C)\mathsf{len}(C^{\prime})}-1}{p^{\mathsf{len}(C)}-1}\cdot\mathsf{eval}(C)
, this yields

\frac{p^{\mathsf{len}(C^{\prime})\mathsf{len}(C)}-1}{p^{\mathsf{len}(C^{\prime})}-1}\cdot\mathsf{eval}(C^{\prime})_{\perp}=\frac{p^{\mathsf{len}(C)\mathsf{len}(C^{\prime})}-1}{p^{\mathsf{len}(C)}-1}\cdot\mathsf{eval}(C)_{\perp}.

Consequently,

\frac{\mathsf{eval}(C^{\prime})_{\perp}}{p^{\mathsf{len}(C^{\prime})}-1}=\frac{\mathsf{eval}(C)_{\perp}}{p^{\mathsf{len}(C)}-1}.

This means that, there exists a constant
\boldsymbol{a}\in\mathbb{Q}^{M}
, such that
\frac{\mathsf{eval}(C)_{\perp}}{p^{\mathsf{len}(C)}-1}=\boldsymbol{a}
for all
C\in L(W,W)
.

Let
\boldsymbol{b}\coloneqq(\boldsymbol{0}_{\Pi},\boldsymbol{a})\in(\mathbb{Q}^{N})^{\Pi(W)}\oplus\mathbb{Q}^{M}
.
Since
p\nmid p^{\mathsf{len}(C)}-1
and
(p^{\mathsf{len}(C)}-1)\boldsymbol{b}=(\boldsymbol{0}_{\Pi},\mathsf{eval}(C)_{\perp})\in\mathbb{Z}^{KN}
, the denominators of
\boldsymbol{b}
are not divisible by
p
.
Then,

\mathsf{eval}(C)-\left(p^{\mathsf{len}(C)}-1\right)\boldsymbol{b}=\mathsf{eval}(C)-(\boldsymbol{0}_{\Pi},\mathsf{eval}(C)_{\perp})=\left(\mathsf{eval}(C)_{\Pi},\boldsymbol{0}_{\perp}\right)\in(\mathbb{Z}^{N})^{\Pi(W)}.

Therefore
\mathsf{eval}(C)\in\left(p^{\mathsf{len}(C)}-1\right)\boldsymbol{b}+(\mathbb{Z}^{N})^{\Pi(W)}
.
∎

We can easily extend Lemma
3.39
from cycles in
L(W,W)
to paths in
L(W,V)
, provided that
W,V
are states in the same strongly connected component of
\mathscr{U}
:

Lemma 3.40
.

Let
W,V
be two states in the same strongly connected component of
\mathscr{U}
.
Then there exist
\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{c}\in\mathbb{Q}^{KN}
, whose denominators are not divisible by
p
, such that for every path
\pi\in L(W,V)
, we have

\mathsf{eval}(\pi)\in p^{\mathsf{len}(\pi)}\boldsymbol{b}+\boldsymbol{c}+(\mathbb{Z}^{N})^{\Pi(W)}.

(3.77)

Proof.

Let
\pi_{VW}
be any path in
L(V,W)
.
Then for any path
\pi\in L(W,V)
, the concatenation
\pi\pi_{VW}
is a cycle in
L(W,W)
.

By Lemma
3.39
, there exists
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{b}}\in\mathbb{Q}^{KN}
such that for all
\pi\in L(W,V)
, we have

\mathsf{eval}(\pi\pi_{VW})\in\left(p^{\mathsf{len}(\pi\pi_{VW})}-1\right)\widetilde{\boldsymbol{b}}+(\mathbb{Z}^{N})^{\Pi(W)}.

Since
\mathsf{eval}(\pi\pi_{VW})=\mathsf{eval}(\pi)+p^{\mathsf{len}(\pi)}\cdot\mathsf{eval}(\pi_{VW})
and
\mathsf{len}(\pi\pi_{VW})=\mathsf{len}(\pi)+\mathsf{len}(\pi_{VW})
, we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{eval}(\pi)

\displaystyle\in-p^{\mathsf{len}(\pi)}\cdot\mathsf{eval}(\pi_{VW})+\left(p^{\mathsf{len}(\pi)+\mathsf{len}(\pi_{VW})}-1\right)\widetilde{\boldsymbol{b}}+(\mathbb{Z}^{N})^{\Pi(W)}

\displaystyle=p^{\mathsf{len}(\pi)}\left(-\mathsf{eval}(\pi_{VW})+p^{\mathsf{len}(\pi_{VW})}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{b}}\right)-\widetilde{\boldsymbol{b}}+(\mathbb{Z}^{N})^{\Pi(W)}.

Thus we obtain the statement (
3.77
) by taking
\boldsymbol{b}\coloneqq-\mathsf{eval}(\pi_{VW})+p^{\mathsf{len}(\pi_{VW})}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{b}}
, and
\boldsymbol{c}\coloneqq-\widetilde{\boldsymbol{b}}
.
The denominators of
\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{c}
are not divisible by
p
since the denominators of
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{b}}
are not divisible by
p
.
∎

For each pair of states
W,V
in the same strongly connected component, we can find such vectors
\boldsymbol{b},\boldsymbol{c}\in\mathbb{Q}^{KN}
as in Lemma
3.40
.
In what follows we will denote them by
\boldsymbol{b}_{W,V},\boldsymbol{c}_{W,V},
when we want to stress their dependence on
W,V
.

For two states
W,V
of the automaton
\mathscr{U}
, define

\Lambda(W,V)\coloneqq\{\mathsf{len}(\pi)\mid\pi\in L(W,V)\}\subseteq\mathbb{N},

that is, the set of lengths of paths from
W
to
V
.
The following folklore result characterizes
\Lambda(W,V)
.

Lemma 3.41

(See
[
Koz12
]
or
[
Haa18
]
)
.

Let
W,V
be two states in the automaton
\mathscr{U}
.
Then
\Lambda(W,V)
can be effectively written as the union of a finite set and finitely many arithmetic progressions.

We now give an intuition of the the term
p^{\ell k_{i}}\boldsymbol{a}_{i}
in the definition (
1.3
) of
p
-succinct sets.
One can see from Lemma
3.40
that
p^{\ell k_{i}}\boldsymbol{a}_{i}
will be a suitable modification of the term
p^{\mathsf{len}(\pi)}\cdot\boldsymbol{b}_{W,V}
, where
\pi
is the part of an accepting run within a strongly connected component.
Note that Lemma
3.41
shows that the value of
\mathsf{len}(\pi)
must fall in a union of a finite set and finitely many arithmetic progressions.
The common difference
\lambda_{j}
of these arithmetic progressions will constitute the value
\ell
in the term
p^{\ell k_{i}}\boldsymbol{a}_{i}
.

Next, we start working towards characterizing the zero set
\mathfrak{Z}(\alpha)
as a finite union of “prototype”
p
-succinct sets.

Finite union of “prototype”
p
-succinct sets.

For a set of paths
L
in the automaton
\mathscr{U}
, denote

\mathsf{eval}(L)\coloneqq\{\mathsf{eval}(\pi)\mid\pi\in L\}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{KN}.

Recall that
\mathcal{F}
denotes the set of all accepting states of
\mathscr{U}
. For any state
W
in
\mathscr{U}
, we have

\mathfrak{Z}(W)=\mathsf{eval}\left(\bigcup_{F\in\mathcal{F}}L(W,F)\right).

The automaton
\mathscr{U}
can be decomposed into strongly connected components.
Accordingly, each path
\pi\in\bigcup_{F\in\mathcal{F}}L(\{\alpha\},F)
can be decomposed as a concatenation

\pi=\pi_{W_{1},V_{1}}\cdot\delta_{V_{1},W_{2}}\cdot\pi_{W_{2},V_{2}}\cdot\delta_{V_{2},W_{3}}\cdot\cdots\cdot\delta_{V_{r-1},W_{r}}\cdot\pi_{W_{r},V_{r}},

(3.78)

where

(1)

For
i=1,\ldots,r
, each
\pi_{W_{i},V_{i}}
is a path in
L(W_{i},V_{i})
, where
W_{i}
and
V_{i}
are in the same strongly connected component of
\mathscr{U}
.

(2)

For
i=1,\ldots,r-1
, each
\delta_{V_{i},W_{i+1}}
is a
transition
from
V_{i}
to
W_{i+1}
, where
V_{i}
and
W_{i+1}
are in
different
strongly connected components of
\mathscr{U}
.

(3)

W_{1}=\{\alpha\}
is the initial state.

(4)

V_{r}=F
is an accepting state.

Let
W_{1},V_{1},\ldots,W_{r},V_{r}
, be states of the automaton
\mathscr{U}
.
We will write the diagram

\{\alpha\}=W_{1}\rightsquigarrow V_{1}\rightarrow W_{2}\rightsquigarrow V_{2}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow W_{r}\rightsquigarrow V_{r}\in\mathcal{F}

if

(1)

For
i=1,\ldots,r
, the states
W_{i},V_{i}
are in the same strongly connected component of
\mathscr{U}
.

(2)

For
i=1,\ldots,r-1
, there exists a transition from
V_{i}
to
W_{i+1}
. Furthermore,
V_{i}
and
W_{i+1}
are in different strongly connected components of
\mathscr{U}
.

(3)

W_{1}=\{\alpha\}
is the initial state.

(4)

V_{r}\in\mathcal{F}
is an accepting state.

See Figure
6
for an illustration. Note that
r
is bounded by the number of strongly connected components of
\mathscr{U}
.

Figure 6:
A path validating
\{\alpha\}=W_{1}\rightsquigarrow V_{1}\rightarrow W_{2}\rightsquigarrow V_{2}\rightarrow W_{3}\rightsquigarrow V_{3}\rightarrow W_{4}\rightsquigarrow V_{4}\in\mathcal{F}
.

The above discussion shows that the zero set
\mathfrak{Z}(\alpha)=\mathsf{eval}\left(\bigcup_{F\in\mathcal{F}}L(\{\alpha\},F)\right)
can be written as a finite union

\bigcup_{\{\alpha\}=W_{1}\rightsquigarrow V_{1}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow W_{r}\rightsquigarrow V_{r}\in\mathcal{F}}\mathsf{eval}\Big(L(W_{1},V_{1})\cdot\delta_{V_{1},W_{2}}\cdot L(W_{2},V_{2})\cdot\cdots\cdot\delta_{V_{r-1},W_{r}}\cdot L(W_{r},V_{r})\Big),

(3.79)

where the concatenation
L\cdot\delta\cdot L^{\prime}\cdots
denotes the set of paths
\{\pi\delta\pi^{\prime}\cdots\mid\pi\in L,\pi^{\prime}\in L^{\prime},\ldots\}
.
Note that this union is not necessarily disjoint because different paths may have the same evaluation.

For
i=1,\ldots,r
, define the integer vector

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{d}_{V_{i},W_{i+1}}\coloneqq\begin{cases}\mathsf{eval}(\delta_{V_{i},W_{i+1}})&\quad\text{ for }1\leq i\leq r-1,\\
0&\quad\text{ for }i=r.\end{cases}

By Lemma
3.40
, there exist vectors
\boldsymbol{b}_{W_{i},V_{i}},\boldsymbol{c}_{W_{i},V_{i}}\in\mathbb{Q}^{KN},\;i=1,\ldots,r
, whose denominators are not divisible by
p
, such that

\mathsf{eval}(\pi)\in p^{\mathsf{len}(\pi)}\cdot\boldsymbol{b}_{W_{i},V_{i}}+\boldsymbol{c}_{W_{i},V_{i}}+(\mathbb{Z}^{N})^{\Pi(W_{i})}

(3.80)

for all
\pi\in L(W_{i},V_{i})
.
We now characterize the zero set
\mathfrak{Z}(\alpha)
using the vectors
\boldsymbol{d}_{V_{i},W_{i+1}},\boldsymbol{b}_{W_{i},V_{i}},\boldsymbol{c}_{W_{i},V_{i}}
,
i=1,\ldots,r
.

Proposition 3.42
.

The zero set
\mathfrak{Z}(\alpha)
is equal to the finite union

\displaystyle\bigcup_{\{\alpha\}=W_{1}\rightsquigarrow V_{1}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow W_{r}\rightsquigarrow V_{r}\in\mathcal{F}}

\displaystyle\Bigg\{\sum_{i=1}^{r}p^{(\ell_{1}+1)+\cdots+(\ell_{i-1}+1)}\left(p^{\ell_{i}}\boldsymbol{b}_{W_{i},V_{i}}+\boldsymbol{c}_{W_{i},V_{i}}+p^{\ell_{i}}\boldsymbol{d}_{V_{i},W_{i+1}}+\boldsymbol{h}_{i}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\;\Bigg|\;\forall i,\ell_{i}\in\Lambda(W_{i},V_{i}),\boldsymbol{h}_{i}\in(\mathbb{Z}^{N})^{\Pi(W_{i})}\Bigg\}.

(3.81)

Before giving the proof of Proposition
3.42
, we would like to clarify a potentially misleading point.
The similarity between the unions (
3.79
) and (
3.42
) might lead one to falsely conjecture a term-wise equality.
However, as we will show later, we only have an inclusion

\mathsf{eval}\Big(L(W_{1},V_{1})\cdot\delta_{V_{1},W_{2}}\cdot L(W_{2},V_{2})\cdot\cdots\cdot\delta_{V_{r-1},W_{r}}\cdot L(W_{r},V_{r})\Big)\subseteq\\
\Bigg\{\sum_{i=1}^{r}p^{(\ell_{1}+1)+\cdots+(\ell_{i-1}+1)}\left(p^{\ell_{i}}\boldsymbol{b}_{W_{i},V_{i}}+\boldsymbol{c}_{W_{i},V_{i}}+p^{\ell_{i}}\boldsymbol{d}_{V_{i},W_{i+1}}+\boldsymbol{h}_{i}\right)\\
\;\Bigg|\;\forall i,\ell_{i}\in\Lambda(W_{i},V_{i}),\boldsymbol{h}_{i}\in(\mathbb{Z}^{N})^{\Pi(W_{i})}\Bigg\},

where the inclusion “
\subseteq
” might be a strict one.
The proof of Proposition
3.42
is more subtle than simply proving a term-wise equality.
We will need to combine the expression (
3.79
) with the stability condition
\mathfrak{Z}(W_{i})=\mathfrak{Z}(W_{i})+(\mathbb{Z}^{N})^{\Pi(W_{i})}
from Lemma
3.36
for each
i=1,2,\ldots,r
.

Proof of Proposition
3.42
.

First we show the inclusion

\mathfrak{Z}(\alpha)\subseteq\bigcup_{\{\alpha\}=W_{1}\rightsquigarrow V_{1}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow W_{r}\rightsquigarrow V_{r}\in\mathcal{F}}\Bigg\{\sum_{i=1}^{r}p^{(\ell_{1}+1)+\cdots+(\ell_{i-1}+1)}\left(p^{\ell_{i}}\boldsymbol{b}_{W_{i},V_{i}}+\boldsymbol{c}_{W_{i},V_{i}}+p^{\ell_{i}}\boldsymbol{d}_{V_{i},W_{i+1}}+\boldsymbol{h}_{i}\right)\\
\;\Bigg|\;\forall i,\ell_{i}\in\Lambda(W_{i},V_{i}),\boldsymbol{h}_{i}\in(\mathbb{Z}^{N})^{\Pi(W_{i})}\Bigg\}.

(3.82)

This is the easier direction.
Take any accepting path
\pi\in L(\{\alpha\},F),F\in\mathcal{F}
, then as in the Decomposition (
3.78
) we can write
\pi
as a concatenation

\pi=\pi_{W_{1},V_{1}}\cdot\delta_{V_{1},W_{2}}\cdot\pi_{W_{2},V_{2}}\cdot\delta_{V_{2},W_{3}}\cdot\cdots\cdot\delta_{V_{r-1},W_{r}}\cdot\pi_{W_{r},V_{r}},

where
W_{1},V_{1},\ldots,W_{r},V_{r}
satisfy the diagram
\{\alpha\}=W_{1}\rightsquigarrow V_{1}\rightarrow W_{2}\rightsquigarrow V_{2}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow W_{r}\rightsquigarrow V_{r}\in\mathcal{F}
.
Denote
\ell_{1}\coloneqq\mathsf{len}(\pi_{W_{1},V_{1}}),\ell_{2}\coloneqq\mathsf{len}(\pi_{W_{2},V_{2}}),\ldots,\ell_{r}\coloneqq\mathsf{len}(\pi_{W_{r},V_{r}})
, then

\displaystyle\;\mathsf{eval}(\pi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\mathsf{eval}\left(\pi_{W_{1},V_{1}}\cdot\delta_{V_{1},W_{2}}\cdot\pi_{W_{2},V_{2}}\cdot\delta_{V_{2},W_{3}}\cdot\cdots\cdot\delta_{V_{r-1},W_{r}}\cdot\pi_{W_{r},V_{r}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\mathsf{eval}\left(\pi_{W_{1},V_{1}}\right)+p^{\ell_{1}}\cdot\mathsf{eval}\left(\delta_{V_{1},W_{2}}\right)+p^{\ell_{1}+1}\cdot\mathsf{eval}\left(\pi_{W_{2},V_{2}}\right)+\cdots+p^{\ell_{1}+1+\cdots+\ell_{r-1}+1}\cdot\mathsf{eval}\left(\pi_{W_{r},V_{r}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\mathsf{eval}\left(\pi_{W_{1},V_{1}}\right)+p^{\ell_{1}}\cdot\boldsymbol{d}_{V_{1},W_{2}}+p^{\ell_{1}+1}\cdot\mathsf{eval}\left(\pi_{W_{2},V_{2}}\right)+\cdots+p^{\ell_{1}+1+\cdots+\ell_{r-1}+1}\cdot\mathsf{eval}\left(\pi_{W_{r},V_{r}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\sum_{i=1}^{r}p^{(\ell_{1}+1)+\cdots+(\ell_{i-1}+1)}\cdot\Big(\mathsf{eval}(\pi_{W_{i},V_{i}})+p^{\ell_{i}}\cdot\boldsymbol{d}_{V_{i},W_{i+1}}\Big).

By Equation (
3.80
), we have
\mathsf{eval}(\pi_{W_{i},V_{i}})\in p^{\ell_{i}}\cdot\boldsymbol{b}_{W_{i},V_{i}}+\boldsymbol{c}_{W_{i},V_{i}}+(\mathbb{Z}^{N})^{\Pi(W_{i})}
.
Therefore

\mathsf{eval}(\pi)\in\sum_{i=1}^{r}p^{(\ell_{1}+1)+\cdots+(\ell_{i-1}+1)}\cdot\Big(p^{\ell_{i}}\cdot\boldsymbol{b}_{W_{i},V_{i}}+\boldsymbol{c}_{W_{i},V_{i}}+(\mathbb{Z}^{N})^{\Pi(W_{i})}+p^{\ell_{i}}\cdot\boldsymbol{d}_{V_{i},W_{i+1}}\Big).

Since
\ell_{i}=\mathsf{len}(\pi_{W_{i},V_{i}})\in\Lambda(W_{i},V_{i})
for all
i=1,\ldots,r
, this proves the inclusion (
3.82
).

Next we show the other inclusion

\bigcup_{\{\alpha\}=W_{1}\rightsquigarrow V_{1}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow W_{r}\rightsquigarrow V_{r}\in\mathcal{F}}\Bigg\{\sum_{i=1}^{r}p^{(\ell_{1}+1)+\cdots+(\ell_{i-1}+1)}\left(p^{\ell_{i}}\boldsymbol{b}_{W_{i},V_{i}}+\boldsymbol{c}_{W_{i},V_{i}}+p^{\ell_{i}}\boldsymbol{d}_{V_{i},W_{i+1}}+\boldsymbol{h}_{i}\right)\\
\;\Bigg|\;\forall i,\ell_{i}\in\Lambda(W_{i},V_{i}),\boldsymbol{h}_{i}\in(\mathbb{Z}^{N})^{\Pi(W_{i})}\Bigg\}\subseteq\mathfrak{Z}(\alpha).

(3.83)

This is the more difficult direction, see Figure
7
for an illustration of the proof.
Take any states
W_{1},V_{1},\ldots,W_{r},V_{r}
satisfying
\{\alpha\}=W_{1}\rightsquigarrow V_{1}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow W_{r}\rightsquigarrow V_{r}\in\mathcal{F}
, and take
\ell_{i}\in\Lambda(W_{i},V_{i}),\boldsymbol{h}_{i}\in(\mathbb{Z}^{N})^{\Pi(W_{i})}
for
i=1,\ldots,r
.

For
j=r,r-1,\ldots,2,1
, define

\boldsymbol{z}_{j}\coloneqq\sum_{i=j}^{r}p^{(\ell_{j}+1)+\cdots+(\ell_{i-1}+1)}\left(p^{\ell_{i}}\boldsymbol{b}_{W_{i},V_{i}}+\boldsymbol{c}_{W_{i},V_{i}}+p^{\ell_{i}}\boldsymbol{d}_{V_{i},W_{i+1}}+\boldsymbol{h}_{i}\right).

In particular,
\boldsymbol{z}_{r}=p^{\ell_{r}}\boldsymbol{b}_{W_{r},V_{r}}+\boldsymbol{c}_{W_{r},V_{r}}+\boldsymbol{h}_{r}
, and

\boldsymbol{z}_{j}=\left(p^{\ell_{i}}\boldsymbol{b}_{W_{i},V_{i}}+\boldsymbol{c}_{W_{i},V_{i}}+p^{\ell_{i}}\boldsymbol{d}_{V_{i},W_{i+1}}+\boldsymbol{h}_{i}\right)+p^{\ell_{j}+1}\boldsymbol{z}_{j+1}

(3.84)

for
j\leq r-1
.
We will now show
\boldsymbol{z}_{j}\in\mathfrak{Z}(W_{j})
inductively for
j=r,r-1,\ldots,2,1
.
Note that showing
\boldsymbol{z}_{1}\in\mathfrak{Z}(W_{1})
will prove the inclusion (
3.83
) since
W_{1}=\{\alpha\}
.

For
j=r
, take some path
\pi_{W_{r},V_{r}}\in L(W_{r},V_{r})
such that
\mathsf{len}(\pi_{W_{r},V_{r}})=\ell_{r}
.
In particular, we have
\mathsf{eval}(\pi_{W_{r},V_{r}})\in\mathfrak{Z}(W_{r})
.
By Equation (
3.80
), we have

\mathsf{eval}(\pi_{W_{r},V_{r}})\in p^{\ell_{r}}\cdot\boldsymbol{b}_{W_{r},V_{r}}+\boldsymbol{c}_{W_{r},V_{r}}+(\mathbb{Z}^{N})^{\Pi(W_{r})}.

Since
\boldsymbol{h}_{r}\in(\mathbb{Z}^{N})^{\Pi(W_{r})}
, this yields

\boldsymbol{z}_{r}-\mathsf{eval}(\pi_{W_{r},V_{r}})=p^{\ell_{r}}\boldsymbol{b}_{W_{r},V_{r}}+\boldsymbol{c}_{W_{r},V_{r}}+\boldsymbol{h}_{r}-\mathsf{eval}(\pi_{W_{r},V_{r}})\in\boldsymbol{h}_{r}-(\mathbb{Z}^{N})^{\Pi(W_{r})}=(\mathbb{Z}^{N})^{\Pi(W_{r})}.

Therefore

\boldsymbol{z}_{r}\in\mathsf{eval}(\pi_{W_{r},V_{r}})+(\mathbb{Z}^{N})^{\Pi(W_{r})}\subseteq\mathfrak{Z}(W_{r})+(\mathbb{Z}^{N})^{\Pi(W_{r})}.

But by Lemma
3.36
, we have
\mathfrak{Z}(W_{r})=\mathfrak{Z}(W_{r})+(\mathbb{Z}^{N})^{\Pi(W_{r})}
, so we obtain
\boldsymbol{z}_{r}\in\mathfrak{Z}(W_{r})
.

Suppose we have proven
\boldsymbol{z}_{j+1}\in\mathfrak{Z}(W_{j+1})
, we now prove
\boldsymbol{z}_{j}\in\mathfrak{Z}(W_{j})
.
Take some path
\pi_{W_{j},V_{j}}\in L(W_{j},V_{j})
such that
\mathsf{len}(v)=\ell_{j}
.
By Equation (
3.80
), we have

\mathsf{eval}(\pi_{W_{j},V_{j}})\in p^{\ell_{j}}\cdot\boldsymbol{b}_{W_{j},V_{j}}+\boldsymbol{c}_{W_{j},V_{j}}+(\mathbb{Z}^{N})^{\Pi(W_{j})}.

(3.85)

By the induction hypothesis
\boldsymbol{z}_{j+1}\in\mathfrak{Z}(W_{j+1})
, there exists a path
\pi^{\prime}_{W_{j+1}}\in L(W_{j+1},F^{\prime})
for some accepting state
F^{\prime}
(not necessarily the same as
V_{r}
), such that
\mathsf{eval}(\pi^{\prime}_{W_{j+1}})=\boldsymbol{z}_{j+1}
.
Consider the concatenation

\pi_{W_{j},V_{j}}\cdot\delta_{V_{j},W_{j+1}}\cdot\pi^{\prime}_{W_{j+1}}\in L(W_{j},F^{\prime}),

we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{eval}\left(\pi_{W_{j},V_{j}}\cdot\delta_{V_{j},W_{j+1}}\cdot\pi^{\prime}_{W_{j+1}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\mathsf{eval}(\pi_{W_{j},V_{j}})+p^{\ell_{j}}\cdot\mathsf{eval}(\delta_{V_{j},W_{j+1}})+p^{\ell_{j}+1}\cdot\mathsf{eval}(\pi^{\prime}_{W_{j+1}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\mathsf{eval}(\pi_{W_{j},V_{j}})+p^{\ell_{j}}\cdot\boldsymbol{d}_{V_{j},W_{j+1}}+p^{\ell_{j}+1}\cdot\boldsymbol{z}_{j+1}

\displaystyle\in

\displaystyle\;p^{\ell_{j}}\cdot\boldsymbol{b}_{W_{j},V_{j}}+\boldsymbol{c}_{W_{j},V_{j}}+p^{\ell_{j}}\cdot\boldsymbol{d}_{V_{j},W_{j+1}}+p^{\ell_{j}+1}\cdot\boldsymbol{z}_{j+1}+(\mathbb{Z}^{N})^{\Pi(W_{j})}\qquad\qquad

(by (
3.85
))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\boldsymbol{z}_{j}-\boldsymbol{h}_{j}+(\mathbb{Z}^{N})^{\Pi(W_{j})}

(by (
3.84
))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\boldsymbol{z}_{j}+(\mathbb{Z}^{N})^{\Pi(W_{j})}.

Since
\pi_{W_{j},V_{j}}\cdot\delta_{V_{j},W_{j+1}}\cdot\pi^{\prime}_{W_{j+1}}\in L(W_{j},F^{\prime})
, we have
\mathsf{eval}(\pi_{W_{j},V_{j}}\cdot\delta_{V_{j},W_{j+1}}\cdot\pi^{\prime}_{W_{j+1}})\in\mathfrak{Z}(W_{j})
.
Consequently,

\boldsymbol{z}_{j}\in\mathsf{eval}(v\cdot\delta_{V_{j},W_{j+1}}\cdot w)+(\mathbb{Z}^{N})^{\Pi(W_{j})}\subseteq\mathfrak{Z}(W_{j})+(\mathbb{Z}^{N})^{\Pi(W_{j})}=\mathfrak{Z}(W_{j})

by Lemma
3.36
.
This proves the inclusion (
3.83
).
∎

Figure 7:
Illustration of proving the inclusion (
3.83
).

We have thus shown that the zero set
\mathfrak{Z}(\alpha)
can be written as a finite union of the sets

\displaystyle\Bigg\{\sum_{i=1}^{r}p^{(\ell_{1}+1)+\cdots+(\ell_{i-1}+1)}\left(p^{\ell_{i}}\boldsymbol{b}_{W_{i},V_{i}}+\boldsymbol{c}_{W_{i},V_{i}}+p^{\ell_{i}}\boldsymbol{d}_{V_{i},W_{i+1}}+\boldsymbol{h}_{i}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 227.62204pt\;\Bigg|\;\forall i,\ell_{i}\in\Lambda(W_{i},V_{i}),\boldsymbol{h}_{i}\in(\mathbb{Z}^{N})^{\Pi(W_{i})}\Bigg\}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle\Bigg\{\boldsymbol{c}_{W_{1},V_{1}}+\sum_{i=1}^{r}p^{\ell_{1}+\cdots+\ell_{i}+(i-1)}\left(\boldsymbol{b}_{W_{i},V_{i}}+p\boldsymbol{c}_{W_{i+1},V_{i+1}}+\boldsymbol{d}_{V_{i},W_{i+1}}\right)+\sum_{i=1}^{r}p^{\ell_{1}+\cdots+\ell_{i-1}+(i-1)}\boldsymbol{h}_{i}

\displaystyle\hskip 227.62204pt\;\Bigg|\;\forall i,\ell_{i}\in\Lambda(W_{i},V_{i}),\boldsymbol{h}_{i}\in(\mathbb{Z}^{N})^{\Pi(W_{i})}\Bigg\},

where
\boldsymbol{c}_{W_{r+1},V_{r+1}}
is defined as zero.
If we denote

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{a}_{i}\coloneqq\begin{cases}\boldsymbol{c}_{W_{1},V_{1}},&\quad i=0,\\
p^{i-1}\left(\boldsymbol{b}_{W_{i},V_{i}}+p\boldsymbol{c}_{W_{i+1},V_{i+1}}+\boldsymbol{d}_{V_{i},W_{i+1}}\right),&\quad i=1,\ldots,r,\\
\end{cases}

(3.86)

then
\mathfrak{Z}(\alpha)
can be written as the finite union

\bigcup_{\{\alpha\}=W_{1}\rightsquigarrow V_{1}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow W_{r}\rightsquigarrow V_{r}\in\mathcal{F}}\Bigg\{\sum_{i=0}^{r}p^{\ell_{1}+\cdots+\ell_{i}}\boldsymbol{a}_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{r}p^{\ell_{1}+\cdots+\ell_{i-1}+(i-1)}\boldsymbol{h}_{i}\\
\;\Bigg|\;\forall i,\ell_{i}\in\Lambda(W_{i},V_{i}),\boldsymbol{h}_{i}\in(\mathbb{Z}^{N})^{\Pi(W_{i})}\Bigg\}.

(3.87)

Furthermore, the denominators of each
\boldsymbol{a}_{i}
are not divisible by
p
, since this is the case for
\boldsymbol{b}_{W_{i},V_{i}}
,
\boldsymbol{c}_{W_{i+1},V_{i+1}}
and
\boldsymbol{d}_{V_{i},W_{i+1}}
.

The form of each component in the union (
3.87
) is very similar to a
p
-succinct set as defined in Equation (
1.3
).
However, there are two important differences:

(i)

The set
\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{r}p^{\ell_{1}+\cdots+\ell_{i-1}+(i-1)}\boldsymbol{h}_{i}\;\middle|\;\forall i,\ell_{i}\in\Lambda(W_{i},V_{i}),\boldsymbol{h}_{i}\in(\mathbb{Z}^{N})^{\Pi(W_{i})}\right\}
doesn’t really form a subgroup of
\mathbb{Z}^{KN}
. While for any
fixed

\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{r-1}
, the set
\big\{p^{\ell_{1}+\cdots+\ell_{i-1}+(i-1)}\boldsymbol{h}_{i}\;\big|\;\boldsymbol{h}_{i}\in(\mathbb{Z}^{N})^{\Pi(W_{i})}\big\}
forms a subgroup, it is generally not true when
\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{r-1}
are allowed to vary.

(ii)

The expression
\sum_{i=0}^{r}p^{\ell_{1}+\cdots+\ell_{i}}\boldsymbol{a}_{i},\forall i,\ell_{i}\in\Lambda(W_{i},V_{i})
is not really of the form
\boldsymbol{a}_{0}+p^{\ell k_{1}}\boldsymbol{a}_{1}+\cdots+p^{\ell k_{r}}\boldsymbol{a}_{r},\forall i,k_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
.
The main problem is that we have the extra constraint
\ell_{1}\leq\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}\leq\cdots\leq\ell_{1}+\cdots+\ell_{r}
.

The following subsection focuses on eliminating these two differences.
Difference (i) is easy to resolve using a variable elimination process that “saturates” the subgroup
p^{\ell_{1}+\cdots+\ell_{i-1}+(i-1)}\cdot(\mathbb{Z}^{N})^{\Pi(W_{i})}
.
Difference (ii) is more difficult to resolve.
To achieve this, we use a so-called “symmetrization” process similar to that of
[
Der07
, Lemma 8.1]
and
[
DM12
, Lemma 12.1]
, in order to bring down the exponents
\ell_{1}+\cdots+\ell_{i}
.

3.7 Saturation and symmetrization

In this subsection we will refine Expression (
3.87
) and finally prove
p
-normality of the zero set
\mathfrak{Z}(\alpha)
.

Saturation.

For a subgroup
H\leq\mathbb{Z}^{KN}
, denote
q\cdot H\coloneqq\{q\boldsymbol{h}\mid\boldsymbol{h}\in H\}
for any
q\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}
, and denote
\boldsymbol{a}+H\coloneqq\{\boldsymbol{a}+\boldsymbol{h}\mid\boldsymbol{h}\in H\}
for any
\boldsymbol{a}\in\mathbb{Z}^{KN}
.

Lemma 3.43

(Subgroup saturation)
.

Let
\Pi
be a partition of
\{1,\ldots,K\}
, and let
\boldsymbol{a}\in\mathbb{Z}^{KN}
.
Suppose
\boldsymbol{a}+q\cdot(\mathbb{Z}^{N})^{\Pi}\subseteq\mathfrak{Z}(\alpha)
for some
q\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}
. Then
\boldsymbol{a}+(\mathbb{Z}^{N})^{\Pi}\subseteq\mathfrak{Z}(\alpha)
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{T}
denote the set
\boldsymbol{a}+q\cdot(\mathbb{Z}^{N})^{\Pi}
.
Let
S
be any block of
\Pi
and let
b\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
, recall the definition of
\boldsymbol{e}_{S,b}\in(\mathbb{Z}^{N})^{\Pi}
in (
3.66
).
Take any
\boldsymbol{z}=(z_{11},\ldots,z_{KN})\in\mathcal{T}
, we have
\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{z}+q\boldsymbol{e}_{S,b}\in\mathfrak{Z}(\alpha)
.
The inclusion
\boldsymbol{z}\in\mathfrak{Z}(\alpha)
means

\sum_{i=1}^{K}A_{1}^{z_{i1}}\cdots A_{N}^{z_{iN}}v_{i}=0,

and the inclusion
\boldsymbol{z}+q\boldsymbol{e}_{S,b}\in\mathfrak{Z}(\alpha)
means

\sum_{i\notin S}A_{1}^{z_{i1}}\cdots A_{b}^{z_{ib}}\cdots A_{N}^{z_{iN}}v_{i}+\sum_{i\in S}A_{1}^{z_{i1}}\cdots A_{b}^{z_{ib}+q}\cdots A_{N}^{z_{iN}}v_{i}=0.

Therefore we have the system of equations

\displaystyle\sum_{i\notin S}A_{1}^{z_{i1}}\cdots A_{N}^{z_{iN}}v_{i}+\sum_{i\in S}A_{1}^{z_{i1}}\cdots A_{N}^{z_{iN}}v_{i}=0,

\displaystyle\sum_{i\notin S}A_{1}^{z_{i1}}\cdots A_{N}^{z_{iN}}v_{i}+A_{b}^{q}\cdot\sum_{i\in S}A_{1}^{z_{i1}}\cdots A_{N}^{z_{iN}}v_{i}=0.

By Lemma
3.34
, we have
A_{b}^{q}-1\not\in\mathfrak{m}
, so
A_{b}^{q}-1\in\mathcal{A}
is invertible.
Therefore the transformation matrix

\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
1&A_{b}^{q}\\
\end{pmatrix}

is invertible.
This yields

\sum_{i\notin S}A_{1}^{z_{i1}}\cdots A_{N}^{z_{iN}}v_{i}=\sum_{i\in S}A_{1}^{z_{i1}}\cdots A_{n}^{z_{in}}v_{i}=0.

Consequently,

\sum_{i\notin S}A_{1}^{z_{i1}}\cdots A_{N}^{z_{iN}}v_{i}+A_{b}\cdot\sum_{i\in S}A_{1}^{z_{i1}}\cdots A_{N}^{z_{iN}}v_{i}=0.

This yields
\boldsymbol{z}+\boldsymbol{e}_{S,b}\in\mathfrak{Z}(\alpha)
.
Since this holds for all
\boldsymbol{z}\in\mathcal{T}
, we have
\mathcal{T}+\mathbb{Z}\boldsymbol{e}_{S,b}\subseteq\mathfrak{Z}(\alpha)
.

We now set
\mathcal{T}
as the new set
\mathcal{T}+\mathbb{Z}\boldsymbol{e}_{S,b}
.
Repeat the above process by taking any other block
S^{\prime}
of
\Pi
and any
b^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
.
The process yields
\mathcal{T}+\mathbb{Z}\boldsymbol{e}_{S,b}+\mathbb{Z}\boldsymbol{e}_{S^{\prime},b^{\prime}}\subseteq\mathfrak{Z}(\alpha)
.
Iterate this for all blocks of
\Pi
and all elements of
\{1,\ldots,N\}
, we obtain

\mathcal{T}+(\mathbb{Z}^{N})^{\Pi}=\mathcal{T}+\sum_{S\in\Pi,b\in\{1,\ldots,N\}}\mathbb{Z}\boldsymbol{e}_{S,b}\subseteq\mathfrak{Z}(\alpha).

Since
\mathcal{T}=\boldsymbol{a}+q\cdot(\mathbb{Z}^{N})^{\Pi}
, this yields
\boldsymbol{a}+(\mathbb{Z}^{N})^{\Pi}\subseteq\mathfrak{Z}(\alpha)
.
∎

Recall from Expression (
3.87
) that
\mathfrak{Z}(\alpha)
can be written as the finite union

\bigcup_{\{\alpha\}=W_{1}\rightsquigarrow V_{1}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow W_{r}\rightsquigarrow V_{r}\in\mathcal{F}}\Bigg\{\sum_{i=0}^{r}p^{\ell_{1}+\cdots+\ell_{i}}\boldsymbol{a}_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{r}p^{\ell_{1}+\cdots+\ell_{i-1}+(i-1)}\boldsymbol{h}_{i}\\
\;\Bigg|\;\forall i,\ell_{i}\in\Lambda(W_{i},V_{i}),\boldsymbol{h}_{i}\in(\mathbb{Z}^{N})^{\Pi(W_{i})}\Bigg\}.

We can apply Lemma
3.43
with

\boldsymbol{a}=\sum_{i=0}^{r}p^{\ell_{1}+\cdots+\ell_{i}}\boldsymbol{a}_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{r}p^{\ell_{1}+\cdots+\ell_{i-1}+(i-1)}\boldsymbol{h}_{i},

and with

q=p^{\ell_{1}+\cdots+\ell_{i-1}+(i-1)},

for each
i=1,\ldots,r
.
This yields

\mathfrak{Z}(\alpha)=\bigcup_{\{\alpha\}=W_{1}\rightsquigarrow V_{1}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow W_{r}\rightsquigarrow V_{r}\in\mathcal{F}}\Bigg\{\sum_{i=0}^{r}p^{\ell_{1}+\cdots+\ell_{i}}\boldsymbol{a}_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{r}\boldsymbol{h}_{i}\\
\;\Bigg|\;\forall i,\ell_{i}\in\Lambda(W_{i},V_{i}),\boldsymbol{h}_{i}\in(\mathbb{Z}^{N})^{\Pi(W_{i})}\Bigg\}.

Since

\sum_{i=1}^{r}(\mathbb{Z}^{N})^{\Pi(W_{i})}\coloneqq\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{r}\boldsymbol{h}_{i}\;\middle|\;\forall i,\boldsymbol{h}_{i}\in(\mathbb{Z}^{N})^{\Pi(W_{i})}\right\}

is a subgroup of
\mathbb{Z}^{KN}
, the above discussion can be summarized as the following corollary:

Corollary 3.44
.

The zero set
\mathfrak{Z}(\alpha)
can be written as the finite union
