\bigcup_{\{\alpha\}=W_{1}\rightsquigarrow V_{1}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow W_{r}\rightsquigarrow V_{r}\in\mathcal{F}}\Bigg\{\sum_{i=0}^{r}p^{\ell_{1}+\cdots+\ell_{i}}\boldsymbol{a}_{i}+\boldsymbol{h}\\
\;\Bigg|\;\forall i,\ell_{i}\in\Lambda(W_{i},V_{i}),\;\boldsymbol{h}\in\sum_{i=1}^{r}(\mathbb{Z}^{N})^{\Pi(W_{i})}\Bigg\},

(3.88)

where
\boldsymbol{a}_{0},\boldsymbol{a}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{a}_{r}\in\mathbb{Q}^{KN}
are defined in (
3.86
). Their denominators are not divisible by
p
.

This resolves the difference (i) in the discussion at the end of the last subsection.
We now start to resolve the difference (ii).

Symmetrization I: arithmetic progressions.

Recall from Lemma
3.41
that for each
i=1,\ldots,r
, the set
\Lambda(W_{i},V_{i})
can be written as a union of a finite set
Q_{i}
and finitely many arithmetic progressions
\{s_{ij}+n\cdot\lambda_{ij}\mid n\in\mathbb{N}\},j=1,2,\ldots
.
Let
\ell
denote the least common multiplier of all
\lambda_{ij}
.
Then each set
\Lambda(W_{i},V_{i})
can be written as a union

\Lambda(W_{i},V_{i})=Q_{i}\cup\bigcup_{s=1}^{u_{i}}\{\sigma_{is}+\ell k\mid k\in\mathbb{N}\}

for some
\sigma_{is}\in\mathbb{N},i=1,\ldots,r;\,s=1,\ldots,u_{i}
.
In other words, we can suppose without loss of generality that the common difference in all the arithmetic progressions is equal to
\ell
.
Thus, let
\ell_{i}
be any element in
\Lambda(W_{i},V_{i})
, then
\ell_{i}
can either be written as
\sigma_{is}+\ell k_{i}
for some
k_{i}\in\mathbb{N},s\in\{1,\ldots,u_{i}\}
, or it is equal to some
q_{i}\in Q_{i}
.

Hence, the set
\Big\{\sum_{i=1}^{r}p^{\ell_{1}+\cdots+\ell_{i}}\left(\boldsymbol{a}_{i}+p^{i}\boldsymbol{h}_{i}\right)\;\Big|\;\forall i,\ell_{i}\in\Lambda(W_{i},V_{i}),\boldsymbol{h}_{i}\in(\mathbb{Z}^{N})^{\Pi(W_{i})}\Big\}

can be written as a finite union

\bigcup_{\underset{q_{1}\in Q_{1},\ldots,q_{r}\in Q_{r}}{\underset{1\leq s_{1}\leq u_{i_{1}},\ldots,1\leq s_{r^{\prime}}\leq u_{i_{r^{\prime}}}}{1\leq i_{1}<i_{2}<\cdots<i_{r^{\prime}}\leq r}}}\Bigg\{\sum_{i=0}^{r}p^{q_{1}+\cdots+q_{i_{1}-1}+(\sigma_{i_{1}s_{1}}+\ell k_{i_{1}})+q_{i_{1}+1}+\cdots+q_{i_{2}-1}+(\sigma_{i_{2}s_{2}}+\ell k_{i_{2}})+q_{i_{2}+1}+\cdots+q_{i}}\boldsymbol{a}_{i}+\boldsymbol{h}\\
\;\Bigg|\;k_{i_{1}},k_{i_{2}},\ldots,k_{i_{r^{\prime}}}\in\mathbb{N},\;\boldsymbol{h}\in\sum_{i=1}^{r}(\mathbb{Z}^{N})^{\Pi(W_{i})}\Bigg\}.

(3.89)

That is, let
\{i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{r^{\prime}}\}
be the set of all indices
i
such that the value of
\ell_{i}
falls in an arithmetic progression
\{\sigma_{is}+\ell k\mid k\in\mathbb{N}\}
; for each of these indices we choose
s\in\{1,\ldots,u_{i}\}
to determine the arithmetic progression, and write
\ell_{i}=\sigma_{is}+\ell k_{i},\;k_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
.
For all the other indices
i\notin\{i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{r^{\prime}}\}
, the value of
\ell_{i}
falls in the finite set
Q_{i}
, and we choose
q_{i}\in Q
so that
\ell_{i}=q_{i}
.
These choices give the decomposition (
3.89
).

By regrouping the terms, each component of the union (
3.89
) can then be rewritten as

\Bigg\{\sum_{j=1}^{r^{\prime}}p^{\ell(k_{i_{i}}+k_{i_{2}}+\cdots+k_{i_{j}})}\cdot\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}_{j}+\boldsymbol{h}\;\Bigg|\;k_{i_{1}},k_{i_{2}},\ldots,k_{i_{r^{\prime}}}\in\mathbb{N},\;\boldsymbol{h}\in\sum_{i=1}^{r}(\mathbb{Z}^{N})^{\Pi(W_{i})}\Bigg\},

(3.90)

for some

\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}_{j}\coloneqq p^{q_{1}+\cdots+q_{i_{1}-1}+\sigma_{i_{1}s_{1}}+q_{i_{1}+1}+\cdots+\sigma_{i_{j}s_{j}}}\cdot\boldsymbol{a}_{i_{j}}+p^{q_{1}+\cdots+q_{i_{1}-1}+\sigma_{i_{1}s_{1}}+q_{i_{1}+1}+\cdots+\sigma_{i_{j}s_{j}}+q_{i_{j}+1}}\cdot\boldsymbol{a}_{i_{j}+1}+\cdots\\
+p^{q_{1}+\cdots+q_{i_{1}-1}+\sigma_{i_{1}s_{1}}+q_{i_{1}+1}+\cdots+\sigma_{i_{j}s_{j}}+q_{i_{j}+1}+\cdots+q_{i_{j+1}-1}}\cdot\boldsymbol{a}_{i_{j+1}-1}\in\mathbb{Q}^{KN}.

Writing
r^{\prime}
as
r
,
k_{i_{j}}
as
k_{j}
,
\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{\prime}
as
\boldsymbol{a}_{j}
, and denoting
H=\sum_{i=1}^{r}(\mathbb{Z}^{N})^{\Pi(W_{i})}
, we can summarize the above discussion by the following corollary.

Corollary 3.45
.

The zero set
\mathfrak{Z}(\alpha)
can be written as a finite union of sets of the form

\Bigg\{\sum_{j=1}^{r}p^{\ell(k_{1}+k_{2}+\cdots+k_{j})}\cdot\boldsymbol{a}_{j}+\boldsymbol{h}\;\Bigg|\;k_{1},\ldots,k_{r}\in\mathbb{N},\;\boldsymbol{h}\in H\Bigg\},

(3.91)

where
H
is a subgroup of
\mathbb{Z}^{KN}
, and the denominators of each
\boldsymbol{a}_{j}
are not divisible by
p
.

Symmetrization II: decreasing exponents.

Recall that
\mathcal{A}
is a local
\mathbb{Z}_{/p^{e}}(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muX\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu)
-algebra with the maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}\ni p
, such that
\mathfrak{m}^{t}=0
for some
t\in\mathbb{N}
.
We prove the following generalization of
[
Der07
, Lemma 8.1]
and
[
DM12
, Lemma 12.1]
, which will serve to “decrease” the exponents
k_{1}+k_{2}+\cdots+k_{j}
in the expression (
3.91
).

Proposition 3.46

(Symmetrization of exponents)
.

Let
\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathbb{Q}^{KN}
be such that the denominators of
\boldsymbol{a}
are not divisible by
p
.
Suppose there exists
m\in\mathbb{N}
such that
p^{\ell n}\cdot\boldsymbol{a}+\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathfrak{Z}(\alpha)
holds for all
n\geq m
. Then
p^{\ell n}\cdot\boldsymbol{a}+\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathfrak{Z}(\alpha)
holds for all
n\geq t^{2}+t
.

First we characterize the denominators of
\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b}
:

Lemma 3.47

(
[
DM15
, p.117]
)
.

Let
\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathbb{Q}^{KN}
be such that the denominators of
\boldsymbol{a}
are not divisible by
p
.
Suppose
p^{\ell n}\cdot\boldsymbol{a}+\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathfrak{Z}(\alpha)
holds for all
n\geq m
for some
m\in\mathbb{N}
, then
(p^{\ell}-1)\boldsymbol{a}\in\mathbb{Z}^{KN}
,
\boldsymbol{a}+\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathbb{Z}^{KN}
,
(p^{\ell}-1)\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathbb{Z}^{KN}
.

Proof.

Since
p^{\ell m}\cdot\boldsymbol{a}+\boldsymbol{b}
and
p^{\ell(m+1)}\cdot\boldsymbol{a}+\boldsymbol{b}
are in
\mathbb{Z}^{KN}
, their difference is also in
\mathbb{Z}^{KN}
:

(p^{\ell(m+1)}\cdot\boldsymbol{a}+\boldsymbol{b})-(p^{\ell m}\cdot\boldsymbol{a}+\boldsymbol{b})=p^{\ell m}(p^{\ell}-1)\cdot\boldsymbol{a}\in\mathbb{Z}^{KN}.

Since
p
does not divide the denominators of
\boldsymbol{a}
, this yields
(p^{\ell}-1)\cdot\boldsymbol{a}\in\mathbb{Z}^{KN}
.
Since
p^{\ell}-1\mid p^{\ell m}-1
, we have
(p^{\ell m}-1)\cdot\boldsymbol{a}\in\mathbb{Z}^{KN}
.
Subtract this from
p^{\ell m}\cdot\boldsymbol{a}+\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathbb{Z}^{KN}
, we obtain
\boldsymbol{a}+\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathbb{Z}^{KN}
.
Finally, since
(p^{\ell}-1)\cdot\boldsymbol{a}\in\mathbb{Z}^{KN}
and
(p^{\ell}-1)\cdot(\boldsymbol{a}+\boldsymbol{b})\in\mathbb{Z}^{KN}
, we have
(p^{\ell}-1)\cdot\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathbb{Z}^{KN}
.
∎

Since
p\in\mathfrak{m}
, the quotient
\mathbb{A}\coloneqq\mathcal{A}/\mathfrak{m}
is a field of characteristic
p
.
Let
\overline{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})
be a tuple of elements in
\mathbb{A}
.
For any vector
\boldsymbol{z}=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{N})\in\mathbb{Z}^{N}
, denote by
\overline{x}^{\boldsymbol{z}}
the product
x_{1}^{z_{1}}x_{2}^{z_{2}}\cdots x_{N}^{z_{N}}
.
Write
\boldsymbol{a}=(\boldsymbol{a}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{a}_{K})
and
\boldsymbol{b}=(\boldsymbol{b}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{b}_{K})
with
\boldsymbol{a}_{i},\boldsymbol{b}_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}^{N}
.

For any
r\in\mathbb{N}
and
i=1,\ldots,K
, denote the sequence

\boldsymbol{x}_{i}^{(\geq r)}\coloneqq(\overline{x}^{p^{\ell r}\boldsymbol{a}_{i}+\boldsymbol{b}_{i}},\overline{x}^{p^{\ell(r+1)}\boldsymbol{a}_{i}+\boldsymbol{b}_{i}},\overline{x}^{p^{\ell(r+2)}\boldsymbol{a}_{i}+\boldsymbol{b}_{i}},\ldots)\in\mathbb{A}^{\mathbb{N}}.

(3.92)

Note that
\boldsymbol{x}_{i}^{(\geq r+1)}
is a subsequence of
\boldsymbol{x}_{i}^{(\geq r)}
.
So if the sequences
\boldsymbol{x}_{i_{1}}^{(\geq r)},\boldsymbol{x}_{i_{2}}^{(\geq r)},\ldots,\boldsymbol{x}_{i_{s}}^{(\geq r)}
are
\mathbb{A}
-linearly dependent for some
i_{1},\ldots,i_{s}\in\{1,\ldots,K\}
, then the sequences
\boldsymbol{x}_{i_{1}}^{(\geq r+1)},\boldsymbol{x}_{i_{2}}^{(\geq r+1)},\ldots,\boldsymbol{x}_{i_{s}}^{(\geq r+1)}
are also
\mathbb{A}
-linearly dependent.
Therefore, let
\mathcal{I}_{r}\subseteq\{1,\ldots,K\}
be a
maximal
subset such that
\boldsymbol{x}_{i}^{(\geq r)},i\in\mathcal{I}
are
\mathbb{A}
-linearly independent (that is, each
\boldsymbol{x}_{j}^{(\geq r)},j\notin\mathcal{I}
can be written as an
\mathbb{A}
-linear combination of
\boldsymbol{x}_{i}^{(\geq r)},i\in\mathcal{I}
), then there exists
\mathcal{I}_{r+1}\subseteq\mathcal{I}_{r}
such that
\mathcal{I}_{r+1}
is maximal subset such that
\boldsymbol{x}_{i}^{(\geq r+1)},i\in\mathcal{I}
are
\mathbb{A}
-linearly independent.
The chain
\mathcal{I}_{r}\supseteq\mathcal{I}_{r+1}\supseteq\mathcal{I}_{r+2}\supseteq\cdots
must stabilize to some
\mathcal{I}
.
Then
\mathcal{I}
is a maximal subset such that
\boldsymbol{x}_{i}^{(\geq r)},i\in\mathcal{I}
are
\mathbb{A}
-linearly independent
for all
r\in\mathbb{N}
.

Lemma 3.48
.

Let
\mathbb{A}
be a field of characteristic
p
.
Let
\overline{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})
be a tuple of non-zero elements in
\mathbb{A}
.
Pick a maximal subset
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\{1,\ldots,K\}
such that
\boldsymbol{x}_{i}^{(\geq r)},i\in\mathcal{I}
are
\mathbb{A}
-linearly independent for all
r
.
Then for any
j\notin\mathcal{I}
, we have

\overline{x}^{\boldsymbol{a}_{j}+\boldsymbol{b}_{j}}=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}c_{j,i}\cdot\overline{x}^{\boldsymbol{a}_{i}+\boldsymbol{b}_{i}},

(3.93)

for some
c_{j,i}\in\mathbb{A},i\in\mathcal{I}
satisfying

c_{j,i}^{p^{\ell}}\cdot\overline{x}^{(p^{\ell}-1)\boldsymbol{b}_{i}}=c_{j,i}\cdot\overline{x}^{(p^{\ell}-1)\boldsymbol{b}_{j}}.

(3.94)

Proof.

Take any
j\notin\mathcal{I}
. For brevity we will omit the subscript
j
from the elements
c_{j,i}
and write them as
c_{i}
.
By the maximality of
\mathcal{I}
, there exists
c_{i}\in\mathbb{A}
for each
i\in\mathcal{I}
, such that

\overline{x}^{p^{\ell r}\boldsymbol{a}_{j}+\boldsymbol{b}_{j}}=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}c_{i}\cdot\overline{x}^{p^{\ell r}\boldsymbol{a}_{i}+\boldsymbol{b}_{i}}

(3.95)

for all large enough
r
.
Since
\mathbb{A}
is of characteristic
p
, taking
p^{\ell}
-th power on both sides yields

\overline{x}^{p^{\ell(r+1)}\boldsymbol{a}_{j}+p^{\ell}\boldsymbol{b}_{j}}=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}c_{i}^{p^{\ell}}\cdot\overline{x}^{p^{\ell(r+1)}\boldsymbol{a}_{i}+p^{\ell}\boldsymbol{b}_{i}}.

(3.96)

Since Equation (
3.95
) also holds for
r+1
, we have
\overline{x}^{p^{\ell(r+1)}\boldsymbol{a}_{j}+\boldsymbol{b}_{j}}=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}c_{i}\cdot\overline{x}^{p^{\ell(r+1)}\boldsymbol{a}_{i}+\boldsymbol{b}_{i}}
.
Multiplying both sides of
\overline{x}^{p^{\ell(r+1)}\boldsymbol{a}_{j}+\boldsymbol{b}_{j}}=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}c_{i}\cdot\overline{x}^{p^{\ell(r+1)}\boldsymbol{a}_{i}+\boldsymbol{b}_{i}}
by
\overline{x}^{(p^{\ell}-1)\boldsymbol{b}_{j}}
(note that
(p^{\ell}-1)\boldsymbol{b}_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}^{N}
by Lemma
3.47
), we have

\overline{x}^{p^{\ell(r+1)}\boldsymbol{a}_{j}+p^{\ell}\boldsymbol{b}_{j}}=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}c_{i}\cdot\overline{x}^{p^{\ell(r+1)}\boldsymbol{a}_{i}+\boldsymbol{b}_{i}}\cdot\overline{x}^{(p^{\ell}-1)\boldsymbol{b}_{j}}.

Subtract this from Equation (
3.96
), we obtain

0=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\left(c_{i}^{p^{\ell}}\cdot\overline{x}^{(p^{\ell}-1)\boldsymbol{b}_{i}}-c_{i}\cdot\overline{x}^{(p^{\ell}-1)\boldsymbol{b}_{j}}\right)\cdot\overline{x}^{p^{\ell(r+1)}\boldsymbol{a}_{i}+\boldsymbol{b}_{i}}

Since this holds also for
r+1,r+2,\ldots
, we have

0=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\left(c_{i}^{p^{\ell}}\cdot\overline{x}^{(p^{\ell}-1)\boldsymbol{b}_{i}}-c_{i}\cdot\overline{x}^{(p^{\ell}-1)\boldsymbol{b}_{j}}\right)\cdot\boldsymbol{x}_{i}^{(\geq r+1)}.

But
\boldsymbol{x}_{i}^{(\geq r+1)},i\in\mathcal{I}
are
\mathbb{A}
-linearly independent, so we must have

c_{i}^{p^{\ell}}\cdot\overline{x}^{(p^{\ell}-1)\boldsymbol{b}_{i}}-c_{i}\cdot\overline{x}^{(p^{\ell}-1)\boldsymbol{b}_{j}}=0

for all
i\in\mathcal{I}
. This shows Equation (
3.94
).

Next we show Equation (
3.93
).
From Equation (
3.94
) we have

c_{i}=c_{i}^{p^{\ell}}\cdot\overline{x}^{(p^{\ell}-1)\boldsymbol{b}_{i}-(p^{\ell}-1)\boldsymbol{b}_{j}}.

Substituting this for
c_{i}
in
\overline{x}^{p^{\ell r}\boldsymbol{a}_{j}+\boldsymbol{b}_{j}}=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}c_{i}\cdot\overline{x}^{p^{\ell r}\boldsymbol{a}_{i}+\boldsymbol{b}_{i}}
yields

\overline{x}^{p^{\ell r}\boldsymbol{a}_{j}+\boldsymbol{b}_{j}}=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}c_{i}^{p^{\ell}}\cdot\overline{x}^{(p^{\ell}-1)\boldsymbol{b}_{i}-(p^{\ell}-1)\boldsymbol{b}_{j}}\cdot\overline{x}^{p^{\ell r}\boldsymbol{a}_{i}+\boldsymbol{b}_{i}},

so

\overline{x}^{p^{\ell r}\boldsymbol{a}_{j}+p^{\ell}\boldsymbol{b}_{j}}=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}c_{i}^{p^{\ell}}\cdot\overline{x}^{p^{\ell r}\boldsymbol{a}_{i}+p^{\ell}\boldsymbol{b}_{i}}.

Since
\mathbb{A}
has characteristic
p
, this can be rewritten as

\left(\overline{x}^{p^{\ell(r-1)}\boldsymbol{a}_{j}+\boldsymbol{b}_{j}}\right)^{p^{\ell}}=\left(\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}c_{i}\cdot\overline{x}^{p^{\ell(r-1)}\boldsymbol{a}_{i}+\boldsymbol{b}_{i}}\right)^{p^{\ell}}.

Since
y^{p^{\ell}}=z^{p^{\ell}}\implies(y-z)^{p^{\ell}}=y^{p^{\ell}}-z^{p^{\ell}}=0\implies y-z=0
, the equation above yields

\overline{x}^{p^{\ell(r-1)}\boldsymbol{a}_{j}+\boldsymbol{b}_{j}}=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}c_{i}\cdot\overline{x}^{p^{\ell(r-1)}\boldsymbol{a}_{i}+\boldsymbol{b}_{i}}.

This means that Equation (
3.95
) still holds if we replace
r
by
r-1
.
Repeat this iteratively for
r-1,r-2,\ldots,2,1
, we obtain

\overline{x}^{\boldsymbol{a}_{j}+\boldsymbol{b}_{j}}=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}c_{i}\cdot\overline{x}^{\boldsymbol{a}_{i}+\boldsymbol{b}_{i}},

which concludes the proof for Equation (
3.93
).
∎

The following lemma can be considered as an extension of Lemma
3.13
.

Lemma 3.49
.

Let
f\in\mathbb{Z}[\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muX\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{\pm}]
, then for all
n\geq t
, we have

f^{p^{\ell n}}(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{k})\equiv f^{p^{\ell t}}(Y_{1}^{p^{\ell(n-t)}},\ldots,Y_{k}^{p^{\ell(n-t)}})\mod p^{t}.

Proof.

We have
f^{p}(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{k})\equiv f(Y_{1}^{p},\ldots,Y_{k}^{p})\mod p
.
Apply Lemma
3.14
to the ring
\mathbb{Z}_{/p^{t}}[\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muX\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{\pm}]
and to the ideal generated by
p
, we obtain

f^{p^{r}}(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{k})\equiv f^{p^{r-1}}(Y_{1}^{p},\ldots,Y_{k}^{p})\mod p^{t}.

(3.97)

for all
r\geq t-1
.
We can apply (
3.97
) for
r=\ell n,\ell n-1,\ldots,\ell t+1
, and obtain

f^{p^{\ell n}}(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{k})\equiv f^{p^{\ell n-1}}(Y_{1}^{p},\ldots,Y_{k}^{p})\equiv f^{p^{\ell n-2}}(Y_{1}^{p^{2}},\ldots,Y_{k}^{p^{2}})\equiv\cdots\equiv f^{p^{\ell t}}(Y_{1}^{p^{\ell(n-t)}},\ldots,Y_{k}^{p^{\ell(n-t)}})\\
\mod p^{t}.

∎

Recall that
\alpha(\boldsymbol{z}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{z}_{K})=\sum_{i=1}^{K}\overline{A}^{\boldsymbol{z}_{i}}v_{i}
, where
\overline{A}=(A_{1},\ldots,A_{N})
is a tuple of elements in the
\mathbb{Z}_{/p^{e}}(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muX\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu)
-algebra
\mathcal{A}
, and
v_{1},\ldots,v_{K}
are elements in the
\mathcal{A}
-module
\mathcal{V}
, with
\mathfrak{m}^{t}\mathcal{V}=0
.
We prove the following slight generalization of Proposition
3.46
.
In particular, if we take
s=0
, then we immediately obtain Proposition
3.46
.
The reason we introduce the additional variable
s
is to perform induction.

Lemma 3.50
.

Let
s\leq t
be an integer, and let
v_{1},\ldots,v_{K}\in\mathfrak{m}^{s}\mathcal{V}
.
Suppose there exists
m\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\sum_{i=1}^{K}\overline{A}^{p^{\ell n}\boldsymbol{a}_{i}+\boldsymbol{b}_{i}}v_{i}=0
holds for all
n\geq m
. Then
\sum_{i=1}^{K}\overline{A}^{p^{\ell n}\boldsymbol{a}_{i}+\boldsymbol{b}_{i}}v_{i}=0
holds for all
n\geq(t+1-s)t
.

Proof.

We use reverse induction on
s
, starting from
t
and gradually decreasing to
0
.
The case where
s=t
is trivial because
\mathfrak{m}^{t}\mathcal{V}=0
. We now focus on the induction step.
Suppose the statement is true for
s
, we show it for
s-1
.
Let
v_{1},\ldots,v_{K}\in\mathfrak{m}^{s-1}\mathcal{V}
.

If
v_{i}\in\mathfrak{m}^{s}\mathcal{V}
for all
i=1,\ldots,K
, then we conclude directly using the induction hypothesis for
s
.
Suppose now that
v_{i}\notin\mathfrak{m}^{s}\mathcal{V}
for some
i
.
Without loss of generality, we can suppose
v_{1}\in\mathfrak{m}^{s-1}\mathcal{V}\setminus\mathfrak{m}^{s}\mathcal{V},\ldots,v_{K^{\prime}}\notin\mathfrak{m}^{s-1}\mathcal{V}\setminus\mathfrak{m}^{s}\mathcal{V}
, and
v_{K^{\prime}+1}\in\mathfrak{m}^{s}\mathcal{V},\ldots,v_{K}\in\mathfrak{m}^{s}\mathcal{V}
, where
1\leq K^{\prime}\leq K
.

Let
\mathbb{A}\coloneqq\mathcal{A}/\mathfrak{m}
, it is a field of characteristic
p
.
Let

x_{1}\coloneqq A_{1}+\mathfrak{m},x_{2}\coloneqq A_{2}+\mathfrak{m},\ldots,x_{N}\coloneqq A_{N}+\mathfrak{m},

be elements of
\mathbb{A}
. These are non-zero since
A_{1},\ldots,A_{N}
are invertible in
\mathcal{A}
. Denote
\overline{x}\coloneqq(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})
.

As in Equation (
3.92
), for each
i=1,\ldots,K
, and
r\in\mathbb{N}
, denote the sequence

\boldsymbol{x}_{i}^{(\geq r)}\coloneqq(\overline{x}^{p^{\ell r}\boldsymbol{a}_{i}+\boldsymbol{b}_{i}},\overline{x}^{p^{\ell(r+1)}\boldsymbol{a}_{i}+\boldsymbol{b}_{i}},\overline{x}^{p^{\ell(r+2)}\boldsymbol{a}_{i}+\boldsymbol{b}_{i}},\ldots)\in\mathbb{A}^{\mathbb{N}}.

Let
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\{1,\ldots,K^{\prime}\}
be a maximal subset such that
\boldsymbol{x}_{i}^{(\geq r)},i\in\mathcal{I}
are
\mathbb{A}
-linearly independent for all
r\in\mathbb{N}
.
Without loss of generality suppose
\mathcal{I}=\{1,\ldots,k\}
for some
k\leq K^{\prime}
.
By Lemma
3.48
, we can write

\overline{x}^{\boldsymbol{a}_{k+1}+\boldsymbol{b}_{k+1}}=c_{k+1,1}\cdot\overline{x}^{\boldsymbol{a}_{1}+\boldsymbol{b}_{1}}+\cdots+c_{k+1,k}\cdot\overline{x}^{\boldsymbol{a}_{k}+\boldsymbol{b}_{k}},\\
\ddots\\
\overline{x}^{\boldsymbol{a}_{K^{\prime}}+\boldsymbol{b}_{K^{\prime}}}=c_{K^{\prime},1}\cdot\overline{x}^{\boldsymbol{a}_{1}+\boldsymbol{b}_{1}}+\cdots+c_{K^{\prime},k}\cdot\overline{x}^{\boldsymbol{a}_{k}+\boldsymbol{b}_{k}},

for some
c_{k+1,1},\ldots,c_{K^{\prime},k}\in\mathbb{A}
that satisfy

c_{j,i}^{p^{\ell}}\cdot\overline{x}^{(p^{\ell}-1)\boldsymbol{b}_{i}}=c_{j,i}\cdot\overline{x}^{(p^{\ell}-1)\boldsymbol{b}_{j}}.

(3.98)

For
j=k+1,\ldots,K^{\prime};i=1,\ldots,k
, take any
\widetilde{c}_{j,i}\in\mathcal{A}
such that
\widetilde{c}_{j,i}=c_{j,i}+\mathfrak{m}
.
This yields

\overline{A}^{\boldsymbol{a}_{k+1}+\boldsymbol{b}_{k+1}}\equiv\widetilde{c}_{k+1,1}\overline{A}^{\boldsymbol{a}_{1}+\boldsymbol{b}_{1}}+\cdots+\widetilde{c}_{k+1,k}\overline{A}^{\boldsymbol{a}_{k}+\boldsymbol{b}_{k}}\mod\mathfrak{m},\\
\;\ddots\;\\
\overline{x}^{\boldsymbol{a}_{K^{\prime}}+\boldsymbol{b}_{K^{\prime}}}\equiv\widetilde{c}_{K^{\prime},1}\overline{A}^{\boldsymbol{a}_{1}+\boldsymbol{b}_{1}}+\cdots+\widetilde{c}_{K^{\prime},k}\overline{A}^{\boldsymbol{a}_{k}+\boldsymbol{b}_{k}}\mod\mathfrak{m}.

Applying Lemma
3.14
to the ring
\mathcal{A}
and the ideal
\mathfrak{m}
, we have

\overline{A}^{p^{\ell n}(\boldsymbol{a}_{k+1}+\boldsymbol{b}_{k+1})}=\left(\widetilde{c}_{k+1,1}\overline{A}^{\boldsymbol{a}_{1}+\boldsymbol{b}_{1}}+\cdots+\widetilde{c}_{k+1,k}\overline{A}^{\boldsymbol{a}_{k}+\boldsymbol{b}_{k}}\right)^{p^{\ell n}},\\
\;\ddots\;\\
\overline{A}^{p^{\ell n}(\boldsymbol{a}_{K^{\prime}}+\boldsymbol{b}_{K^{\prime}})}=\left(\widetilde{c}_{K^{\prime},1}\overline{A}^{\boldsymbol{a}_{1}+\boldsymbol{b}_{1}}+\cdots+\widetilde{c}_{K^{\prime},k}\overline{A}^{\boldsymbol{a}_{k}+\boldsymbol{b}_{k}}\right)^{p^{\ell n}},

for all
\ell n\geq t-1
.
Then for all
n\geq t-1
, the equation
\sum_{i=1}^{K}\overline{A}^{p^{\ell n}\boldsymbol{a}_{i}+\boldsymbol{b}_{i}}v_{i}=0
is equivalent to

\sum_{i=1}^{k}\overline{A}^{p^{\ell n}\boldsymbol{a}_{i}+\boldsymbol{b}_{i}}v_{i}+\sum_{j=k+1}^{K^{\prime}}\left(\widetilde{c}_{j,1}\overline{A}^{\boldsymbol{a}_{1}+\boldsymbol{b}_{1}}+\cdots+\widetilde{c}_{j,k}\overline{A}^{\boldsymbol{a}_{k}+\boldsymbol{b}_{k}}\right)^{p^{\ell n}}\overline{A}^{(1-p^{\ell n})\boldsymbol{b}_{j}}v_{j}+\sum_{i=K^{\prime}+1}^{K}\overline{A}^{p^{\ell n}\boldsymbol{a}_{i}+\boldsymbol{b}_{i}}v_{i}=0.

(3.99)

For
j=k+1,\ldots,K^{\prime}
, consider the polynomial
f(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{k})\coloneqq Y_{1}+\cdots+Y_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}[Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{k}]
.
By Lemma
3.49
we have

f^{p^{\ell n}}(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{k})\equiv f^{p^{\ell t}}(Y_{1}^{p^{\ell(n-t)}},\ldots,Y_{k}^{p^{\ell(n-t)}})\mod p^{t},

for all
n\geq t
.
We can write the polynomial
f^{p^{\ell t}}(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{k})=(Y_{1}+\cdots+Y_{k})^{p^{\ell t}}
in the form

Y_{1}^{p^{\ell t}}+\cdots+Y_{k}^{p^{\ell t}}+p\sum_{i\in\mathcal{J}}u_{i}Y_{1}^{d_{i1}}Y_{2}^{d_{i2}}\cdots Y_{k}^{d_{ik}}

for some finite index set
\mathcal{J}
, and with
u_{i}\in\mathbb{Z},d_{i1}+\cdots+d_{ik}=p^{\ell t},
for all
i\in\mathcal{J}
.
Then

(Y_{1}+\cdots+Y_{k})^{p^{\ell n}}\equiv Y_{1}^{p^{\ell n}}+\cdots+Y_{k}^{p^{\ell n}}+p\sum_{i\in\mathcal{J}}u_{i}Y_{1}^{p^{\ell(n-t)}d_{i1}}Y_{2}^{p^{\ell(n-t)}d_{i2}}\cdots Y_{k}^{p^{\ell(n-t)}d_{ik}}\mod p^{t}.

(3.100)

For each
j=k+1,\ldots,K^{\prime}
, consider the ring homomorphism from
\mathbb{Z}[Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{k}]
to
\mathcal{A}
, that sends
Y_{1},Y_{2},\ldots,Y_{k}
respectively to
\widetilde{c}_{j,1}\overline{A}^{\boldsymbol{a}_{1}+\boldsymbol{b}_{1}},\widetilde{c}_{j,2}\overline{A}^{\boldsymbol{a}_{2}+\boldsymbol{b}_{2}},\ldots,\widetilde{c}_{j,k}\overline{A}^{\boldsymbol{a}_{k}+\boldsymbol{b}_{k}}
.
Since
p^{t}=0
in
\mathcal{A}
, applying this homomorphism to Equation (
3.100
) yields

\left(\widetilde{c}_{j,1}\overline{A}^{\boldsymbol{a}_{1}+\boldsymbol{b}_{1}}+\cdots+\widetilde{c}_{j,k}\overline{A}^{\boldsymbol{a}_{k}+\boldsymbol{b}_{k}}\right)^{p^{\ell n}}=\widetilde{c}_{j,1}^{p^{\ell n}}\cdot\overline{A}^{p^{\ell n}(\boldsymbol{a}_{1}+\boldsymbol{b}_{1})}+\cdots+\widetilde{c}_{j,k}^{p^{\ell n}}\cdot\overline{A}^{p^{\ell n}(\boldsymbol{a}_{k}+\boldsymbol{b}_{k})}+\\
p\sum_{i\in\mathcal{J}}u_{i}\widetilde{c}_{j,1}^{p^{\ell(n-t)}d_{i1}}\cdot\overline{A}^{p^{\ell(n-t)}d_{i1}(\boldsymbol{a}_{1}+\boldsymbol{b}_{1})}\cdots\widetilde{c}_{j,k}^{p^{\ell(n-t)}d_{ik}}\cdot\overline{A}^{p^{\ell(n-t)}d_{ik}(\boldsymbol{a}_{k}+\boldsymbol{b}_{k})}.

(3.101)

Recall from Equation (
3.98
) that
c_{j,i}^{p^{\ell}}\cdot\overline{x}^{(p^{\ell}-1)\boldsymbol{b}_{i}}=c_{j,i}\cdot\overline{x}^{(p^{\ell}-1)\boldsymbol{b}_{j}}
for all
j,i
.
So

\widetilde{c}_{j,i}^{p^{\ell}}\cdot\overline{A}^{(p^{\ell}-1)\boldsymbol{b}_{i}}\equiv\widetilde{c}_{j,i}\cdot\overline{A}^{(p^{\ell}-1)\boldsymbol{b}_{j}}\mod\mathfrak{m}.

For any
n\geq t
, taking
p^{\ell(n-1)}
-th power,
p^{\ell(n-2)}
-th power,
\ldots,

p^{\ell(t-1)}
-th power, on both sides and using Lemma
3.14
yield respectively

\displaystyle\widetilde{c}_{j,i}^{p^{\ell n}}\cdot\overline{A}^{(p^{\ell n}-p^{\ell(n-1)})\boldsymbol{b}_{i}}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{c}_{j,i}^{p^{\ell(n-1)}}\cdot\overline{A}^{(p^{\ell n}-p^{\ell(n-1)})\boldsymbol{b}_{j}},

\displaystyle\widetilde{c}_{j,i}^{p^{\ell(n-1)}}\cdot\overline{A}^{(p^{\ell(n-1)}-p^{\ell(n-2)})\boldsymbol{b}_{i}}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{c}_{j,i}^{p^{\ell(n-2)}}\cdot\overline{A}^{(p^{\ell(n-1)}-p^{\ell(n-2)})\boldsymbol{b}_{j}},

\displaystyle\widetilde{c}_{j,i}^{p^{\ell t}}\cdot\overline{A}^{(p^{\ell t}-p^{\ell(t-1)})\boldsymbol{b}_{i}}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{c}_{j,i}^{p^{\ell(t-1)}}\cdot\overline{A}^{(p^{\ell t}-p^{\ell(t-1)})\boldsymbol{b}_{j}}.

Their product yields for all
n\geq t
,

\widetilde{c}_{j,i}^{p^{\ell n}}=\widetilde{c}_{j,i}^{p^{\ell(t-1)}}\cdot\overline{A}^{(p^{\ell n}-p^{\ell(t-1)})(\boldsymbol{b}_{j}-\boldsymbol{b}_{i})}.

(3.102)

For all
n\geq 2t
, replacing
n
with
n-t
in Equation (
3.102
) yields

\widetilde{c}_{j,i}^{p^{\ell(n-t)}}=\widetilde{c}_{j,i}^{p^{\ell(t-1)}}\cdot\overline{A}^{(p^{\ell(n-t)}-p^{\ell(t-1)})(\boldsymbol{b}_{j}-\boldsymbol{b}_{i})}.

(3.103)

Substituting the terms
\widetilde{c}_{j,i}^{p^{\ell n}}
and
\widetilde{c}_{j,i}^{p^{\ell(n-t)}}
in the right hand side of Equation (
3.101
) using Equations (
3.102
) and (
3.103
), we obtain

\left(\widetilde{c}_{j,1}\overline{A}^{\boldsymbol{a}_{1}+\boldsymbol{b}_{1}}+\cdots+\widetilde{c}_{j,k}\overline{A}^{\boldsymbol{a}_{k}+\boldsymbol{b}_{k}}\right)^{p^{\ell n}}=\\
\left(\widetilde{c}_{j,1}^{p^{\ell(t-1)}}\cdot\overline{A}^{p^{\ell n}\boldsymbol{a}_{1}+p^{\ell(t-1)}\boldsymbol{b}_{1}}+\cdots+\widetilde{c}_{j,k}^{p^{\ell(t-1)}}\cdot\overline{A}^{p^{\ell n}\boldsymbol{a}_{k}+p^{\ell(t-1)}\boldsymbol{b}_{k}}\right)\cdot\overline{A}^{(p^{\ell n}-p^{\ell(t-1)})\boldsymbol{b}_{j}}+\\
p\sum_{i\in\mathcal{J}}\left(u_{i}\widetilde{c}_{j,1}^{p^{\ell(t-1)}d_{i1}}\overline{A}^{\left(p^{\ell(n-t)}\boldsymbol{a}_{1}+p^{\ell(t-1)}\boldsymbol{b}_{1}\right)d_{i1}}\cdots\widetilde{c}_{j,k}^{p^{\ell(t-1)}d_{ik}}\overline{A}^{\left(p^{\ell(n-t)}\boldsymbol{a}_{k}+p^{\ell(t-1)}\boldsymbol{b}_{k}\right)d_{ik}}\right)\overline{A}^{(p^{\ell(n-t)}-p^{\ell(t-1)})\boldsymbol{b}_{j}\cdot p^{\ell t}},

(3.104)

for all
n\geq 2t
.
Note that the term
p^{\ell t}
on the final exponent comes from using
d_{i1}+\cdots+d_{ik}=p^{\ell t}
in the above substitution.
Using Equation (
3.104
) to substitute
\left(\widetilde{c}_{j,1}\overline{A}^{\boldsymbol{a}_{1}+\boldsymbol{b}_{1}}+\cdots+\widetilde{c}_{j,k}\overline{A}^{\boldsymbol{a}_{k}+\boldsymbol{b}_{k}}\right)^{p^{\ell n}}
in Equation (
3.99
) yields

\sum_{i=1}^{k}\overline{A}^{p^{\ell n}\boldsymbol{a}_{i}+\boldsymbol{b}_{i}}v_{i}\\
+\sum_{j=k+1}^{K^{\prime}}\left(\widetilde{c}_{j,1}^{p^{\ell(t-1)}}\cdot\overline{A}^{p^{\ell n}\boldsymbol{a}_{1}+p^{\ell(t-1)}\boldsymbol{b}_{1}}+\cdots+\widetilde{c}_{j,k}^{p^{\ell(t-1)}}\cdot\overline{A}^{p^{\ell n}\boldsymbol{a}_{k}+p^{\ell(t-1)}\boldsymbol{b}_{k}}\right)\cdot\overline{A}^{(1-p^{\ell(t-1)})\boldsymbol{b}_{j}}v_{j}\\
+p\sum_{j=k+1}^{K^{\prime}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{J}}u_{i}\widetilde{c}_{j,1}^{p^{\ell(t-1)}d_{i1}}\cdots\widetilde{c}_{j,k}^{p^{\ell(t-1)}d_{ik}}\cdot\overline{A}^{p^{\ell(n-t)}(\boldsymbol{a}_{1}d_{i1}+\cdots+\boldsymbol{a}_{k}d_{ik})+p^{\ell(t-1)}(\boldsymbol{b}_{1}d_{i1}+\cdots+\boldsymbol{b}_{k}d_{ik})}\cdot\overline{A}^{(1-p^{\ell(2t-1)})\boldsymbol{b}_{j}}v_{j}\\
+\sum_{i=K^{\prime}+1}^{K}\overline{A}^{p^{\ell n}\boldsymbol{a}_{i}+\boldsymbol{b}_{i}}v_{i}=0.

(3.105)

Combining all the terms containing
\overline{A}^{p^{\ell n}\boldsymbol{a}_{i}}
yields

\sum_{i=1}^{k}\overline{A}^{p^{\ell n}\boldsymbol{a}_{i}+\boldsymbol{b}_{i}}\left(v_{i}+\sum_{j=k+1}^{K^{\prime}}\widetilde{c}_{j,i}^{p^{\ell(t-1)}}\cdot\overline{A}^{(p^{\ell(t-1)}-1)(\boldsymbol{b}_{i}-\boldsymbol{b}_{j})}v_{j}\right)\\
+p\sum_{j=k+1}^{K^{\prime}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{J}}u_{i}\widetilde{c}_{j,1}^{p^{\ell(t-1)}d_{i1}}\cdots\widetilde{c}_{j,k}^{p^{\ell(t-1)}d_{ik}}\cdot\overline{A}^{p^{\ell(n-t)}(\boldsymbol{a}_{1}d_{i1}+\cdots+\boldsymbol{a}_{k}d_{ik})+p^{\ell(t-1)}(\boldsymbol{b}_{1}d_{i1}+\cdots+\boldsymbol{b}_{k}d_{ik})}\cdot\overline{A}^{(1-p^{\ell(2t-1)})\boldsymbol{b}_{j}}v_{j}\\
+\sum_{i=K^{\prime}+1}^{K}\overline{A}^{p^{\ell n}\boldsymbol{a}_{i}+\boldsymbol{b}_{i}}v_{i}=0.

(3.106)

We have shown that the Equation
\sum_{i=1}^{K}\overline{A}^{p^{\ell n}\boldsymbol{a}_{i}+\boldsymbol{b}_{i}}v_{i}=0
is equivalent to Equation (
3.106
) for
n\geq 2t
.

Note that
pv_{k+1},\ldots,pv_{K^{\prime}},v_{K^{\prime}+1},\ldots,v_{K}\in\mathfrak{m}^{s}\mathcal{V}
.
Therefore for all
n\geq\max\{m,2t\}
, Equation (
3.106
) yields

\sum_{i=1}^{k}\overline{A}^{p^{\ell n}\boldsymbol{a}_{i}+\boldsymbol{b}_{i}}\left(v_{i}+\sum_{j=k+1}^{K^{\prime}}\widetilde{c}_{j,i}^{p^{\ell(t-1)}}\cdot\overline{A}^{(p^{\ell(t-1)}-1)(\boldsymbol{b}_{i}-\boldsymbol{b}_{j})}v_{j}\right)\in\mathfrak{m}^{s}\mathcal{V}.

(3.107)

Note that
v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}\in\mathfrak{m}^{s-1}\mathcal{V}
, so Equation (
3.107
) is equivalent the following equation in the
\mathbb{A}
-module
\mathfrak{m}^{s-1}\mathcal{V}/\mathfrak{m}^{s}\mathcal{V}
:

\sum_{i=1}^{k}\overline{x}^{p^{\ell n}\boldsymbol{a}_{i}+\boldsymbol{b}_{i}}\left(v_{i}+\sum_{j=k+1}^{K^{\prime}}c_{j,i}^{p^{\ell(t-1)}}\cdot\overline{x}^{(p^{\ell(t-1)}-1)(\boldsymbol{b}_{i}-\boldsymbol{b}_{j})}v_{j}+\mathfrak{m}^{s}\mathcal{V}\right)=0.

Note that
\mathfrak{m}^{s-1}\mathcal{V}/\mathfrak{m}^{s}\mathcal{V}
is a finitely generated module over the field
\mathbb{A}
, and is therefore a finite dimensional
\mathbb{A}
-vector space.
Let
\pi
be any
\mathbb{A}
-linear map from
\mathfrak{m}^{s-1}\mathcal{V}/\mathfrak{m}^{s}\mathcal{V}
to
\mathbb{A}
, then

\sum_{i=1}^{k}\overline{x}^{p^{\ell n}\boldsymbol{a}_{i}+\boldsymbol{b}_{i}}\cdot\pi\left(v_{i}+\sum_{j=k+1}^{K^{\prime}}c_{j,i}^{p^{\ell(t-1)}}\cdot\overline{x}^{(p^{\ell(t-1)}-1)(\boldsymbol{b}_{i}-\boldsymbol{b}_{j})}v_{j}+\mathfrak{m}^{s}\mathcal{V}\right)=0

for all
n\geq\max\{m,2t\}
.
Recall that for all
n
, the sequences

\boldsymbol{x}_{i}^{(\geq n)}=\left(\overline{x}^{p^{\ell n}\boldsymbol{a}_{i}+\boldsymbol{b}_{i}},\overline{x}^{p^{\ell(n+1)}\boldsymbol{a}_{i}+\boldsymbol{b}_{i}},\ldots\right)\in\mathbb{A}^{\mathbb{N}}

for
i=1,2,\ldots,k
, are
\mathbb{A}
-linearly independent.
Therefore we must have

\pi\left(v_{i}+\sum_{j=k+1}^{K^{\prime}}c_{j,i}^{p^{\ell(t-1)}}\cdot\overline{x}^{(p^{\ell(t-1)}-1)(\boldsymbol{b}_{i}-\boldsymbol{b}_{j})}v_{j}+\mathfrak{m}^{s}\mathcal{V}\right)=0.

for
i=1,2,\ldots,k
.
Since this is true for all linear maps
\pi\colon\mathfrak{m}^{s-1}\mathcal{V}/\mathfrak{m}^{s}\mathcal{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}
, we have

v_{i}+\sum_{j=k+1}^{K^{\prime}}c_{j,i}^{p^{\ell(t-1)}}\cdot\overline{x}^{(p^{\ell(t-1)}-1)(\boldsymbol{b}_{i}-\boldsymbol{b}_{j})}v_{j}+\mathfrak{m}^{s}\mathcal{V}=0

for
i=1,2,\ldots,k
.
Denote

w_{i}\coloneqq v_{i}+\sum_{j=k+1}^{K^{\prime}}c_{j,i}^{p^{\ell(t-1)}}\cdot\overline{x}^{(p^{\ell(t-1)}-1)(\boldsymbol{b}_{i}-\boldsymbol{b}_{j})}v_{j}

for
i=1,\ldots,k
, then
w_{i}\in\mathfrak{m}^{s}\mathcal{V}
for all
i
.
Equation (
3.106
) can be rewritten as

\sum_{i=1}^{k}\overline{A}^{p^{\ell n}\boldsymbol{a}_{i}+\boldsymbol{b}_{i}}w_{i}\\
+p\sum_{j=k+1}^{K^{\prime}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{J}}u_{i}\widetilde{c}_{j,1}^{p^{\ell(t-1)}d_{i1}}\cdots\widetilde{c}_{j,k}^{p^{\ell(t-1)}d_{ik}}\cdot\overline{A}^{p^{\ell(n-t)}(\boldsymbol{a}_{1}d_{i1}+\cdots+\boldsymbol{a}_{k}d_{ik})+p^{\ell(t-1)}(\boldsymbol{b}_{1}d_{i1}+\cdots+\boldsymbol{b}_{k}d_{ik})}\cdot\overline{A}^{(1-p^{\ell(2t-1)})\boldsymbol{b}_{j}}\cdot v_{j}\\
+\sum_{i=K^{\prime}+1}^{K}\overline{A}^{p^{\ell n}\boldsymbol{a}_{i}+\boldsymbol{b}_{i}}v_{i}=0.

(3.108)

Note that
w_{1},\ldots,w_{k},pv_{k+1},\ldots,pv_{K^{\prime}},v_{K^{\prime}+1},\ldots,v_{K}
are now all in
\mathfrak{m}^{s}\mathcal{V}
.
The left hand side of Equation (
3.108
) is a sum of terms of the form

\overline{A}^{p^{\ell(n-t)}\boldsymbol{a}+\boldsymbol{b}}v
,
for
v\in\mathfrak{m}^{s}\mathcal{V}
and
\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathbb{Q}^{N}
whose denominators are not divisible by
p
.
Indeed,

(i)

the terms
\overline{A}^{p^{\ell n}\boldsymbol{a}_{i}+\boldsymbol{b}_{i}}w_{i},i=1,\ldots,k
, can be written in the form
\overline{A}^{p^{\ell(n-t)}\boldsymbol{a}+\boldsymbol{b}}v
, with

\boldsymbol{a}\coloneqq(p^{\ell n}-p^{\ell(n-t)})\boldsymbol{a}_{i}\;,\quad\boldsymbol{b}\coloneqq\boldsymbol{b}_{i}\;,\quad v\coloneqq w_{i}.

Similarly, the terms
\overline{A}^{p^{\ell n}\boldsymbol{a}_{i}+\boldsymbol{b}_{i}}v_{i},i=K^{\prime}+1,\ldots,K
, can be written in the form
\overline{A}^{p^{\ell(n-t)}\boldsymbol{a}+\boldsymbol{b}}v
.

(ii)

The terms

p\left(u_{i}\widetilde{c}_{j,1}^{p^{\ell(t-1)}d_{i1}}\cdots\widetilde{c}_{j,k}^{p^{\ell(t-1)}d_{ik}}\cdot\overline{A}^{p^{\ell(n-t)}(\boldsymbol{a}_{1}d_{i1}+\cdots+\boldsymbol{a}_{k}d_{ik})+p^{\ell(t-1)}(\boldsymbol{b}_{1}d_{i1}+\cdots+\boldsymbol{b}_{k}d_{ik})}\cdot\overline{A}^{(1-p^{\ell(2t-1)})\boldsymbol{b}_{j}}\right)\cdot v_{j},

for
j=k+1,\ldots,K^{\prime};i\in\mathcal{J}
,
can be written in the form
\overline{A}^{p^{\ell(n-t)}\boldsymbol{a}+\boldsymbol{b}}v
with

\boldsymbol{a}\coloneqq\boldsymbol{a}_{1}d_{i1}+\cdots+\boldsymbol{a}_{k}d_{ik}\;,\quad\boldsymbol{b}\coloneqq p^{\ell(t-1)}(\boldsymbol{b}_{1}d_{i1}+\cdots+\boldsymbol{b}_{k}d_{ik})+(1-p^{\ell(2t-1)})\boldsymbol{b}_{j},

and

v\coloneqq p\cdot u_{i}\widetilde{c}_{j,1}^{p^{\ell(t-1)}d_{i1}}\cdots\widetilde{c}_{j,k}^{p^{\ell(t-1)}d_{ik}}\cdot v_{j}.

Since Equation (
3.108
) can be written as a sum of terms
\overline{A}^{p^{\ell(n-t)}\boldsymbol{a}+\boldsymbol{b}}v
,
v\in\mathfrak{m}^{s}\mathcal{V}
, and it holds for all
n-t\geq\max\{m,2t\}-t
, we can apply the induction hypothesis on
s
, and conclude that Equation (
3.108
) holds for all
n-t\geq(t+1-s)t
.
Since Equation (
3.108
) is equivalent to
\sum_{i=1}^{K}\overline{A}^{p^{\ell n}\boldsymbol{a}_{i}+\boldsymbol{b}_{i}}v_{i}=0
for
n\geq 2t
(which is true whenever
n-t\geq(t+1-s)t
), we conclude that

\sum_{i=1}^{K}\overline{A}^{p^{\ell n}\boldsymbol{a}_{i}+\boldsymbol{b}_{i}}v_{i}=0

holds for all
n\geq t+(t+1-s)t=\big(t+1-(s-1)\big)t
.
This finishes the induction step on
s
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
3.46
.

Proposition
3.46
follows directly from Lemma
3.50
by taking
s=0
.
∎

Symmetrization III: conclusion.

In this subsection we finish the proof of Theorem
1.3
.
Recall from Corollary
3.45
that
\mathfrak{Z}(\alpha)
is a finite union of sets of the form

\Bigg\{\sum_{j=1}^{r}p^{\ell(k_{1}+k_{2}+\cdots+k_{j})}\cdot\boldsymbol{a}_{j}+\boldsymbol{h}\;\Bigg|\;k_{1},\ldots,k_{r}\in\mathbb{N},\;\boldsymbol{h}\in H\Bigg\}\\
=\Bigg\{\sum_{j=1}^{r}p^{\ell n_{j}}\cdot\boldsymbol{a}_{j}+\boldsymbol{h}\;\Bigg|\;0\leq n_{1}\leq n_{2}\leq\cdots\leq n_{r}\in\mathbb{N},\;\boldsymbol{h}\in H\Bigg\}.

(3.109)

We will use Proposition
3.46
to decrease the exponents
\ell n_{i}
, and show
\mathfrak{Z}(\alpha)
is a finite union of
p
-succinct sets.

Proof of Theorem
1.3
.

See Figures
10
-
10
for an illustration of the proof.
Denote

\mathcal{T}\coloneqq\Bigg\{\sum_{j=1}^{r}p^{\ell n_{j}}\cdot\boldsymbol{a}_{j}+\boldsymbol{h}\;\Bigg|\;0\leq n_{1}\leq n_{2}\leq\cdots\leq n_{r}\in\mathbb{N},\;\boldsymbol{h}\in H\Bigg\}.

Figure 8:
Decomposition of the set
\mathcal{T}
.

Figure 9:
Applying Proposition
3.46
.

Figure 10:
A finite union of
p
-succinct sets.

We can write
\mathcal{T}
as a union
\mathcal{T}_{0}\cup\mathcal{T}_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\mathcal{T}_{r}
, where

\mathcal{T}_{0}\coloneqq\Bigg\{\sum_{j=1}^{r}p^{\ell n_{j}}\cdot\boldsymbol{a}_{j}+\boldsymbol{h}\;\Bigg|\;t^{2}+t\leq n_{1}\leq n_{2}\leq\cdots\leq n_{r},\;\boldsymbol{h}\in H\Bigg\},\\
\mathcal{T}_{1}\coloneqq\Bigg\{\sum_{j=1}^{r}p^{\ell n_{j}}\cdot\boldsymbol{a}_{j}+\boldsymbol{h}\;\Bigg|\;0\leq n_{1}\leq t^{2}+t\leq n_{2}\leq\cdots\leq n_{r},\;\boldsymbol{h}\in H\Bigg\},\\
\ddots\\
\mathcal{T}_{r}\coloneqq\Bigg\{\sum_{j=1}^{r}p^{\ell n_{j}}\cdot\boldsymbol{a}_{j}+\boldsymbol{h}\;\Bigg|\;0\leq n_{1}\leq n_{2}\leq\cdots\leq n_{r}\leq t^{2}+t,\;\boldsymbol{h}\in H\Bigg\}.

For each
i=0,1,\ldots,r
, consider the expression
\sum_{j=1}^{r}p^{\ell n_{j}}\cdot\boldsymbol{a}_{j}+\boldsymbol{h},0\leq n_{1}\leq\cdots\leq n_{i}\leq t^{2}+t\leq n_{i+1}\leq\cdots\leq n_{r},
in the set
\mathcal{T}_{i}
.
Apply Proposition
3.46
successively for
n=n_{r},n_{r-1},\ldots,n_{i+2}
, we obtain a new set
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{i}\supseteq\mathcal{T}_{i}
such that
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{i}\subseteq\mathfrak{Z}(\alpha)
, where

\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{i}\coloneqq\Bigg\{\sum_{j=1}^{r}p^{\ell n_{j}}\cdot\boldsymbol{a}_{j}+\boldsymbol{h}\;\Bigg|\;0\leq n_{1}\leq\cdots\leq n_{i}\leq t^{2}+t,\;t^{2}+t\leq n_{i+1},\ldots,t^{2}+t\leq n_{r},\;\boldsymbol{h}\in H\Bigg\}.

Each set
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{i}
can be written as a finite union of
p
-succinct sets

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{i}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{0\leq n_{1}\leq\cdots\leq n_{i}\leq t^{2}+t}\Bigg\{\sum_{j=1}^{r}p^{\ell n_{j}}\cdot\boldsymbol{a}_{j}+\boldsymbol{h}\;\Bigg|\;t^{2}+t\leq n_{i+1},\ldots,t^{2}+t\leq n_{r},\;\boldsymbol{h}\in H\Bigg\}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{0\leq n_{1}\leq\cdots\leq n_{i}\leq t^{2}+t}\Bigg\{\left(p^{\ell n_{1}}\boldsymbol{a}_{1}+\cdots+p^{\ell n_{i}}\boldsymbol{a}_{i}\right)+\sum_{j=i+1}^{r}p^{\ell n^{\prime}_{j}}\cdot\left(p^{\ell(t^{2}+t)}\boldsymbol{a}_{j}\right)+\boldsymbol{h}\;\Bigg|\;

\displaystyle\hskip 284.52756ptn^{\prime}_{i+1},\ldots,n^{\prime}_{r}\in\mathbb{N},\;\boldsymbol{h}\in H\Bigg\}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{0\leq n_{1}\leq\cdots\leq n_{i}\leq t^{2}+t}S\left(\ell;p^{\ell n_{1}}\boldsymbol{a}_{1}+\cdots+p^{\ell n_{i}}\boldsymbol{a}_{i},p^{\ell(t^{2}+t)}\boldsymbol{a}_{i+1},\ldots,p^{\ell(t^{2}+t)}\boldsymbol{a}_{r};H\right).

Since
\mathfrak{Z}(\alpha)\supseteq\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{0}\cup\cdots\cup\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{r}\right)\supseteq\left(\mathcal{T}_{0}\cup\cdots\cup\mathcal{T}_{r}\right)=\mathcal{T}
, and
\mathfrak{Z}(\alpha)
is a finite union of different
\mathcal{T}
’s, we obtain that
\mathfrak{Z}(\alpha)
is a finite union of different
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}\coloneqq\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{0}\cup\cdots\cup\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{r}
.
Since each
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{i}
is a finite union of
p
-succinct sets, we conclude that
\mathfrak{Z}(\alpha)
is also a finite union of
p
-succinct sets, hence
p
-normal.
∎

4
Linear-exponential Diophantine equations to S-unit equation

In this section we reduce linear-exponential Diophantine equations to S-unit equations:

Proposition 4.1
.

Let
T=p_{1}^{e_{1}}p_{2}^{e_{2}}\cdots p_{k}^{e_{k}}
be as in Theorem
1.4
.
Deciding whether a system of linear-exponential Diophantine equations (Equations (
(2)
)) admits a solution reduces to deciding whether an S-unit equation in a
\mathbb{Z}_{/T}[X_{1}^{\pm},\ldots,X_{N}^{\pm}]
-module (Equation (
1.5
)) admits a solution.

The main idea is as follows.
Suppose we are given a system of the form (
(2)
):

\displaystyle c_{1,1}\cdot q_{1}^{n_{1}}+\cdots+c_{1,d}\cdot q_{d}^{n_{d}}+c_{1,d+1}\cdot z_{d+1}+\cdots+c_{1,D}\cdot z_{D}

\displaystyle=b_{1},

\displaystyle c_{L,1}\cdot q_{1}^{n_{1}}+\cdots+c_{L,d}\cdot q_{d}^{n_{d}}+c_{L,d+1}\cdot z_{d+1}+\cdots+c_{L,D}\cdot z_{D}

\displaystyle=b_{L},

with
1\leq d\leq D
,
q_{1},\ldots,q_{d}\in\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}\}
.
We will construct a finitely presented
\mathbb{Z}_{/T}[X_{1}^{\pm},\ldots,X_{D}^{\pm}]
-module
\mathcal{M}
and its elements
m_{0},m_{1},\ldots,m_{K}
, satisfying the following desired property.

Desired property:

Let
(z_{11},\ldots,z_{1D})\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}
, the equation

X_{1}^{z_{11}}X_{2}^{z_{12}}\cdots X_{D}^{z_{1D}}\cdot m_{1}+\cdots+X_{1}^{z_{K1}}X_{2}^{z_{K2}}\cdots X_{D}^{z_{KD}}\cdot m_{K}=m_{0}

(4.1)

can be satisfied for some
(z_{21},\ldots,z_{2D},\ldots,z_{K1},\ldots,z_{KD})\in\mathbb{Z}^{(K-1)D}
, if and only if

\displaystyle c_{1,1}\cdot z_{11}+\cdots+c_{1,D}\cdot z_{1D}=b_{1},

\displaystyle\hskip 99.58464pt\vdots

(4.2)

\displaystyle c_{L,1}\cdot z_{11}+\cdots+c_{L,D}\cdot z_{1D}=b_{L},

\displaystyle z_{11}\in q_{1}^{\mathbb{N}},z_{12}\in q_{2}^{\mathbb{N}},\ldots,z_{1d}\in q_{d}^{\mathbb{N}}.

Here,
p^{\mathbb{N}}
denotes the set
\{p^{n}\mid n\in\mathbb{N}\}
.
This desired property will allow us to reduce solving the system of linear-exponential Diophantine equations (
4
) to solving the S-unit equation (
4.1
).
Our strategy is to, for each equation in (
4
), construct an S-unit equation in some module
\mathcal{M}
.
We then combine these S-unit equations (in different modules
\mathcal{M}
) into a single one by taking the direct product of these modules.

First, let us take care of the linear equations in (
4
).

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
c_{1},\ldots,c_{D},b\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Define the
\mathbb{Z}_{/T}[X_{1}^{\pm},\ldots,X_{D}^{\pm}]
-module

\mathcal{M}\coloneqq\mathbb{Z}_{/T}[X_{1}^{\pm},\ldots,X_{D}^{\pm},Y^{\pm}]/\left\langle{X_{1}-Y^{c_{1}},X_{2}-Y^{c_{2}},\ldots,X_{D}-Y^{c_{D}}}\right\rangle.

(4.3)

Then
(z_{11},\ldots,z_{1D})\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}
satisfies

X_{1}^{z_{11}}X_{2}^{z_{12}}\cdots X_{D}^{z_{1D}}=Y^{b}\qquad\text{ in }\mathcal{M},

(4.4)

if and only if
c_{1}\cdot z_{11}+\cdots+c_{D}\cdot z_{1D}=b
.

Proof.

Note that
\mathcal{M}
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}_{/T}[Y^{\pm}]
by the map
X_{i}\mapsto Y^{c_{i}},i=1,\ldots,D
.
Under this map, Equation (
4.4
) becomes
Y^{c_{1}z_{11}}Y^{c_{2}z_{12}}\cdots Y^{c_{D}z_{1D}}=Y^{b}
, which is equivalent to
c_{1}\cdot z_{11}+\cdots+c_{D}\cdot z_{1D}=b
.
∎

The
\mathbb{Z}_{/T}[X_{1}^{\pm},\ldots,X_{D}^{\pm}]
-module
\mathcal{M}
defined in Equation (
4.3
) might not be finitely generated.
However, both sides of Equation (
4.4
) fall in the submodule generated by
1
and
Y^{b}
, so we can restrict
\mathcal{M}
to this submodule.
Then
\mathcal{M}
becomes finitely generated and hence finitely presented.
7
7

7

Computing the finite presentation of a finitely generated submodule of
\mathcal{M}
is effective, see
[
BCMI81
, Theorem 2.14]
or
[
BCR94
, Theorem 2.6]
.

Next, let us take care of the “exponential” parts of (
4
), using a similar construction to Example
3.2
(or
[
Der07
, Example 1.3]
).

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
z\in\mathbb{Z}
and
p
be a prime number. Then
z\in p^{\mathbb{N}}
if and only if

X_{2}^{z}-X_{1}^{z}=1

in the
\mathbb{F}_{p}[X_{1}^{\pm},X_{2}^{\pm}]
-module
\mathbb{F}_{p}[X_{1}^{\pm},X_{2}^{\pm}]/\left\langle{X_{2}-X_{1}-1}\right\rangle
.

Proof.

If
z=p^{n}
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
, then
(X_{1}+1)^{p^{n}}=X_{1}^{p^{n}}+1
.
We then have
X_{2}^{z}-X_{1}^{z}=(X_{1}+1)^{p^{n}}-X_{1}^{p^{n}}=1
in the module
\mathbb{F}_{p}[X_{1}^{\pm},X_{2}^{\pm}]/\left\langle{X_{2}-X_{1}-1}\right\rangle
.

For the other implication, suppose
X_{2}^{z}-X_{1}^{z}=1
in
\mathbb{F}_{p}[X_{1}^{\pm},X_{2}^{\pm}]/\left\langle{X_{2}-X_{1}-1}\right\rangle
.
If
z\leq 0
, write
z=-y
with
y\geq 0
.
Then
X_{1}^{y}-X_{2}^{y}=X_{1}^{y}X_{2}^{y}
in
\mathbb{F}_{p}[X_{1}^{\pm},X_{2}^{\pm}]/\left\langle{X_{2}-X_{1}-1}\right\rangle
.
This is equivalent to

X_{1}^{y}-(X_{1}+1)^{y}=X_{1}^{y}(X_{1}+1)^{y}

(4.5)

in
\mathbb{F}_{p}[X_{1}]
.
The form of Equation (
4.5
) suggest we must have
X_{1}^{y}\mid(X_{1}+1)^{y}
, so
y=0
. But
y=0
is not a solution of (
4.5
).

If
z>0
, then the situation is similar to Example
3.2
.
For a rigorous proof, the equation
X_{2}^{z}-X_{1}^{z}=1
in
\mathbb{F}_{p}[X_{1}^{\pm},X_{2}^{\pm}]/\left\langle{X_{2}-X_{1}-1}\right\rangle
is equivalent to

(X_{1}+1)^{z}=X_{1}^{z}+1

in
\mathbb{F}_{p}[X_{1}]
.
Write
z=p^{n}+a
with
0\leq a<p^{n},\;n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Then

(X_{1}+1)^{z}=(X_{1}+1)^{p^{n}}(X_{1}+1)^{a}=(X_{1}^{p^{n}}+1)(X_{1}+1)^{a}=X_{1}^{p^{n}}(X_{1}+1)^{a}+(X_{1}+1)^{a}.

Every monomial appearing in
X_{1}^{p^{n}}(X_{1}+1)^{a}
has degree larger than every monomial appearing
(X_{1}+1)^{a}
. But
(X_{1}+1)^{z}=X_{1}^{z}+1
has only two monomials.
So both
X_{1}^{p^{n}}(X_{1}+1)^{a}
and
(X_{1}+1)^{a}
must be monomials, meaning
a=0
.
Therefore
z=p^{n}
, and we conclude that
z\in p^{\mathbb{N}}
.
∎

We then need to express the equation in Lemma
4.3
as a system of “full” S-unit equations:

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
p
be a prime number and let
z_{1i}\in\mathbb{Z}
for some
i\in\{1,\ldots,D\}
.
One can construct a system of S-unit equations (
4.1
) with
K=2
, in finitely presented
\mathbb{F}_{p}[X_{1}^{\pm},\ldots,X_{D}^{\pm}]
-modules, such that
z_{1i}\in p^{\mathbb{N}}
if and only if it extends to a solution
(z_{11},\ldots,z_{1D},z_{21},\ldots,z_{2D})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2D}
for this system.

Proof.

Without loss of generality suppose
i=1
.
We claim that
z_{11}\in p^{\mathbb{N}}
if and only if it extends to a solution for the following system

\begin{cases}&X_{1}^{z_{11}}X_{2}^{z_{12}}\cdots X_{D}^{z_{1D}}\cdot(-1)+X_{1}^{z_{21}}X_{2}^{z_{22}}\cdots X_{D}^{z_{2D}}\cdot 1=1\\
&\hskip 142.26378pt\text{ in }\;\mathbb{F}_{p}[X_{1}^{\pm},\ldots,X_{D}^{\pm}]/\left\langle{X_{2}-X_{1}-1,X_{3}-1,\ldots,X_{D}-1}\right\rangle\\
&X_{1}^{z_{11}}X_{2}^{z_{12}}\cdots X_{D}^{z_{1D}}\cdot 0+X_{1}^{z_{21}}X_{2}^{z_{22}}\cdots X_{D}^{z_{2D}}\cdot 1=1\\
&\hskip 142.26378pt\text{ in }\;\mathbb{F}_{p}[X_{1}^{\pm},\ldots,X_{D}^{\pm}]/\left\langle{X_{2}-1,X_{3}-1,\ldots,X_{D}-1}\right\rangle\\
&X_{1}^{z_{11}}X_{2}^{z_{12}}\cdots X_{D}^{z_{1D}}\cdot 1+X_{1}^{z_{21}}X_{2}^{z_{22}}\cdots X_{D}^{z_{2D}}\cdot 0=1\\
&\hskip 142.26378pt\text{ in }\;\mathbb{F}_{p}[X_{1}^{\pm},\ldots,X_{D}^{\pm}]/\left\langle{X_{1}-1,X_{3}-1,\ldots,X_{D}-1}\right\rangle\\
&X_{1}^{z_{11}}X_{2}^{z_{12}}\cdots X_{D}^{z_{1D}}\cdot(-1)+X_{1}^{z_{21}}X_{2}^{z_{22}}\cdots X_{D}^{z_{2D}}\cdot 1=0\\
&\hskip 142.26378pt\text{ in }\;\mathbb{F}_{p}[X_{1}^{\pm},\ldots,X_{D}^{\pm}]/\left\langle{X_{2}-X_{1},X_{3}-1,\ldots,X_{D}-1}\right\rangle.\\
\end{cases}

(4.6)

Note that the elements
X_{3}-1,\ldots,X_{D}-1
in the quotient means that
X_{3}=1,\ldots,X_{D}=1
in the respective modules.
Thus, the second equation of (
4.6
) is equivalent to
X_{1}^{z_{21}}=1
, which means
z_{21}=0
.
The third equation of (
4.6
) is equivalent to
X_{2}^{z_{12}}=1
, which means
z_{12}=0
.
Then, the fourth equation of (
4.6
) becomes
-X_{1}^{z_{11}}+X_{2}^{z_{22}}=0
, in
\mathbb{F}_{p}[X_{1}^{\pm},\ldots,X_{D}^{\pm}]/\left\langle{X_{2}-X_{1},X_{3}-1,\ldots,X_{D}-1}\right\rangle
this yields
z_{11}=z_{22}
.
Finally, putting
z_{12}=z_{21}=0,\;z_{11}=z_{22}
, in the first equation of (
4.6
) yields
-X_{1}^{z_{11}}+X_{2}^{z_{11}}=1
in
\mathbb{F}_{p}[X_{1}^{\pm},X_{2}^{\pm}]/\left\langle{X_{2}-X_{1}-1}\right\rangle
.
Using Lemma
4.3
, this is equivalent to
z_{11}=z_{22}\in p^{\mathbb{N}}
.
Thus, the system (
4.6
) has a solution if and only if
z_{11}\in p^{\mathbb{N}}
.
∎

We are now ready to complete the proof of Proposition
4.1
, and consequently, of Theorem
1.4
.

Proof of Proposition
4.1
.

Suppose we are given a system of the form (
(2)
), which we rewrite as

\displaystyle c_{1,1}\cdot z_{11}+\cdots+c_{1,D}\cdot z_{1D}=b_{1},

\displaystyle\hskip 99.58464pt\vdots

(4.7)

\displaystyle c_{L,1}\cdot z_{11}+\cdots+c_{L,D}\cdot z_{1D}=b_{L},

\displaystyle z_{11}\in q_{1}^{\mathbb{N}},z_{12}\in q_{2}^{\mathbb{N}},\ldots,z_{1d}\in q_{d}^{\mathbb{N}}.

Note that Equation (
4.4
) in Lemma
4.2
can also be written as

X_{1}^{z_{11}}\cdots X_{D}^{z_{1D}}\cdot 1+X_{1}^{z_{21}}\cdots X_{D}^{z_{2D}}\cdot 0=Y^{b}\qquad\text{ in }\mathcal{M}

over the variables
(z_{11},\ldots,z_{1D},z_{21},\ldots,z_{2D})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2D}
. And we can restrict the module
\mathcal{M}
to its finitely presented submodule
\left\langle{1,Y^{b}}\right\rangle
.

Therefore by Lemma
4.2
and Lemma
4.4
, we can construct a system of S-unit equations

\displaystyle X_{1}^{z_{11}}\cdots X_{D}^{z_{1D}}\cdot m_{11}+X_{1}^{z_{21}}\cdots X_{D}^{z_{2D}}\cdot m_{12}=m_{10}

\displaystyle\qquad\text{ in }\mathcal{M}_{1},

\displaystyle\hskip 170.71652pt\vdots

(4.8)

\displaystyle X_{1}^{z_{11}}\cdots X_{D}^{z_{1D}}\cdot m_{s1}+X_{1}^{z_{21}}\cdots X_{D}^{z_{2D}}\cdot m_{s2}=m_{s0}

\displaystyle\qquad\text{ in }\mathcal{M}_{s},

where each
\mathcal{M}_{i}
is a finitely presented
\mathbb{F}_{q_{i}}[X_{1}^{\pm},\ldots,X_{D}^{\pm}]
-module for some
q_{i}\in\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}\}
, with the following property.
For a tuple
(z_{11},\ldots,z_{1D})\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}
, it satisfies the system (
4
) if and only if it extends to a solution
(z_{11},\ldots,z_{1D},z_{21},\ldots,z_{2D})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2D}
for the system (
4
).

Since
T=p_{1}^{e_{1}}p_{2}^{e_{2}}\cdots p_{k}^{e_{k}}
and
q_{i}\in\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}\}
, each
\mathcal{M}_{i}
is also a finitely presented module over the ring
\mathbb{Z}_{/T}[X_{1}^{\pm},\ldots,X_{D}^{\pm}]
.
Let
\mathcal{M}
denote the direct product
\mathcal{M}_{1}\times\mathcal{M}_{2}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{M}_{s}
, it is also a finitely presented
\mathbb{Z}_{/T}[X_{1}^{\pm},\ldots,X_{D}^{\pm}]
-module.
Thus, the system of equations (
4
) can be written as a single equation

X_{1}^{z_{11}}\cdots X_{D}^{z_{1D}}\cdot(m_{11},\ldots,m_{s1})+X_{1}^{z_{21}}\cdots X_{D}^{z_{2D}}\cdot(m_{12},\ldots,m_{s2})=(m_{10},\ldots,m_{s0})

in the
\mathbb{Z}_{/T}[X_{1}^{\pm},\ldots,X_{D}^{\pm}]
-module
\mathcal{M}
.
The concludes the proof.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.4
.

Theorem
1.4
follows directly from Corollary
3.1
and Proposition
4.1
.
∎

Appendix A
Intersection of
p
-normal sets is
p
-normal

In this appendix we provide a self-contained proof of Proposition
3.8
:

See
3.8

Definition A.1
.

A subset
N\subseteq\mathbb{N}^{R}
is called a
rectangular coset
if it is of the form
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{0}+\mathbb{N}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{1}+\cdots+\mathbb{N}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{r}
for some
r\in\mathbb{N}
, where each
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{i},i=1,\ldots,r
is of the form
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{i}=(\epsilon_{i1},\ldots,\epsilon_{iR})
,

\epsilon_{ij}=\begin{cases}c_{i}\quad j\in S_{i},\\
0\quad j\notin S_{i},\end{cases}

with
c_{1},\ldots,c_{r}\in\mathbb{N}
and
S_{1},\ldots,S_{r}
are pairwise disjoint subsets of
\{1,\ldots,R\}
.

For example,
\{(2a,2a,3b,3b+1,7)\mid a,b\in\mathbb{N}\}
is a rectangular coset in
\mathbb{N}^{5}
, with
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{0}=(0,0,0,1,7)
,
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{1}=(2,2,0,0,0)
and
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{2}=(0,0,3,3,0)
.

Lemma A.2
.

Let
q\geq 2
be an integer,
G
be a subgroup of
\mathbb{Z}^{KN}
, and
\boldsymbol{a}_{0},\boldsymbol{a}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{a}_{R}\in\mathbb{Z}^{KN}
.
Then the set of
(k_{1},\ldots,k_{R})\in\mathbb{N}^{R}
satisfying

\boldsymbol{a}_{0}+q^{k_{1}}\boldsymbol{a}_{1}+\cdots+q^{k_{R}}\boldsymbol{a}_{R}\in G

(A.1)

is (effectively) a finite union of rectangular cosets.

Proof.

Let
\boldsymbol{g}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{g}_{s}
be a
\mathbb{Z}
-basis of
G
.
Define the following
\mathbb{Q}
-linear subspace of
\mathbb{Q}^{KN}
:

\overline{G}\coloneqq\mathbb{Q}\boldsymbol{g}_{1}+\cdots\mathbb{Q}\boldsymbol{g}_{s}.

Then the quotient
\mathbb{Q}^{KN}/\overline{G}\cong\mathbb{Q}^{KN-s}
is again a
\mathbb{Q}
-linear space.
Let
\|\cdot\|
be any norm on
\mathbb{Q}^{KN}/\overline{G}\cong\mathbb{Q}^{KN-s}
.

We use induction on
R
.
Consider the case
R=1
.
If
\boldsymbol{a}_{1}\in\overline{G}
, then there exists
t\in\mathbb{N}
such that
t\boldsymbol{a}_{1}\in G
.
Let
c>b\geq 1
be two different integers such that
q^{c}\equiv q^{b}\mod t
, then for all
k\geq c
we have
\boldsymbol{a}_{0}+q^{k}\boldsymbol{a}_{1}\in G\iff\boldsymbol{a}_{0}+q^{k-(c-b)}\boldsymbol{a}_{1}\in G
.
Therefore, the set of
k_{1}\in\mathbb{N}
satisfying
\boldsymbol{a}_{0}+q^{k_{1}}\boldsymbol{a}_{1}\in G
is a finite subset of
\{0,1,\ldots,b\}
plus a finite union of arithmetic progressions
\{i+(c-b)\mathbb{N}\}
with
b\leq i<c
.
This is a finite union of rectangular cosets.

If
\boldsymbol{a}_{1}\notin\overline{G}
, then
\|\boldsymbol{a}_{1}\|>0
. Therefore if
\boldsymbol{a}_{0}+q^{k_{1}}\boldsymbol{a}_{1}\in G
we must have
q^{k_{1}}\leq\frac{\|\boldsymbol{a}_{0}\|}{\|\boldsymbol{a}_{1}\|}
.
Therefore
k_{1}
is bounded, so the solution set is finite.

For the induction step, consider two cases.
Let
\Lambda
denote the solution set of Equation (
A.1
).

Case 1:

If there is some
i\in\{1,\ldots,R\}
such that
\boldsymbol{a}_{i}\in\overline{G}
, then there exists
t\in\mathbb{N}
such that
t\boldsymbol{a}_{i}\in G
. Let
c>b\geq 1
be two integers such that
q^{c}\equiv q^{b}\mod t
, then for all
k\geq c
we have
\boldsymbol{a}_{0}+q^{k_{1}}\boldsymbol{a}_{1}+\cdots+q^{k_{i}}\boldsymbol{a}_{i}+\cdots+q^{k_{R}}\boldsymbol{a}_{R}\in G\iff\boldsymbol{a}_{0}+q^{k_{1}}\boldsymbol{a}_{1}+\cdots+q^{k_{i}-(c-b)}\boldsymbol{a}_{i}+\cdots+q^{k_{R}}\boldsymbol{a}_{R}\in G
.
Therefore, the solution set
\Lambda
can be decomposed into a finite union

\Lambda=\bigcup_{r=0}^{b-1}\Lambda_{r}\cup\bigcup_{r=b}^{c-1}\Lambda_{r},

where for
r=0,\ldots,b-1
,

\Lambda_{r}=\left\{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{i-1},r,k_{i+1},\ldots,k_{R})\;\middle|\;\boldsymbol{a}_{0}+q^{k_{1}}\boldsymbol{a}_{1}+\cdots+q^{r}\boldsymbol{a}_{i}+\cdots+q^{k_{R}}\boldsymbol{a}_{R}\in G\right\},

and for
r=b,\ldots,c-1
,

\Lambda_{r}=\left\{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{i-1},r+n(c-b),k_{i+1},\ldots,k_{R})\;\middle|\;n\in\mathbb{N},\boldsymbol{a}_{0}+q^{k_{1}}\boldsymbol{a}_{1}+\cdots+q^{r}\boldsymbol{a}_{i}+\cdots+q^{k_{R}}\in G\right\}.

By the induction hypothesis, for each
r=0,\ldots,b-1,b,\ldots,c-1
, the set

\left\{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{i-1},k_{i+1},\ldots,k_{R})\;\middle|\;\boldsymbol{a}_{0}+q^{k_{1}}\boldsymbol{a}_{1}+\cdots+q^{r}\boldsymbol{a}_{i}+\cdots+q^{k_{R}}\in G\right\}

is a finite union of rectangular cosets of
\mathbb{N}^{R-1}
.
Therefore each
\Lambda_{r}
is also a finite union of rectangular cosets of
\mathbb{N}^{R}
.

Case 2:

If
\boldsymbol{a}_{i}\notin\overline{G}
for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,R\}
, that is,
\|\boldsymbol{a}_{i}\|>0
for all
i
.
Choose
D\in\mathbb{N}
such that

q^{D}\|\boldsymbol{a}_{i}\|>\sum_{j\neq i}\|\boldsymbol{a}_{i}\|

(A.2)

for all
i=1,\ldots,R
.

Define
k_{0}\coloneqq 0
.
We claim that for every
(k_{1},\ldots,k_{R})\in\Lambda
there exist distinct indices
i,j\in\{0,1,\ldots,R\}
such that
|k_{j}-k_{i}|\leq D
.
Take
i
such that
k_{i}
is maximal. Suppose on the contrary that
k_{i}>D+k_{j}
for all
j\neq i
. Then by
\sum_{j=0}^{R}q^{k_{j}}\boldsymbol{a}_{j}=0
we have

q^{k_{i}}\|\boldsymbol{a}_{i}\|=\left\|-\sum_{j\neq i}^{R}q^{k_{j}}\boldsymbol{a}_{j}\right\|\leq\sum_{j\neq i}^{R}q^{k_{j}}\|\boldsymbol{a}_{j}\|\leq\sum_{j\neq i}^{R}q^{k_{i}-D}\|\boldsymbol{a}_{j}\|.

This contradicts (
A.2
).
Therefore
|k_{j}-k_{i}|\leq D
for some
j\neq i
.

We can thus write

\Lambda=\bigcup_{0\leq i<j\leq R}\bigcup_{r=-d}^{d}\Lambda_{i,j,r},

where

\Lambda_{i,j,r}=\{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{R})\in\Lambda\mid k_{j}=k_{i}+r\}\\
=\left\{(k_{1},\ldots,k_{i},\ldots,k_{j}=k_{i}+r,\ldots,k_{R})\;\middle|\;\boldsymbol{a}_{0}+\cdots+q^{k_{i}}\boldsymbol{a}_{i}+\cdots+q^{k_{i}+r}\boldsymbol{a}_{j}+\cdots+q^{k_{R}}\boldsymbol{a}_{R}\in G\right\}.

Apply the induction hypothesis with
\boldsymbol{a}_{i}
as
\boldsymbol{a}_{i}+q^{r}\boldsymbol{a}_{j}
, we conclude that
\Lambda_{i,j,r}
is a finite union of rectangular cosets.
Therefore,
\Lambda
is a finite union of rectangular cosets.

∎

Proof of Proposition
3.8
.

Since a
p
-normal set is a finite union of
p
-succinct sets, it suffices to show that the intersection of two
p
-succinct sets is effectively
p
-normal.
Let

S=\left\{\boldsymbol{a}_{0}+p^{\ell k_{1}}\boldsymbol{a}_{1}+\cdots+p^{\ell k_{r}}\boldsymbol{a}_{r}+\boldsymbol{h}\;\middle|\;k_{1},k_{2},\ldots,k_{r}\in\mathbb{N},\boldsymbol{h}\in H\right\}

and

S^{\prime}=\left\{\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}_{0}+p^{\ell^{\prime}k_{1}}\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}_{1}+\cdots+p^{\ell^{\prime}k_{r^{\prime}}}\boldsymbol{a}_{r^{\prime}}+\boldsymbol{h}\;\middle|\;k_{1},k_{2},\ldots,k_{r^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{N},\boldsymbol{h}\in H^{\prime}\right\}

be two
p
-succinct sets.
Let
L
be a common multiplier of
\ell
and
\ell^{\prime}
.
Then
S
can be written as a finite union of
p
-succinct sets

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{i_{1}=0}^{\frac{L}{\ell}-1}\cdots\bigcup_{i_{r}=0}^{\frac{L}{\ell}-1}\left\{\boldsymbol{a}_{0}+p^{Lk_{1}}(p^{\ell i_{1}}\boldsymbol{a}_{1})+\cdots+p^{Lk_{r}}(p^{\ell i_{r}}\boldsymbol{a}_{r})+\boldsymbol{h}\;\middle|\;k_{1},k_{2},\ldots,k_{r}\in\mathbb{N},\boldsymbol{h}\in H\right\}.

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{i_{1}=0}^{\frac{L}{\ell}-1}\cdots\bigcup_{i_{r}=0}^{\frac{L}{\ell}-1}S\left(L;\boldsymbol{a}_{0},p^{\ell i_{1}}\boldsymbol{a}_{1},\ldots,p^{\ell i_{r}}\boldsymbol{a}_{r};H\right).

Therefore we can without loss of generality replace
\ell
with
L
.
Similarly we can without loss of generality replace
\ell^{\prime}
with
L
.
Therefore from now on we suppose
\ell=\ell^{\prime}
.

Let
U=\{u_{1},\ldots,u_{k}\}
be a
\mathbb{Z}
-basis for
H\cap H^{\prime}
. Extend
U
to a maximal
\mathbb{Z}
-independent subset
\{u_{1},\ldots,u_{k},v_{1},\ldots,v_{m}\}
of
H
. That is,
\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathbb{Z}u_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbb{Z}v_{i}
is a finite index subgroup of
H
.
Similarly, extend
U
to a maximal
\mathbb{Z}
-independent subset
\{u_{1},\ldots,u_{k},v^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,v^{\prime}_{m^{\prime}}\}
of
H^{\prime}
.
Note that
u_{1},\ldots,u_{k},v_{1},\ldots,v_{m},v^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,v^{\prime}_{m^{\prime}}
are
\mathbb{Z}
-independent. Indeed, suppose
\sum_{i=1}^{k}y_{i}u_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{m}z_{i}v_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{m^{\prime}}z^{\prime}_{i}v^{\prime}_{i}=0
for some
y_{i},z_{i},z^{\prime}_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
, then
H^{\prime}\ni\sum_{i=1}^{m^{\prime}}z^{\prime}_{i}v^{\prime}_{i}=-(\sum_{i=1}^{k}y_{i}e_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{m}z_{i}v_{i})\in H
.
Therefore
\sum_{i=1}^{m}z_{i}v_{i}\in H\cap H^{\prime}
, so
z_{i}=0
for all
i
. Similarly
z^{\prime}_{i}=0
for all
i
. Consequently
\sum_{i=1}^{k}y_{i}u_{i}=0
, so
y_{i}=0
for all
i
.

Let
h_{1},\ldots,h_{s}\in H
be the representatives of
H/(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathbb{Z}u_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbb{Z}v_{i})
, and let
h^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,h^{\prime}_{s^{\prime}}\in H^{\prime}
be the representatives of
H^{\prime}/(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathbb{Z}u_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{m^{\prime}}\mathbb{Z}v^{\prime}_{i})
.
Let

\widetilde{S}\coloneqq\boldsymbol{a}_{0}+p^{\ell\mathbb{N}}\boldsymbol{a}_{1}+\cdots+p^{\ell\mathbb{N}}\boldsymbol{a}_{r}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathbb{Z}u_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbb{Z}v_{i}

and

\widetilde{S}^{\prime}\coloneqq\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}_{0}+p^{\ell\mathbb{N}}\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}_{1}+\cdots+p^{\ell\mathbb{N}}\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}_{r^{\prime}}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathbb{Z}u_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{m^{\prime}}\mathbb{Z}v^{\prime}_{i}.

Then
S\cap S^{\prime}=\bigcup_{i=1}^{s}\bigcup_{j=1}^{s^{\prime}}\big((h_{i}+\widetilde{S})\cap(h^{\prime}_{j}+\widetilde{S}^{\prime})\big)
.
Therefore it suffices to show that each
(h_{i}+\widetilde{S})\cap(h^{\prime}_{j}+\widetilde{S}^{\prime})
is
p
-normal. By replacing
\boldsymbol{a}_{0}
with
\boldsymbol{a}_{0}+h_{i}
and
\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}_{0}
with
\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}_{0}+h^{\prime}_{j}
we can without loss of generality suppose
h_{i}=h^{\prime}_{j}=0
and show
\widetilde{S}\cap\widetilde{S}^{\prime}
is
p
-normal.

We then extend the set
\{u_{1},\ldots,u_{k},v_{1},\ldots,v_{m},v^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,v^{\prime}_{m^{\prime}}\}
to a maximal
\mathbb{Z}
-independent subset

\{u_{1},\ldots,u_{k},v_{1},\ldots,v_{m},v^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,v^{\prime}_{m^{\prime}},w_{1},\ldots,w_{n}\}

of
\mathbb{Z}^{KN}
.
Denote by
U,V,V^{\prime},W\subseteq\mathbb{Q}^{KN}
respectively the
\mathbb{Q}
-linear spaces generated by
\{u_{1},\ldots,u_{k}\}
,
\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{m}\}
,
\{v^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,v^{\prime}_{m^{\prime}}\}
,
\{w_{1},\ldots,w_{n}\}
.
Then
U+V+V^{\prime}+W=\mathbb{Q}^{KN}
.
For any
x\in\mathbb{Q}^{KN}
, we can uniquely write
x=u+v+v^{\prime}+w
with
u\in U,v\in V,v^{\prime}\in V^{\prime},w\in W
; in this case we define
\pi_{U+V}(x)\coloneqq u+v
and
\pi_{V^{\prime}+W}(x)\coloneqq v^{\prime}+w
.

Let
\Lambda
denote the set of solutions
(k_{1},\ldots,k_{r},k^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,k^{\prime}_{r^{\prime}})\in\mathbb{N}^{r+r^{\prime}}
to

\Big(\boldsymbol{a}_{0}+p^{\ell k_{1}}\boldsymbol{a}_{1}+\cdots+p^{\ell k_{r}}\boldsymbol{a}_{r}\Big)-\Big(\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}_{0}+p^{\ell k^{\prime}_{1}}\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}_{1}+\cdots+p^{\ell k^{\prime}_{r^{\prime}}}\boldsymbol{a}_{r^{\prime}}\Big)\in\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathbb{Z}u_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbb{Z}v_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbb{Z}v^{\prime}_{i}.

(A.3)

By Lemma
A.2
, we know that
\Lambda
is a finite union of rectangular cosets.

Consider the set

T\coloneqq\\
\Big\{\pi_{V^{\prime}+W}(\boldsymbol{a}_{0})+p^{\ell k_{1}}\pi_{V^{\prime}+W}(\boldsymbol{a}_{1})+\cdots+p^{\ell k_{r}}\pi_{V^{\prime}+W}(\boldsymbol{a}_{r})+\pi_{U+V}(\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}_{0})+p^{\ell k^{\prime}_{1}}\pi_{U+V}(\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}_{1})+\cdots+p^{\ell k^{\prime}_{r}}\pi_{U+V}(\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}_{r})\\
\;\Big|\;(k_{1},\ldots,k^{\prime}_{r^{\prime}})\in\Lambda\Big\}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathbb{Z}u_{i}.

We claim that
T
is
p
-normal and
\widetilde{S}\cap\widetilde{S}^{\prime}=T
, this would show that
\widetilde{S}\cap\widetilde{S}^{\prime}
is
p
-normal and conclude the proof.

1.

First we show
T
is
p
-normal.

Recall that a finite union of
p
-normal sets is still
p
-normal, and
\Lambda
is a finite union of rectangular cosets. Therefore it suffices to show that
T
is
p
-normal when
\Lambda
is a single rectangular coset
\{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{0}+n_{1}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{1}+\cdots+n_{s}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s}\mid n_{1},\ldots,n_{s}\in\mathbb{N}\}
.
Replacing
(k_{1},\ldots,k_{r},k^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,k^{\prime}_{r^{\prime}})
with
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{0}+n_{1}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{1}+\cdots+n_{s}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{s}
, we can rewrite

\pi_{V^{\prime}+W}(\boldsymbol{a}_{0})+p^{\ell k_{1}}\pi_{V^{\prime}+W}(\boldsymbol{a}_{1})+\cdots+p^{\ell k_{r}}\pi_{V^{\prime}+W}(\boldsymbol{a}_{r})+\pi_{U+V}(\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}_{0})+p^{\ell k^{\prime}_{1}}\pi_{U+V}(\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}_{1})+\cdots+p^{\ell k^{\prime}_{r}}\pi_{U+V}(\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}_{r})

as

\widetilde{\boldsymbol{a}}_{0}+p^{\ell c_{1}n_{1}}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{a}}_{1}+\cdots+p^{\ell c_{s}n_{s}}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{a}}_{s}

for some
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{a}}_{0},\ldots,\widetilde{\boldsymbol{a}}_{s}\in\mathbb{Q}^{KN}
.
Here
c_{1},\ldots,c_{s}\in\mathbb{N}
are as in Definition
A.1
.
Let
C
be a common multiplier of
c_{1},\ldots,c_{s}
, then
\{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{a}}_{0}+p^{\ell c_{1}n_{1}}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{a}}_{1}+\cdots+p^{\ell c_{s}n_{s}}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{a}}_{s}\mid n_{1},\ldots,n_{s}\in\mathbb{N}\}
can be written as a finite union of sets of the form

\{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{a}}^{\prime}_{0}+p^{\ell Cn_{1}}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{a}}^{\prime}_{1}+\cdots+p^{\ell Cn_{s}}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{a}}^{\prime}_{s}\mid n_{1},\ldots,n_{s}\in\mathbb{N}\},

which are
p
-normal.

2.

Then we show
\widetilde{S}\cap\widetilde{S}^{\prime}\subseteq T
.

Let

s\coloneqq\boldsymbol{a}_{0}+p^{\ell k_{1}}\boldsymbol{a}_{1}+\cdots+p^{\ell k_{r}}\boldsymbol{a}_{r}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}x_{i}u_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{m}y_{i}v_{i}=\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}_{0}+p^{\ell k^{\prime}_{1}}\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}_{1}+\cdots+p^{\ell k^{\prime}_{r^{\prime}}}\boldsymbol{a}_{r^{\prime}}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}x^{\prime}_{i}u_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{m}y^{\prime}_{i}v^{\prime}_{i}

be any element of
\widetilde{S}\cap\widetilde{S}^{\prime}
.
Then
(k_{1},\ldots,k^{\prime}_{r^{\prime}})\in\Lambda
.
We have
\pi_{V^{\prime}+W}(s)=\pi_{V^{\prime}+W}(\boldsymbol{a}_{0})+p^{\ell k_{1}}\pi_{V^{\prime}+W}(\boldsymbol{a}_{1})+\cdots+p^{\ell k_{r}}\pi_{V^{\prime}+W}(\boldsymbol{a}_{r})
and
\pi_{U+V}(s)\in\pi_{U+V}(\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}_{0})+p^{\ell k^{\prime}_{1}}\pi_{U+V}(\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}_{1})+\cdots+p^{\ell k^{\prime}_{r}}\pi_{U+V}(\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}_{r})+\sum_{i=1}^{k}x^{\prime}_{i}u_{i}
.
Therefore

\displaystyle s

\displaystyle=\pi_{V^{\prime}+W}(s)+\pi_{U+V}(s)

\displaystyle=\pi_{V^{\prime}+W}(\boldsymbol{a}_{0})+\cdots+p^{\ell k_{r}}\pi_{V^{\prime}+W}(\boldsymbol{a}_{r})+\pi_{U+V}(\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}_{0})+\cdots+p^{\ell k^{\prime}_{r}}\pi_{U+V}(\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}_{r})+\sum_{i=1}^{k}x^{\prime}_{i}u_{i}

\displaystyle\in T.

We conclude that
\widetilde{S}\cap\widetilde{S}^{\prime}\subseteq T
.

3.

Finally we show
T\subseteq\widetilde{S}\cap\widetilde{S}^{\prime}
.

Let

t\coloneqq\pi_{V^{\prime}+W}(\boldsymbol{a}_{0})+\cdots+p^{\ell k_{r}}\pi_{V^{\prime}+W}(\boldsymbol{a}_{r})+\pi_{U+V}(\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}_{0})+\cdots+p^{\ell k^{\prime}_{r}}\pi_{U+V}(\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}_{r})+\sum_{i=1}^{k}x^{\prime}_{i}u_{i}

be any element in
T
, with
x^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,x^{\prime}_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}
and
(k_{1},\ldots,k^{\prime}_{r^{\prime}})\in\Lambda
.
Since
(k_{1},\ldots,k^{\prime}_{r^{\prime}})\in\Lambda
, by the definition of
\Lambda
we have

\Big(\boldsymbol{a}_{0}+p^{\ell k_{1}}\boldsymbol{a}_{1}+\cdots+p^{\ell k_{r}}\boldsymbol{a}_{r}\Big)-\Big(\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}_{0}+p^{\ell k^{\prime}_{1}}\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}_{1}+\cdots+p^{\ell k^{\prime}_{r^{\prime}}}\boldsymbol{a}_{r^{\prime}}\Big)\in\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathbb{Z}u_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbb{Z}v_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbb{Z}v^{\prime}_{i}.

So

\displaystyle t-\Big(\boldsymbol{a}_{0}+p^{\ell k_{1}}\boldsymbol{a}_{1}+\cdots+p^{\ell k_{r}}\boldsymbol{a}_{r}\Big)

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle\big(\pi_{V^{\prime}+W}(\boldsymbol{a}_{0})-\boldsymbol{a}_{0}\big)+\cdots+p^{\ell k_{r}}\big(\pi_{V^{\prime}+W}(\boldsymbol{a}_{r})-\boldsymbol{a}_{r}\big)+\pi_{U+V}(\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}_{0})+\cdots+p^{\ell k^{\prime}_{r}}\pi_{U+V}(\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}_{r})+\sum_{i=1}^{k}x^{\prime}_{i}u_{i}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle-\pi_{U+V}(\boldsymbol{a}_{0})-\cdots-p^{\ell k_{r}}\pi_{U+V}(\boldsymbol{a}_{r})+\pi_{U+V}(\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}_{0})+\cdots+p^{\ell k^{\prime}_{r}}\pi_{U+V}(\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}_{r})+\sum_{i=1}^{k}x^{\prime}_{i}u_{i}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle\pi_{U+V}\Big(\big(\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}_{0}+p^{\ell k^{\prime}_{1}}\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}_{1}+\cdots+p^{\ell k^{\prime}_{r^{\prime}}}\boldsymbol{a}_{r^{\prime}}\big)-\big(\boldsymbol{a}_{0}+p^{\ell k_{1}}\boldsymbol{a}_{1}+\cdots+p^{\ell k_{r}}\boldsymbol{a}_{r}\big)\Big)+\sum_{i=1}^{k}x^{\prime}_{i}u_{i}

\displaystyle\in\;

\displaystyle\pi_{U+V}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathbb{Z}u_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbb{Z}v_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbb{Z}v^{\prime}_{i}\right)+\sum_{i=1}^{k}x^{\prime}_{i}u_{i}

\displaystyle\subseteq\;

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathbb{Z}u_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbb{Z}v_{i}.

Therefore
t\in\boldsymbol{a}_{0}+p^{\ell\mathbb{N}}\boldsymbol{a}_{1}+\cdots+p^{\ell\mathbb{N}}\boldsymbol{a}_{r}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathbb{Z}u_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbb{Z}v_{i}=\widetilde{S}
.

Similarly,

\displaystyle t-\Big(\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}_{0}+p^{\ell k^{\prime}_{1}}\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}_{1}+\cdots+p^{\ell k^{\prime}_{r}}\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}_{r}\Big)

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle\pi_{V^{\prime}+W}(\boldsymbol{a}_{0})+\cdots+p^{\ell k_{r}}\pi_{V^{\prime}+W}(\boldsymbol{a}_{r})+\big(\pi_{U+V}(\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}_{0})-\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}_{0}\big)+\cdots+p^{\ell k_{r}}\big(\pi_{U+V}(\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}_{r})-\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}_{r}\big)+\sum_{i=1}^{k}x^{\prime}_{i}u_{i}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle\pi_{V^{\prime}+W}(\boldsymbol{a}_{0})+\cdots+p^{\ell k_{r}}\pi_{V^{\prime}+W}(\boldsymbol{a}_{r})-\pi_{V^{\prime}+W}(\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}_{0})-\cdots-p^{\ell k^{\prime}_{r}}\pi_{V^{\prime}+W}(\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}_{r})+\sum_{i=1}^{k}x^{\prime}_{i}u_{i}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle\pi_{V^{\prime}+W}\Big(\big(\boldsymbol{a}_{0}+p^{\ell k_{1}}\boldsymbol{a}_{1}+\cdots+p^{\ell k_{r}}\boldsymbol{a}_{r}\big)-\big(\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}_{0}+p^{\ell k^{\prime}_{1}}\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}_{1}+\cdots+p^{\ell k^{\prime}_{r^{\prime}}}\boldsymbol{a}_{r^{\prime}}\big)\Big)+\sum_{i=1}^{k}x^{\prime}_{i}u_{i}

\displaystyle\in\;

\displaystyle\pi_{V^{\prime}+W}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathbb{Z}u_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbb{Z}v_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbb{Z}v^{\prime}_{i}\right)+\sum_{i=1}^{k}x^{\prime}_{i}u_{i}

\displaystyle\subseteq\;

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathbb{Z}u_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbb{Z}v^{\prime}_{i}.

Therefore
t\in\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}_{0}+p^{\ell\mathbb{N}}\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}_{1}+\cdots+p^{\ell\mathbb{N}}\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}_{r}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathbb{Z}u_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbb{Z}v^{\prime}_{i}=\widetilde{S}^{\prime}
.
This yields
T\subseteq\widetilde{S}\cap\widetilde{S}^{\prime}
.

We conclude that
\widetilde{S}\cap\widetilde{S}^{\prime}=T
, which is
p
-normal.
∎
